# Preface
## by the Editor.

---

This Egyptian Masonry <term>Egyptian Masonry: Likely a reference to the "Egyptian Rite" of Freemasonry, which became popular in the late 18th century. The author suggests this version is academic and harmless rather than subversive.</term> is too innocent for any Court of the Inquisition <note>The Inquisition was still a potent symbol of censorship and intellectual repression in the 1790s; the author is playfully asserting that the book contains nothing heretical.</note> to justify burning it. —

A well-ordered arrangement of explanations regarding the most prominent symbols or hieroglyphs of the Egyptians constitutes the content of this small volume. It will certainly not fail in its goal of expanding useful and beautiful concepts <note>In the 18th-century context, "concepts" (Begriffe) refers to the refinement of the reader's knowledge and aesthetic judgment.</note>, as it [proceeds] in such an... <note>The original German sentence breaks mid-word with "an-", likely continuing as "pleasant" or "appealing" on the following page.</note>

<summary>In this preface, the editor introduces the work as a harmless and educational collection of Egyptian symbols and hieroglyphs, designed to improve the reader's understanding of ancient aesthetics.</summary>
<keywords>Preface, Egyptian Masonry, Inquisition, symbols, hieroglyphs, Enlightenment, education, aesthetics</keywords>

succession of stages of the growth of human knowledge is to be regarded as neither so regular, nor so decisively perfect, in proportion to its greater or lesser antiquity. <note>The author is challenging the idea that older civilizations were automatically more enlightened or that history follows a constant upward trajectory.</note>

The little that is presented here in the following pages regarding the **symbolic wisdom** of the **Egyptians** contains, to be sure, no great **mysteries** or special traces of **secret arts**—and in this respect, certain curious seekers of that kind may not find their expectations met here; for one sees here only the symbols through which those sages of antiquity expressed certain features of **morality** and **knowledge of human nature** <note>The German term "Menschenkenntniß" refers to the practical understanding of human character and social behavior, a major focus of Enlightenment study.</note>, and thus what is to be made—at least of this part—of the so-called **Egyptian Masonry**, which for a time has come back into fashion. <note>This refers to "Egyptian Rite" Freemasonry, such as the one promoted by Count Cagliostro in the 1780s, which claimed to revive ancient mystical secrets but was often viewed with skepticism by scholars.</note>

exists; and it would certainly be too innocent to be burned. But what has not been burned in Rome since time immemorial? — <note>A reference to the Roman Inquisition’s history of burning heretical or forbidden books.</note>

What the remainder of Egyptian Masonry <term>Egyptian Masonry: A specific rite of Freemasonry founded by Count Cagliostro, which incorporated elements of alchemy and mysticism.</term> consists of is as little apparent from these pages as the secret of Masonry itself is known to us. For a long time, it has been said that the secret of the Masons consists precisely in the fact that they have none. However, if one knows nothing of another, how can he say what this other person has or does not have? And even if a Mason himself knows of no secret: it does not follow from this that Masonry itself has none. Perhaps its secret is so great, so all-encompassing, that they only know it *collectively* <note>original: "in corpore." A Latin term meaning "as a whole body" or "as a group."</note>, and no one can know it for himself alone. For Masonry is allegedly a brotherhood for the promotion of good and the reduction of evil everywhere,

and wherever it may be, and for the practical processing <note>The author envisions Masonry as a system for organizing and applying all human learning toward ethical or social improvement.</note> of all possible human knowledge and inquiry, whatever ever was and is. All else are detours, are human frailties <note>original: "Menschlichkeiten." In this context, it refers to the unavoidable limitations and mistakes inherent to human nature.</note>, and the wise man views—even without formal brotherhood—those who fail as erring brothers who, just as much as he, have truth as their goal, and that the common lot of error <note>A philosophical acknowledgment that no human possesses absolute truth; error is a shared human condition that should foster humility.</note> weighs upon him just as heavily as upon them.

<sig>A</sig>

## Introduction.

Curiosity and the spirit of inquiry are, as it were, an instinct born with man, which develops, sharpens, and strengthens only in proportion to the greater or lesser refinement of one’s organization <note>In the philosophical context of the late 18th and early 19th centuries, "organization" refers to a person's physical and mental constitution or biological makeup.</note> and the opportunity for culture. Therefore, at all times, there have been geniuses in all kinds of knowledge and arts, known to us under the famous names of antiquity as gods, heroes, and sages.

We recognize the Egyptians only as the oldest people whose scope of insight into geometry <note>original: "Meßkunst," literally the "art of measuring," which encompassed both abstract geometry and practical land surveying.</note>, astronomy, architecture, music <note>original: "Tonkunst," the art of tones or composition.</note>, sculpture, chemistry, mining science, agriculture, political science, and rural economy, lets us judge what predecessors they must have had and what geniuses must have lived among them.

But above all else, the science of hieroglyphs was esteemed among them, or the knowl- <note>The text breaks off mid-word ("Kennt-") and will continue on the next page, likely as "Kenntnis" (knowledge).</note>

<summary>This introductory page argues that the drive for knowledge is an innate human instinct and identifies the ancient Egyptians as a peak of early civilization across various scientific and artistic disciplines.</summary>
<keywords>human instinct, antiquity, Egyptian civilization, history of science, hieroglyphs, geometry, enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 1, completing the word "Kenntniß" (knowledge) and continuing a discussion on the secret knowledge of Egyptian priests and the education of Moses.</meta>

...ledge of the sacred <term>hieroglyphic writing<note>German: *Bilderschrift*; literally "picture-script." This refers to the symbolic writing system of ancient Egypt.</note></term>, which was known to only a few, and specifically the most distinguished priests. Had it, however, contained nothing more than the little, or in general only something similar to what we find in the following pages: then this would certainly be regarded as of very little consequence and almost only as the useless speculation of idle minds—such as those very same distinguished priests might well have been. For the first-mentioned arts are indeed only the practical and most essential ones for human society; but it is known that these, too, were in the hands of the priests, and shrouded in their sacred script, and the scope of all this knowledge was certainly not small for those times. Therefore it is also said: *Moses* was educated in all the wisdom of the Egyptians (Acts 7:22) <note>original: "Ap. Gesch. 7. 22." refers to the Book of Acts in the New Testament.</note>. To this wisdom, *Philo* <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who lived during the Roman Empire.</note> in his *Life of Moses*—beyond the aforementioned knowledge—especially reckons that hidden philosophy which was contained in the so-called hieroglyphs or figures of all kinds of animals that were worshiped as deities.

How ancient, then, was the wisdom that had *Moses* as a pupil. *Plato* attributes the invention of Egyptian hieroglyphs to *Theut* or *Taaut* <note>Ancient names for Thoth, the Egyptian god of wisdom, writing, and magic.</note>, and it is known that the Greeks were initially cultivated by the Egyptians...

<summary>This page discusses the secret nature of Egyptian hieroglyphs as a repository for priestly knowledge and "hidden philosophy," citing the education of Moses as evidence of its depth and antiquity.</summary>
<keywords>hieroglyphs, Moses, Egyptian priests, Philo of Alexandria, Thoth, Plato, ancient wisdom, sacred script</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the antiquity of Egyptian wisdom, specifically completing the thought regarding how the Egyptians influenced the earliest Greek thinkers.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>A 2</sig>

gave. Also **Philobiblius** <note>Philo of Byblos, a historian who claimed to translate the works of the Phoenician priest Sanchuniathon.</note>, who according to the testimony of **Eusebius**, translated **Sanchuniathon** very carefully into Greek, names **Taaut** <note>A Phoenician name for the Egyptian god Thoth, the mythical inventor of writing and science.</note> as the first teacher of the **Egyptians**.

The earliest, knowledge-seeking **Greeks** traveled through **Egypt**, such as **Orpheus**, **Solon**, and **Pythagoras**, and sought to be instructed by these people. **Proclus** <note>A Greek Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> tells us that **Solon** heard the priest **Patanit** in **Sais** in **Egypt**, **Oclapis** in **Heliopolis**, and **Ethimon** in **Sebennytos** <note>OCR: "Sebesmiton." This refers to an ancient city in the Nile Delta.</note>, and from them undoubtedly received this symbolic wisdom, of which **Pythagoras** also left traces in his maxims, having likewise heard **Aenopheus** in **Heliopolis**.

The Jewish historian **Alexander** <note>Alexander Polyhistor, a Greek scholar from the 1st century BC who wrote about Jewish history.</note>, whom **Eusebius** cites, says that **Abraham** also lived in **Heliopolis** among **Egyptian** priests and taught them <term>astronomy<note>German: *Sternkunde*; the study of the stars and celestial objects.</note></term>, which had been handed down to him from the time of **Enoch**; and it is highly probable that he already possessed symbolic knowledge from that source. For, in general, the first humans viewed all created things as so many images through which the rays of the Deity shone forth, from whom they had their existence. And **Epictetus**

holds that certain eternal symbols of the Deity are imprinted upon the human being, which are unmistakable. No wonder, then, that the fiery imagination of the first humans viewed themselves and the creatures around them as so many divinely shining letters, through which the Eternal One intended to suggest His inexpressible name, which is to be revered above all else.

The Egyptian priests <note>original: "Sacerdoten"</note> also claimed that the study of their sacred pictorial writing <note>Hieroglyphs; the author uses the descriptive term "Bilderschrift."</note> enlightened the soul and led through the thickest darkness to the knowledge of the most hidden things. They also believed there were no better ornaments with which to decorate their magnificent obelisks and pyramids, as well as the interiors and even the doors of the temples, than their sacred pictorial writing, through which they hoped at the same time to eternalize their wisdom. Thus **Clement of Alexandria** <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who wrote extensively on the relationship between Greek philosophy and Egyptian mystery traditions.</note> mentions the symbols of a boy, an old man, an eagle, a fish, and a crocodile as an inscription in the temple <term>pylon<note>A monumental gateway of an Egyptian temple, consisting of two tapering towers joined by a bridge.</note></term> at **Diospolis** <note>The Greek name for the ancient Egyptian city of Thebes.</note> in **Egypt**, and explains them thus: "Hear, young and old, God hates the impudent!" Specifically: the eagle is the image of the Deity; the fish, of hatred; and the crocodile, the image of impudence.

But that symbolic representations were also not unknown and un- <note>The word is cut off: likely "ungebraucht" (unused) or "ungewohnt" (unfamiliar).</note> even to the **Scythians** <note>Ancient nomadic people of Central Eurasia, often cited in historical texts as a contrast to the "civilized" Egyptians or Greeks.</note>

<meta>This page completes the word "unusual" (ungewöhnlich) from the previous page's final sentence, which discussed how symbolic communication was not uncommon among ancient peoples.</meta>

The Persians now immediately understood the meaning of these symbols; however, the application was still doubtful, like an oracle. For the military commander *Orontopagas* explained it thus: as if the Scythians wanted to surrender themselves and their entire land, and interpreted the mouse as their dwellings, the frog as their waters, the bird as their climate, the spear as their weapons, and the plow generally as the land and the people. *Xiphodres*, however, explained the thing quite differently and said: "If we do not fly away like birds, or crawl away like mice and frogs, their arrows will destroy us." *Anacharsis* <note>A famous Scythian philosopher who traveled to Athens in the 6th century BC and became a symbol of the "noble savage" to the Greeks.</note> also describes the Scythians to us as a very symbolic people, in that, as he says, a Scythian, even when he sleeps, by covering the genitals with the left hand and the mouth with the right, makes the symbol known that one must indeed keep both in check, but more so the tongue than lust.

# E x p l a n a t i o n
#### of Some of the Most Notable Symbols or Hieroglyphs of the Egyptians.

## The Deity.

An eye above a scepter is the symbol of the deity, who, exalted above all, sees and governs everything. <note>The author is likely referring to the Eye of Providence or a variation of the Eye of Horus, which in Western esoteric traditions was often interpreted as an all-seeing divine gaze.</note> One also occasionally finds the image of a staff as a sign of the immutable, ever-steadfast divine nature that founds and sustains all things.

## Eternity.

The sun and moon are the image of eternity, because they were regarded as eternal elements. Eternity was also designated by a serpent coiling itself into a circle; <note>This is the Ouroboros, a widespread ancient symbol depicting a serpent or dragon eating its own tail, representing the infinite cycle of nature.</note> although the world is indicated in almost the same way, yet with the difference that the serpent is then depicted with many scales to represent the stars.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Egyptian hieroglyphs from the previous page, moving from the concepts of the Deity and Eternity to the specific measurements and manifestations of "Time" and "The Year."</meta>

## Time.

A snake, crawling slowly along in many curves. According to our current notions, this image does not seem fitting, as it lacks the fleetingness and transience that we more commonly attribute to time. However, the Egyptians had several symbols to express this as well, as will appear in the following.

In general, snakes and dragons were shown a special divine reverence; they were also symbols of royal dignity and the highest unrestricted power, and formed a special ornament of the diadem. This crown was, however, also interwoven with ears of grain as a sign of mildness <note>The ears of grain likely refer to the goddess Isis or Demeter, symbolizing fertility and the provider-aspect of a ruler.</note>; an idea which a certain Scottish King **Hungus** <note>Hungus refers to Angus (Óengus) II, a legendary 9th-century King of the Picts. Legend says he adopted the thistle as a symbol after a divine vision before a battle.</note> seemed to have imitated as a sign of his despotic severity, with the difference that he wore a crown interwoven with thistles, with the inscription: *No one provokes me with impunity.* <note>original Latin: "Nemo me impune lacessit." This remains the national motto of Scotland and the Order of the Thistle.</note>

## The Year.

This was designated by a palm tree, because it grows a branch with every month and thus completes a year with twelve branches. <note>This belief stems from ancient natural histories, such as those by Horapollo, which claimed the palm tree produced twelve shoots a year, one for each lunar cycle.</note> — The goddess **Isis** was also the image of the year. Actually, **Isis** was a

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the goddess Isis, identifying her with the celestial body Sothis (Sirius) and explaining the Egyptian calendrical system.</meta>

heavenly body, which was also called Sothis, and was known to the Greeks by the name *Astrokyon* <note>original Greek: $A\sigma\tau\rho\omicron\kappa\acute{\upsilon}\omicron\nu$</note>, or Dog-Star. In this constellation, two stars were primarily to be noted: Isis at the head, and Sirius on the tongue of this star-sign. The Egyptians saw in this heavenly body the prognostic <note>or "prognostikon," a sign used to predict future events</note> of the entire year; they also held it to be the point of generation for the entire structure of the universe. It is still the opinion of astronomers today that our solar system, along with several others, revolves around Sirius. <note>This reflects a historical astronomical theory that Sirius served as a "central sun" for our part of the galaxy.</note>

The Egyptians also did not place the beginning of the year in Aquarius, as the Romans did, but in Cancer or the Dog-Star. <note>The "Dog-Star" refers to the heliacal rising of Sirius, which coincided with the annual flooding of the Nile.</note>

### The Twofold Year of the Egyptians.

They had a common year of 365 days, and a "great year" that spanned a period of four years. For this reason, they designated the former by a fourth part of a field, representing the fourth part of the "great year," which concerned the sun alone and in which the moon was not considered at all; despite the fact that both were their most prominent deities—the former being **Osiris** and the latter **Isis**. They called the "great year" the solar year or the Year of God, and the intercalation <note>The insertion of extra days to keep the calendar aligned with the seasons</note> was a secret of the priests; for a leap year cycle <note>known as the Sothic Cycle</note> was only completed after the passage of 1,461 years.

<summary>This page explains the Egyptian association of the star Sirius (Sothis) with the goddess Isis and the beginning of their year. it also details the difference between the common 365-day year and the "Great Year" or Sothic cycle used by the priesthood.</summary>

## The Month.

The month was represented by a palm branch, which can be explained by the previously mentioned palm tree serving as an image of the year. <note>In Egyptian iconography, the palm tree was often associated with time because it was believed to sprout a new branch every month.</note> They also designated the beginning of a month with a crescent moon with its tips pointed upward; downward-facing tips indicated the completed month.

## The Eagle.

This is a high symbol of the Egyptians. A king who loves solitude and upholds justice was represented by an eagle; because this bird builds its nest in high, lonely places and soars far above all other fowl. The eagle was also the image of the sun, the deity, greatness of soul, victory, and in general, all sublimity and great, noble actions, all of which were easily distinguished by the secondary symbols associated with them. A man who always has high, noble goals in sight resembles the eagle, which flies toward the sun with an unwavering gaze. The rays do not blind its eyes but rather strengthen them; and when old age dims them, Pliny says, it rubs them with hawkweed <note>original: *hieracium*</note> and they pierce toward the sun as be-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the eagle as a symbol of the soul and the divine, linking it to Egyptian etymology and the ancient concept of the soul as part of the universal ether.</meta>

fore. He is, as it were, the eternal bird, for he attains an immense age and is therefore the image of the deity.

The eagle was called *Bajeth* by the Egyptians, from *Baj* (soul, ether) and *eth* (heart); it thus designated a spirited, swift, and fiery being. For this reason, it was taken as the image of the human soul in general and for every specific greatness of soul. According to the ancient opinion of the wise, our soul—our thinking being—is a part of the universal ether <note>In ancient and early modern science, "ether" was the fifth element that filled the universe beyond the terrestrial sphere.</note> which never passes away and cannot be dissolved by anything. Within the circles of the stars, this ether is compressed and becomes visible to our eyes—the ethereal vehicles—and it is the first and greatest mass of the whole, to which our ethereal thinking being returns as soon as it is separated from its organic shell.

From this arise the secondary ideas, which were modified in so many ways among all nations, regarding the outflows and influences of the stars upon mortals: a subject which Magnetism <note>likely a reference to "animal magnetism" or Mesmerism, a popular 18th-century theory about invisible natural forces between living things.</note> has recently sought to refine. Hence also the brilliant poetic idea of the ancients, among whom it was an accepted dogma that great souls were moved among the stars and became stars themselves. High virtue and a pure spirit radiate far and wide; they usually shine forth from the mossy huts of the po- <note>The text cuts off here; it likely intended to say "the poor" (German: *Armen*).</note>

*Aelian* <note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175 – c. 235 AD), a Roman author known for his works on the nature of animals.</note> and *Porphyry* <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234 – c. 305 AD), a Neoplatonic philosopher who wrote extensively on philosophy and religion.</note> mention a world-famous *eagle oracle* in *Egypt*, whose priests drank eagle’s blood in order to gain the *power of foresight*. It was also believed that the eagle drinks no water, but only blood.

In the *Cyranides* <note>A famous ancient Greek compendium of lore regarding the magical and medicinal properties of plants, animals, and stones.</note> there is also an Egyptian recipe for a mystical ring: Have an eagle engraved into a *xiphic stone* (*sapphire*), holding a *swordfish* in its talons. Beneath the stone, seal a bit of the root of the herb *xiphon* (*gladiolus or blue lilies*): thus one has a *sacred ring* that serves as an oracle in all cases; furthermore, any image upon which this ring is placed will provide signs and information regarding what one wishes to know. *It has been proven* <note>original: "Probatum erat"—a common Latin phrase used in historical medical and alchemical texts to certify that a formula was effective.</note> — — —

The connection of the *soul* with the *heart*, in the Egyptian naming of the eagle, signifies the idea of the ancients, who held the *brain* to be the *seat of the intellect*, the *nape of the neck* to be the seat of stubbor- <note>The word is cut off; it likely intended "stubbornness" (German: *Hartnäckigkeit*).</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding ancient beliefs about the biological seats of the soul and human character, transitioning from the nape of the neck (associated with stubbornness) to the internal organs.</meta>

...the remaining hiding places of opinion, holding the **heart** to be the seat of **courage** and **reason**, and the **spleen** and **liver** to be the vessels of **desires**. This bears a similarity to the content of an ancient distich <note>A poem or verse consisting of two lines.</note>:

> The heart tastes wisdom and the lung speaks, the bile stirs up anger,
> The spleen creates laughter, and the liver compels one to love;
<note>original Latin: "Cor sapit et pulmo loquitur, Fel commuet iras, / Splen ridere facit, cogit amare iecur;" — A traditional medieval mnemonic describing the "four humors" and the functions of the organs.</note>

Accordingly, the **heart** would be the *principium* <note>Latin: the beginning, origin, or fundamental principle.</note> of **wisdom**; the **lung** the principle of **speech**; the **bile** the principle of **anger**; the **spleen** the principle of **laughter**; and the **liver** the principle of **love**. The skeptical modern scholars, of course, limit themselves to the view that the heart is the source and the seat of natural heat; that the lungs draw breath; that the bile is a <term>secretion (an "excrement")</term> of the blood and the liver; that the spleen attracts the "melancholic humor" <note>In ancient medicine, "black bile," one of the four fluids thought to influence temperament.</note> and the liver forms the blood, etc. etc.

The **heart** was, moreover, the most important part of the human being to the ancients. This led to the custom of kissing the chest of the wise and wearing the sacred symbols of wisdom over the heart; just as the Egyptian High Priest wore the "Sapphire of Truth" upon his chest; and likewise the Jewish High Priest wore the <term>Urim and Thummim (sacred stones or objects used for seeking divine guidance)</term>, just as Moses did—along with other rituals—based on refined Egyptian and Phoenician ideas.

ordered. — The heart was the most noble object toward which evil magicians directed their incantations. A certain Nectanabanus <note>Likely referring to Nectanebo II, the last native pharaoh of Egypt, who featured prominently in later legends as a master sorcerer and the secret father of Alexander the Great.</note> was particularly ingenious in this regard; he was the first to hit upon the method of molding a person's heart in wax and, amidst a thousand incantations, piercing it with a multitude of needles to strip that person of all courage and composure. This "praiseworthy" custom—also applied to the liver, which can be understood as synonymous with the heart—is evident from a verse by Ovid:

> The sorceress fashioned the divine images in crimson wax, 
> and drove thin needles into the middle of the liver. 
> <note>original Latin: "Sagaque punicea definxit numina cera, / Et medium tenues in iecur egit acus." From Ovid's Heroides VI.</note>

## The Ibis, the Stork.

Among the Egyptians, this was the image of the highest reason, the seat of which—as has been said—resides only in the human heart. The principle may hold true that we are more or less rational depending on whether our heart is more or less evil; that is, according to whether we possess a better or worse will, or whether we are more or less inclined to act according to whims, passions, or minor caprices. However, it would be a troubling truth if this

This cannot be entirely true, because we see that many people possess the ability to restrain themselves and refrain from doing evil—even in the grip of the most violent passion—for political and other reasons that impose a stronger constraint upon them. In contrast, religion and conscience have not the slightest influence over them, as for many it remains a question whether these are not all merely empty prejudices <note>In this context, the author uses "prejudices" to mean unexamined beliefs or superstitions that people might dismiss as having no rational basis.</note>. 

The use of <term>Reason</term> <note>Vernunft: Often used in Enlightenment philosophy to describe the capacity for logical, moral, and universal thought.</note> and its true, constant application therefore require a high cultivation of the intellect and a noble sense of honor. One must strive to be regarded as a rational, honor-loving man in every action and in all conduct, just as the sages of antiquity accepted the principle that *only things which are honorable are truly good* <note>original Latin: "Sola bona quæ honesta." This is a fundamental Stoic maxim, suggesting that moral virtue is the only genuine good, independent of external circumstances.</note>. 

To be sure, one does not then play a "glittering role" in the eyes of most people, and for that reason this teaching appeals to so few. Indeed, some may even attribute our temperate behavior—which costs us much study, much effort, and much self-mastery—merely to a happy temperament, if not to our simple-mindedness. But the worth or worthlessness of a thing never consists in the applause or the censure of the masses.

## Marriage.

The image of married and domestic life, along with the mutual duties of parents and children, was represented by a stork’s nest. Since storks are generally considered symbols of the highest <term>Reason</term> <note>In historical emblem books and natural histories, storks were often celebrated as "pious" birds because of their perceived intelligence and care for their kin.</note>: it was also said of them that they are moved to their marital duties neither by indulgent charms nor by impetuousness against the will of the other. The young never separate from the old, but rather demonstrate their help and devotion to them for as long as they live; on long flights, they take the elders upon their backs and carry them along for entire stretches.

---

The union of a bridal couple was symbolized by two crows, a symbol which the Greeks also maintained. Crows are said to possess the virtues of harmony and fidelity above all else, never associating with others; even when separated by death or other circumstances, they never pair with others again. Therefore, it was also considered an omen of widowhood if one encountered a single crow alone. At an upcoming wedding, it was the custom to call out to the bride: <term>Εκκορει κορη κορωνην</term> <note>original Greek: "Ekkorei kore koronen." This was an ancient ritual chant. While the author translates it here as an instruction to "feed" the crow, it more literally means "Maiden, sweep out the crow!"—a ritual act intended to drive away the solitary crow of widowhood to ensure a long-lasting marriage.</note>; "Be diligent in feeding the crow, young bride!" For she

Courage and anger were signified by the image of the **lion**; strength by its foreparts. The large, round head of the lion...

<page-num>18</page-num>

...[lio]ns, with scattered, radiant manes, were also the image of the sun and the great god <note>Likely referring to the Egyptian sun god Ra or other solar deities whose power was equated with the lion's majesty.</note>, as it was believed there was a pre-eminent similarity between them. The <term>throne of hours</term> <note>A symbolic representation of the sun's journey through the divisions of the day.</note> of the sun was also made to rest upon lions, and they were placed as protectors and guardians at the entrances of temples; just as a vigilant, industrious man was generally represented by a lion’s head. The lion, it is said, watches with closed eyes and sleeps with open eyes, and therefore provides the best symbol of protection and vigilance.

The excess of all power and strength—a terrible and fearsome warrior, or a devastating enemy—was signified by the symbol of a raging lion. The flooding of the Nile was also represented by the image of the lion, because when the sun enters this constellation <note>The zodiac sign of Leo, which the sun enters in July, coinciding with the annual rise of the Nile.</note> and for as long as it remains there, the Nile often swells twice as high; for this reason, the image of the lion was also placed at the entrances of canals and springs.

Immoderate anger and the resulting burning fever were signified by a lion that lashes its own cubs with its tail so that sparks fly about. — At least, it is said that during this action a great many electrical sparks spray <note>A reference to static electricity or "animal electricity," a topic of great scientific interest in the late 18th and early 19th centuries.</note>.

<meta>continuity: This page continues the discussion of lion symbolism from page 18. <page-num>19</page-num>

A person who cures their own fever was represented by a lion eating a monkey in order to heal itself of a similar illness; which is said to truly occur shortly after this meal. <note>An ancient zoological myth suggesting that lions would eat primates to cure their internal ailments, later adopted as a symbol for self-healing.</note>

Furthermore, the lion is a universal symbol of majesty and is still used today for all kinds of insignia, emblems, and decorations. The image of the lion was used not only by the Egyptians, Greeks, and Romans, but also by the Jews, in whose Temple even the sacrificial altar bore its image. This is clear from the Hebrew name **Ariel** <note>original: אריאל</note>, derived from **Ari** <note>original: ארי</note>, a lion, and **El** <note>original: אל</note>, God—meaning the "Lion of God." This was intended partly as a symbol of divine majesty, as Jewish commentators say, and partly because the altar, like a lion, consumes the flesh of the slaughtered livestock. The lion is also said to be an image of grace and mercy toward the humble and the weak. In several passages of the Old and New Testaments, the lion is also depicted as an image of power, heroic courage, and even arbitrary punitive authority, for example: "The Lion of the tribe of Judah has triumphed." <note>A reference to Revelation 5:5, where the "Lion of the tribe of Judah" is a title for Jesus Christ.</note> And when Paul says: "At my first defense, no one stood by me; but the Lord stood by me, and I was re-" <note>This quote from 2 Timothy 4:16–17 concludes on the next page with "rescued from the lion’s mouth." Here, the "lion" likely represents the threat of execution or the Roman Emperor Nero.</note>

Fourteen lions surrounded Solomon's throne, which the antiquarian *Josephus* calls "a magnificent seat upon which the king sat to judge." <note>original Greek: εξέδραν διαπρεπη εν ἡ καθεζόμενος ὁ βασιλευς εκρινεν</note>. Lions were also frequently found on the graves of heroes. For instance, *Pausanias* <note>A 2nd-century AD Greek traveler whose "Description of Greece" is a vital source for ancient art and topography.</note> describes the communal grave of the Thebans who fell in the battle against Philip <note>Philip II of Macedon at the Battle of Chaeronea in 338 BC, where the "Lion of Chaeronea" monument still stands today.</note>. It was without any inscription, he says, but a mighty lion stretched across it: a custom which, according to *Hephaestion*, dates back to *Hercules*. This hero is said to have lost a finger in his duel with the Nemean Lion, and therefore had only nine...

### The Crocodile

The symbol of all falsehood and malice, of rapacity and madness, and also of fertility, because this egg-laying animal produces many young at once. If it chases a prey in vain, it rages against itself in its wickedness. — A crocodile with a stork feather on its head is the image of laziness and inactivity; for it is said that if touched with a stork feather, this animal will remain motionless. — It also signifies the rising and the setting <note>In the context of Egyptian symbolism, this often refers to the sun’s cycle or the rising and falling of the Nile.</note>. The former when its head projects out of the water, the latter in the reverse position. — The tail of the crocodile signifies darkness and ruin, for in this part of the body lies its greatest strength, with which it strikes everything to the ground. The crocodile is also the image of impudence <note>In this historical context, "impudence" (Unverschämtheit) implies a lack of shame, or a bold, disrespectful arrogance.</note>. In a temple at Diospolis <note>Greek name for "City of Zeus," referring to several cities in Egypt, most famously Thebes (Diospolis Magna).</note> in Egypt, the following hieroglyphs stood: a boy and an old man, an eagle, a fish, and a crocodile, and the interpretation was: O you mortals! God hates impudence.

<summary>This page concludes a story about a lion monument for Hercules' finger before detailing the multifaceted symbolism of the crocodile—ranging from malice and laziness to celestial cycles and impudence—in Egyptian and classical thought.</summary>
<keywords>crocodile, symbolism, Egypt, Diospolis, hieroglyphs, Hercules, Nemean Lion, fertility, impudence</keywords>

## The Elephant

The symbol of superior strength of both mind and body. The elephant, however, is said to flee before a ram or a bull as soon as it catches sight or scent of one; this is proof that it considers it beneath its dignity to soil itself with these creatures or to destroy them. It is also too just and magnanimous to pursue them or take their lives, since they cannot harm it anyway. It therefore prefers to get out of their way, leaving them to what nature has granted them. The people of Megara <note>Megara was a Greek city-state famous for its strategic position; its people were often depicted in ancient history as clever and resourceful.</note> once utilized this elephantine loathing for pigs as an ingenious ruse of war. They smeared a whole herd of pigs with pitch, set them on fire, and drove them like stinking firebrands among the elephants of Antipater <note>Antipater (c. 397–319 BC) was a powerful Macedonian general and a primary successor to Alexander the Great.</note>; the elephants fled in all directions, causing the most terrible disorder.

When an elephant's tusks <term>Zähne: literally "teeth," but in the context of elephants, it refers to their ivory tusks.</term> fall out, it is said to bury them in the earth. If one wished to represent a person arranging their own funeral, one would depict an elephant burying its own tusks.

The elephant is also an image of prudence and cunning, regarding which Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175 – c. 235 AD) was a Roman author and teacher whose work "On the Nature of Animals" was a popular source for animal lore in later centuries.</note> [provides] a... <meta>The text breaks off at the bottom of the page with the syllable "ar-", likely intended to be "artige" (fine/remarkable) or "ausführliche" (detailed) in the following sentence.</meta>

<summary>This page explores the elephant as a symbol of noble strength and prudence, detailing its legendary behavior toward smaller animals, its role in ancient military history, and its symbolic association with preparing for one's death.</summary>
<keywords>elephant, symbolism, Megara, military tactics, Antipater, tusks, funeral rites, prudence, Aelian</keywords>

<meta>continuity: This page continues the discussion of the elephant as a symbol of prudence and cunning, completing a sentence from the previous page regarding an anecdote from the Roman author Aelian.</meta>

...remarkable example. An elephant had been cheated out of a portion of its fodder by its keeper, who had placed several stones at the bottom of the vessel to make up the measure. The elephant therefore bided its time, and when the keeper went to cook some porridge for himself, the elephant took a portion of it with its trunk and poured sand into the pot in return.

The elephant is said to sigh and groan in secret over its servitude <term>Knechtschaft: literally "bondage" or "slavery," referring here to the animal's captivity and labor.</term>, but as soon as it becomes aware of its keeper or any other human, it suddenly stops; it is therefore the image of an unfortunate person who buries their secret sorrow within themselves.

An elephant is said to be tamed and restrained only through music, and is therefore the image of those temperaments that can only be guided through kindness.

It was also customary to indicate a noble and venerable old age through the image of an elephant. During the encounter with King Porus <note>Porus was an ancient Indian king who famously fought Alexander the Great at the Battle of the Hydaspes in 326 BC.</note> in India, Alexander captured, among several elephants, an exceptionally beautiful and strong one named Ajax, who is said to have lived until the time of Tiberius <note>Tiberius (42 BC – 37 AD) was the second Roman Emperor. If the story were true, the elephant would have been over 350 years old.</note>, and thus reached several hundred years of age.

Finally, the elephant is also the symbol of skill and vain ambition. Mutianus <note>Gaius Licinius Mucianus was a Roman general and writer of the 1st century AD who recorded many wonders of the natural world.</note> mentions an elephant that even learned to write with its trunk, and after it [was]...

## The Horse.

The symbol of freedom and boldness, of pride and stubbornness, of war and combativeness, of docility and temperance, etc. When the athlete Phidolas <note>Pheidolas of Corinth was a celebrated horseman of the 6th century BC. According to legend, his mare "Aura" threw him at the start of the race but continued running and won on her own.</note> carried away the victory in the race, he left his horse alone on the field the following day, and it performed all the feats on its own without a rider, just as well as if its master had been seated upon it to lead and direct it; the people therefore also erected a special honorary column for the horse. —

Caligula bestowed upon his victor of the circus games <note>This refers to Incitatus, the favorite horse of the Roman Emperor Caligula (r. 37–41 AD). Ancient sources claim Caligula loved the horse so much he intended to make it a consul.</note> a marble stable, an ivory manger, purple blankets, a harness with precious jewels, and even the **consular dignity**, complete with a palace and a household staff. There, feasts were held in the name of the highly exalted Imperial Steed, to which the most prominent Romans were invited and most splendidly entertained.

A horse fighting with wolves is the image of **steadfastness** and high courage in the face of adversity. The Greeks also trained horses through this method and called them *wolf-footed* <note>original Greek: λυκοπαδας (lykopadas). This likely refers to a specific gait or a horse trained to be as agile and fierce as a wolf.</note>, as they thereby became far more agile, courageous, and swift as an arrow.

A horse with the *Anthos* <note>The "Anthos" is often identified in classical mythology as a small bird, possibly the yellow wagtail, which was said to have a natural enmity or a parasitic relationship with horses.</note>, a small colorful bird, upon its back is the image of the weak taming the strong. The little bird is said to be unable to find food easily due to its dim-sightedness <note>The original German "blöden Gesichts" implies weak or dim vision rather than "stupid."</note> and therefore constantly grazes in the company of the horse; it hops upon its neck, and by its jumping and plucking between the animal's ears, it continually disturbs the horse during its meal.

The little adder slays the wide-spanning ox;
Just as a lowly dog catches the bristly boar.

Another painter had long sought in vain to paint a foaming steed; the steed was finished and well-executed, but the foam had already cost him far more time and fruitless effort. Finally, out of indignation and frustration, the artist threw his paint-laden brush at the painting; the foam scattered across the canvas exactly as one could have wished. <note>This anecdote is frequently attributed to the Greek painters Protogenes or Nealces, illustrating the classical idea that "chance" or "nature" sometimes completes what the artist’s "art" cannot.</note>

## The Hippopotamus

The image of **Spring**, because it emerges from the Nile as soon as the first grass and herbs begin to sprout. It is also the image of exceptional size and strength, as it has the body of an ox besides its enormous horse-like head, is larger than a camel, and often weighs 6,000 pounds. It is also the symbol of the **invincible**, because its iron-hard hide resembles a suit of armor from which all arrows (and even musket balls) bounce off. For this reason, many consider this monster to be the **Behemoth** <note>A massive primeval beast mentioned in the Bible (Job 40:15–24), which historical scholars often identified as the hippopotamus.</note> described by **Job**. A most heinous deed was also signified by the image of this beast, for it was claimed that it would kill its father and mate with its mother. <note>This specific lore regarding the hippopotamus's supposed impiety comes from ancient Egyptian symbology, often cited in Renaissance emblem books and works like the *Hieroglyphica*.</note> It was also the image

# The Ox

The symbol of **magnanimity** and **temperance**, of **industry** and **agriculture**, of **good-naturedness** and the **amorous gaze**, but also of **dullness**. <note>The "amorous gaze" likely refers to "ox-eyed" (boopis), a common Homeric epithet for the goddess Hera, signifying large, beautiful eyes.</note> Among the five senses, an ox's ear is the image of **Hearing**, because it is said to be able to hear from a very great distance. — An ox bound by the right leg is the image of **indecision** and **instability**. — A man who has become wise through misfortune, or improved through adversity, was represented by an ox entwined with **wild fig leaves**; for these leaves are truly said to make wild oxen tame and calm again. <note>This refers to an ancient folk belief, recorded by authors like Pliny the Elder, that the sap or scent of the wild fig tree had a miraculous calming effect on even the most untamable animals.</note> An ox with a gnat on its back was the image of a powerful person's mercy toward the powerless and the lowly. Hence the following fable from Babrius: <note>The original text uses the name "Gabrios," a common early modern variation of Babrius (Valerius Babrius), the 2nd-century creator of Greek fables.</note>

> A lowly gnat sat upon a bull's horn:
> He asked the bull if he wished for him to fly away,
> To which the bull replied: "I did not even know you were sitting there,
> Nor truly shall I notice you when you fly away."
> <note>original: "Tauri sedebat vilis in cornu culex: / Volare num se vellet ipsum interrogat, / Respondet ille: nec sedentem noueram / Nec euolantem te profecto sentiam."</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of moral lessons derived from fables and historical anecdotes, transitioning from the arrogance of the small and the cruelty of the powerful to the symbolic nature of the camel.</meta>

Upon an ox sat the gnat
And prided itself on its luck
Of flight. "Will you come along?" she asks:
"Stay or flee," said he, "I never knew you were there."

A counterpart to this is what Suetonius <note>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus (c. 69–122 AD), a Roman historian famous for his biographies of emperors</note> tells of Caligula <note>The third Roman Emperor, often depicted in historical texts as a cruel and capricious tyrant</note>, who in a fencing match stabbed an old fighter with a dagger, even though the man had voluntarily thrown himself down before him, and then ran about in great jubilation with the palm of victory <term>Siegespalme: a classical symbol of triumph, originally awarded to victorious gladiators and athletes</term>. It is the most hideous image of any powerful person who feels happy when he can sacrifice to his anger and displeasure a lowly person who has offended him, or by whom he perhaps only believes himself to have been offended.

## The Camel.

The camel is the image of a slow-moving person. It is the only animal among all that bends its hip while walking; hence it is called *kamēlos* <note>original: κάμηλος</note> in Greek. It also denotes a man who can endure hunger and thirst and all hardships during heavy labor; for the camel has these qualities, and if it becomes tired under its load and from the long journey in immense, burning deserts of sand, it is encouraged not by scourges and blows, but by music and song encour... <note>The original German text ends mid-word with "anges-", likely meaning "angeregt" (encouraged or stimulated)</note>

# The Ox

The symbol of magnanimity and temperance, of industriousness and agriculture, of good-naturedness and the "loving gaze" <note>likely a reference to the classical "ox-eyed" epithet used to describe large, soulful, and beautiful eyes</note>, as well as of tastelessness. Among the five senses, an ox's ear is the image of hearing, because it is said to truly hear from a great distance. — An ox tied by its right leg is the image of irresolution and instability. — A man who has become wise through harm, or improved through adversity, was represented by an ox entwined with wild fig leaves; for these leaves are said to actually make wild oxen tame and calm again <note>This refers to an ancient folk belief, recorded by writers like Pliny the Elder, that the pungent sap of the wild fig tree could soothe even the most aggressive bull</note>. An ox with a gnat on its back was the image of a powerful person showing mercy <term>Schonung: the act of showing leniency or sparing someone who is weaker or vulnerable</term> toward the powerless and lowly. Hence the following fable by Babrius <note>original: "Gabrios"; referring to Valerius Babrius, a Roman-era author who wrote fables in Greek, which were widely adapted into Latin</note>:

> A lowly gnat sat upon the horn of a bull:
> It asked him whether he wished it to fly away.
> He responded: "I did not even know you were sitting there,
> Nor indeed shall I feel you as you fly off."
<note>original Latin: "Tauri sedebat vilis in cornu culex: / Volare num se vellet ipsum interrogat, / Respondet ille: nec sedentem noueram / Nec euolantem te profecto sentiam."</note>

<vocab>Ox, Symbol, Magnanimity, Temperance, Hearing, Babrius, Fable, Agriculture</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a description of a gluttonous creature before detailing the symbolic meanings of the ox, ranging from industriousness and sharp hearing to the mercy the powerful show the weak.</summary>
<keywords>ox symbolism, Babrius, fables, animal imagery, temperance, agriculture, hearing, animal behavior</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the ox and its symbolic relationship with smaller creatures, specifically finishing the fable of Babrius mentioned on the previous page. It then transitions into the symbolism of the camel, focusing on its physical endurance and temperament.</meta>

Upon an ox there sat a gnat,
Who, puffed with pride at how she sat,
Bragged of her flight. "D'ye wish to stay?"
"Remain or fly," he said, "away;
I never knew you were there today."
<note>This verse concludes the fable of the Gnat and the Ox. The moral emphasizes that the powerful are often entirely indifferent to the posturing of the insignificant.</note>

A counterpart to this is what Suetonius <note>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus (c. 69 – 122 AD), a Roman historian famous for his biographies of the first twelve Roman emperors</note> tells of Caligula, who, during a fencing match, pierced with a dagger an old fighter who had voluntarily cast himself down before him, and then ran about in great jubilation with the palm of victory. It is the most hideous image of any powerful man who feels happy when he can sacrifice to his anger and displeasure a lowly person who has offended him, or by whom he merely believes himself to have been offended.

## The Camel

The camel is the image of a slow person. It is the only one among all animals that bends its hip while walking; for this reason, it is also called *kammēlos* <note>original: "καμμηλος"; likely a folk etymology linking the Greek word for camel (*kamēlos*) to the bending (*kam-*) of its joints</note> in Greek. — It also signifies a man who can endure hunger and thirst and every hardship during heavy labor; for the camel possesses these qualities, and if it indeed grows weary under its burden and from the long journey through vast, burning deserts of sand, it is encouraged not through scourges <term>Geißel: a whip or lash, historically used for punishment or driving livestock</term> and blows, but through music and song...

<keywords>Babrius, Caligula, Suetonius, fables, animal symbolism, camel, patience, Greek etymology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the camel from the previous page, specifically regarding how it is driven by song rather than force. It then transitions into the moral symbolism of the leopard and the tiger, drawing on traditional bestiary lore.</meta>

...encouraged, for through the former <note>referring to the whips and blows mentioned on the previous page</note> one would achieve nothing at all. The latter <note>music and song</note> is the image of studious, good-natured youth, with whom one gains more through kindness than through coercion.

## The Leopard
<note>original: "Der Parder"; an archaic term for a leopard or panther</note>

Through this beautiful monster, one designated a hypocrite who conceals his evil undertakings, just as the leopard stalks its prey in this manner. It hides itself in deep foliage, and since it naturally possesses a particularly lovely scent, the defenseless forest animals track it and are torn to pieces. <note>This reflects a common medieval belief from the *Physiologus* that the leopard (panther) emitted a sweet fragrance after sleeping to lure other animals.</note> The leopard has such a hatred toward humans that it tears apart even a mere image of one, yet greedily devours dung. For this reason, one designated through this symbol a person who hates all virtue and loves only vice. The leopard is also the image of anyone about whom little good, but much evil, can be said, and of whom one therefore prefers to mention nothing.

A hungry tiger is the image of a cruel tyrant; a satiated one, the image of laziness;

<note>original: "Der Bär"</note>

The bear licks his shapeless young and,

he takes one on his back, which clings
tightly, and one in his mouth, hastens up a tree
with them, and seeks in every way to escape destruction
along with his own.

Besides the snakes, scorpions, and other poisonous and malicious creatures that belong to the symbols of the royal court, the supposedly fortunate position and lifestyle of the courtiers is represented by a water wheel to which they are bound; in this manner, they are thrown now into the heights, now into the abyss. <note>This imagery draws from the classical "Wheel of Fortune," illustrating how quickly a politician can fall from grace.</note> They were therefore also called Ixion's friends, or "counting-fingers," who represent ten thousand one moment and a single unit the next. <note>Ixion, in Greek mythology, was punished by being bound to a perpetually spinning wheel. "Counting-fingers" refers to the way digits are used in calculation—a finger's value changes from "one" to "ten thousand" based purely on its position, much like a courtier's value depends on their proximity to the King.</note>

## The Wolf.

The image of a robber, of one who is hungry; a portent of death. A wolf with a stone beside him signifies anxiety and a dark premonition. For the wolf courageously faces all weapons, except for a thrown stone; this is because worms are said to find their way into such wounds on his body. <note>This reflects a historical folk belief regarding the wolf's unique vulnerability to blunt trauma and subsequent infection.</note> — He is also, however, the image of a person who fears hollow things, because he flees from the noise of a drum like a hare. — A wolf without a tail designates a person who has been driven to the extreme and seeks to save himself only through the smallest possible loss. This animal is said to actually [pull out/bite off] its hair with

## The Stag

The image of haste and fleetingness, of violent desire, of vain pomp, and of temptation. A stag with an adder <note>original: "Otter"; here referring generally to a venomous snake.</note> indicates a person who acts hastily without judgment; for at the sight of an adder, the stag plunges blindly through the thicket toward the utmost danger. A stag with a flute player is the image of a person who allows himself to be captivated by the sweet tones of flattery; for he easily falls into the hunters' snare when they strike up some lively song. <note>It was a common belief in medieval bestiaries that stags were so charmed by music that they would stand still and allow themselves to be captured.</note> A stag with a snake is the image of failed cunning; as the snake is said to be made dizzy by the breath of a stag. <note>This draws on the ancient legend that stags could draw snakes out of their burrows by blowing air into them, then trampling or eating them to rejuvenate themselves.</note>

Dogs were also kept as guards at temples and sanctuaries; and when once in Athens a temple robber escaped the watchful dog, he was nonetheless subsequently discovered and punished through the incessant barking and pursuit of this dog whenever it caught sight of him. <note>This likely refers to the story of the dog of the Parthenon, mentioned by authors like Plutarch and Aelian, which pursued a thief named Candidus until he was caught.</note>

A dog drinking from the Nile while running signifies caution in human life, even amidst the most necessary pleasures <note>original: "Wollust"; here meaning physical gratification or appetite rather than just sexual lust.</note>; for Egyptian dogs are said not to stand still in one place while drinking until they are satisfied, but rather quench their thirst while running, because they are not certain that some lurking crocodile might not dash forth and devour them. Hence the passage in Phaedrus <note>Phaedrus (c. 15 BC – c. AD 50) was a Roman fabulist who adapted Aesop's fables into Latin verse.</note> in the fable: *The Crocodile and the Dog*:

->--- Lick as much as you like, and quickly.<-
->Come closer, drink at your leisure, and do not fear<-
->any trickery. But the other replied: "By Hercules, I would do so,<-
->did I not know that you are greedy for my flesh."<-
<note>original Latin: "Quamlibet lambe ocyus / Accede, pota leniter, et noli dolos / Vereri: At ille: facerem mehercule / Ni esse scirem carnis te cupidum meae."</note>

->--- Hear me!<-
->Do not drink so hastily, dear friend, come near,<-
->and fear nothing! — How gladly, by my faith,<-
->would I do so. Yet, you only lust for my<-
->flesh.<-
<note>This section is a German poetic translation of the Latin verse above. The dog represents the wise man who enjoys life's necessities with a wary eye on potential dangers.</note>

## The Donkey

The image of slavery, laziness, and ignorance, but also of perseverance. The Ambraciots <note>original: "Ambrasier," residents of Ambracia in ancient Greece</note> dedicated a donkey in the temple of the Delphic Apollo to commemorate a nocturnal victory over the Molossians <note>The braying of a donkey reportedly alerted the Ambraciots to a night attack</note>. — A donkey with laundry equipment and coals on its back is the image of a person who strives to teach and improve others, but never themselves. — — A running donkey is the image of a beginning diligence that soon fades; of a hasty undertaking that comes to nothing. —

The Nauplians <note>original: "Nauplier," inhabitants of the port city Nauplia</note> in the Peloponnese honored the image of the donkey during wine-drinking, because, according to legend, they had the donkey to thank for its discovery <note>This refers to the legend that a donkey's grazing taught humans the benefit of pruning grapevines</note>; they thereby showed that one must not refuse gratitude even to the lowliest, nor be ashamed to learn from him! —

who did not yet know it, even in a dream—from which he awoke—such a decision was indicated beforehand. Without, by the way, speaking in favor of dreams here, or opening up a new, limitless field for them, it is certain that our dreams—the so-called "remarkable" ones—are for the most part symbols or figurative; they almost always require an explanation or interpretation first. Thus, common sense can never find a reason to pay attention to them. However, the matter itself is not yet so entirely settled. Symbolic representations lie in the essence and nature of the soul; language is an art and is different everywhere, whereas those symbols are the same everywhere and are universally understood.

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of donkey symbolism, transitioning from historical portents and punishments for ignorance to a specific literary anecdote regarding the poet Simonides, before moving on to the symbolism of the fox.</meta>

The Epeian Donkey of Simonides had become a proverb for despotism <note>original: "Despotismus," used here to describe the arbitrary and self-serving rules imposed by the poet upon his students.</note>. This donkey was required to provide food and drink for Simonides, for which it received nothing more than a small portion of the fines collected from the students. For whoever appeared late in the lecture hall had to pay half a measure of barley for the hero Epeus; this was the mocking nickname given to the donkey. This was because the hero Epeus had likewise been the water-bearer for the Atreidae—the two sons of Atreus, Menelaus and Agamemnon <note>In Greek mythology, Epeus is most famous for building the Trojan Horse, but in some traditions, he was a lowly water-carrier for the royal family of Argos.</note>. Hence the epigram:

->I say that he who does not wish to carry the prize of the cicada<-
->Shall provide a great feast for Epeus, the son of Panopeus.<-
<note>original: "Φημὶ τον ουκ εθελοντα φερειν τεττιγος αεθλον / Τῳ πανοπηιαδη δωσειν μεγα δειπνον Επειῳ." The "prize of the cicada" refers to singing or participating in the poetic chorus. If a student refused, they paid the fine that fed Simonides’s donkey.</note>

->Whoever here does not wish to compete, let him go,<-
->And give to Epeus a grand supper.<-

Which, however, was not received by the pack-animal Epeus, but rather by Simonides himself.

## The Fox.

The symbol of cunning <note>original: "Verschlagenheit"</note>, wicked schemes and plots, of fraud and guile. He throws himself down as if dead in the open field, and in this manner suddenly catches the approaching poultry, which does not suspect him. — When dogs surround him and he has neither a way out nor—

<meta>This page continues the description of the fox's cunning behaviors from the previous page, specifically detailing its deceptive hunting tactics, before introducing the symbolic meanings associated with the pig.</meta>

...knows <note>The text likely completes the thought from the previous page: "knows no other way out."</note>, he himself begins to bark like a dog in order to lead the others astray, and in this manner he not infrequently escapes destruction. He approaches porcupines with great caution, flattering them as if he only wanted to play with them and stroking them until, by repeatedly rolling them over, he suddenly seizes their soft parts, tears them apart, and consumes them. With equal cunning he catches water ducks; by hiding his head and holding his tail up like a duck’s neck until the little creature approaches him; then he quickly snatches and consumes it. Furthermore, he also sticks his bushy tail into the water until it is full of small fish; then he pulls it back, shakes out the little fish, and feeds himself with them.

## The Pig.

The image of filth and immorality, of gluttony and drunkenness; the image of a good-for-nothing, of a corrupt and lost person. Revelry <note>original: "Schwelgerey"</note> was also indicated by the image of this animal, as well as all excessive shouting and all despondency.

## The Hedgehog.

The symbol of caution and of constant armament against danger; the latter <note>Referring to "constant armament."</note> because he is armed with quills everywhere and curls himself into a round ball when attacked; the former <note>Referring to "caution."</note> because he provides his den with two openings, namely, one toward the north and one toward the south; and since he always has a premonition of the changing wind, he always closes the one from which the wind will come. The female is said, when she is pregnant, to always postpone the time of birth <note>original: "Werfens," literally "throwing," used for animals giving birth.</note> out of fear of the pain caused by the small quills of the young, and thereby only makes this task all the more difficult; therefore, it is the image of the cowardly postponement of a matter that must ultimately happen in the end anyway, and whose labor one only makes more burdensome through such delay.

## The Snake.

The image of slander <note>original: "Verläumdung"</note>, because this animal has no other limb with which it does harm than the tongue: [also the image] of rejuvenation through abstinence, because around the time the snake casts off its old skin, it fasts for forty days beforehand; then it squeezes through a narrow crack in a rock, in this way stripping off the old skin and shining joyfully with the new—

<note>The text ends mid-sentence, likely continuing to describe the snake's renewed appearance on the following page.</note>

## The Scorpion.

The image of murderous hatred. The scorpion originated, according to the opinion of the ancients, from the tail of the crocodile, which he kills with his poisonous sting. <note>This reflects a pre-scientific belief found in some ancient bestiaries that certain animals spontaneously generated from the remains of others.</note> Both animals were said to have such a burning hatred for one another that they commonly destroyed each other in battle; and both are therefore the image of the highest revenge. The scorpion is also the image of suicide in despair; for if one places fire all around him so that he can escape nowhere, he stabs himself with his own sting. <note>This is a long-standing myth; in reality, scorpions are immune to their own venom and their "stinging" motion near fire is a reflexive muscle spasm from the heat.</note> The scorpion, like the snake, is also the image of slander, because he is always ready to cause harm with his sting. Of this poison of slander, Martial <note>Marcus Valerius Martialis (c. 40 – c. 104 AD), a Roman poet famous for his biting wit and epigrams.</note> says:

> What will you not dare to speak, treacherous tongue:
> With you fashioning such wickedness, Orestes would have hated Pylades,
> [And] the love of Pirithous would have forsaken Theseus;
> <note>original: "Quid non audebis perfida lingua loqui: / Te fingente nefas Pyladen odisset Orestes / Thesea Pirithoi destituisset amor;" These pairs—Orestes and Pylades, and Theseus and Pirithous—were the most famous examples of inseparable friendship in Greek mythology.</note>

### The Salamander.

The image of steadfastness in the greatest suffering; because the salamander is said not only to live in fire, but even to extinguish it. <note>This is a common myth from ancient natural history, popularized by Pliny the Elder. In reality, salamanders often hibernated in damp logs; when the logs were placed on a fire, the creatures would scurry out, leading observers to believe they were born from or could survive the flames.</note> King Francis I <note>Francis I (reigned 1515–1547), a Renaissance King of France, famously used the salamander as his personal emblem with the motto "Nutrisco et extinguo" (I nourish [the good] and extinguish [the bad]).</note> had as his motto:

The fierce bear, the grim eagles, and the twisting snake
Have now yielded to your flame, O Salamander.
<note>original: "Ursus atrox, aquilaeve truces et tortilis anguis / Cesserunt flammae jam Salamandra tuae."</note>

Before the salamander’s fire,
Fear struck the bear, the eagle, and the snake.

The salamander is everywhere the image of the most sublime virtue, of patience and composure, which Horace <note>Quintus Horatius Flaccus (65–8 BC), a leading Roman lyric poet.</note> praises in the following stanza:

Thus the brave race from burned Ilion,
Tossed on the Tuscan seas, carried their sacred rites,
Their children, and their aged fathers
Through to the Ausonian cities.
<note>original: "Sic gens cremato fortis ab Ilio / Jactata Tuscis æquoribus, sacra / Gnatosque, maturosque patres / Pertulit Ausonias ad urbes." From Horace’s Odes (Book IV, 4). It describes the resilience of the Trojans (the "brave race") who survived the fires of Troy (Ilion) to reach Italy (Ausonia).</note>

So the brave people carried away from Ilion’s flames,
Through Tuscan storms,
The children, fathers, and the gods
Of their homeland to the cities of Ausonia.

## The Scorpion.

The image of deadly hatred. The scorpion originated, according to the opinion of the ancients, from the tail of the crocodile, which it then killed with its poisonous sting. <note>This reflects a bizarre theory in ancient "spontaneous generation" where one creature was thought to be born from the decaying parts of another.</note> Both animals were said to harbor such a burning hatred for one another that they usually destroyed each other in battle; both are therefore the image of the highest revenge.

The scorpion is also the image of suicide in despair; for if one places fire all around it so that it can escape nowhere, it stabs itself with its own sting. <note>While a popular myth in the 18th and 19th centuries, scorpions do not actually commit suicide; the "stinging" motion is a muscular spasm caused by the heat, and they are immune to their own venom.</note> Furthermore, the scorpion, like the snake, is the image of slander, because it is always ready to cause harm with its sting. Of this poison of slander, Martial <note>Marcus Valerius Martialis (c. 38–104 AD), a Roman poet famous for his short, witty, and often biting epigrams.</note> says:

> What will you not dare to speak, treacherous tongue?
> With you fashioning an unspeakable crime, Orestes would have hated Pylades,
> And the love of Pirithous would have forsaken Theseus;
> <note>original: "Quid non audebis perfida lingua loqui: / Te fingente nefas Pyladen odisset Orestes / Thesea Pirithoi destituisset amor;" Martial lists famous pairs of inseparable friends from Greek mythology—Orestes and Pylades, Theseus and Pirithous—to show that a slanderer's lies are powerful enough to break even the strongest bonds of loyalty.</note>

...thing the ancients believed would appear during storms.

The ancients believed that the salamander appears most frequently during violent storms and tempests, especially when incessant downpours cause widespread flooding.

Others have conversely applied this image to rebels and thieves, who are most likely to appear during general conflagrations and civil wars.

## The Crane.

A flying crane is the symbol of the sublime, of advanced knowledge, and of astronomy <term>astronomy: at the time, this often referred to the study of the heavens as a source of divine or philosophical wisdom</term>, because it soars above the clouds during its flight. It fears no tempest and is therefore the image of fearlessness, as well as vigilance and prudence, because it stands on only one foot while sleeping. <note>In traditional heraldry and animal lore, the crane was said to hold a stone in its raised foot while sleeping; if the crane fell into a deep sleep, the stone would drop and wake it, making the bird a symbol of constant watchfulness.</note> It is steadfast and constant—a quality that extends even to its feathers, whose color remains unchanged by time or weather. Consequently, it represents a settled mind which, as Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), the Roman Stoic philosopher who taught that true happiness comes from inner stability.</note> says, is self-contained and self-sufficient: It is the mark of a well-composed mind to stand with oneself and to dwell within oneself. <note>original: "Bene compositæ mentis, secum consistere, secum morari."</note>

<summary>This page concludes the entry on the salamander, linking it to social unrest, and introduces the crane as a symbol of scholarly pursuit, vigilance, and Stoic self-sufficiency.</summary>
<keywords>salamander, crane, astronomy, vigilance, prudence, Seneca, Stoicism, symbols, animal lore</keywords>

## The Vulture.

The symbol of keen-sightedness and avarice, as well as the image of a "laughing heir." <note>A "laughing heir" is a legal and literary term for an heir who is so distantly related to the deceased that they feel no grief, only joy at their inheritance.</note> The Egyptians represented various hieroglyphs through this bird. It was the image of a mother, because it was believed that there were no male vultures, but only females, which were impregnated by the wind at certain times. <note>This was a common belief in antiquity, recorded by writers like Plutarch and Horapollo, who claimed vultures were a symbol of motherhood and nature.</note>

The vulture was therefore also the image of the year, because it was assumed that it divided the year into three parts through its activities: it was said to be pregnant for 120 days, to require 120 days for the upbringing of its young, and another 120 days for self-care; the five remaining days of the year, however, were used for new conception by the wind. <note>Ancient calendars often consisted of 360 days plus 5 "epagomenal" or intercalary days.</note>

War was also represented by this bird, which was considered a true omen of conflict; it was believed that even in the field, seven days in advance, the vulture would indicate the location where a battle would occur and where there would be the most corpses for it to scavenge. Consequently, ancient generals always sent out scouts who had to pay close attention and report where the most vultures were gathering; from this, they sought to determine in advance the potential loss of life in both their own and the enemy's army, and thus predict whether victory would fall to their side or the enemy's.

<summary>This page describes the vulture as a symbol of greed, motherhood, and the passage of time, while highlighting its historical role as a supernatural omen used by generals to predict battle outcomes.</summary>
<keywords>vulture, Egyptian hieroglyphs, motherhood, parthenogenesis, calendar year, omen of war, military scouts, avarice</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of animal symbolism, following previous entries on natural history and moral emblems.</meta>

The ancients <note>The author likely refers to classical naturalists such as Pliny the Elder.</note> held that the salamander shows itself most often during violent storms and tempests, and when incessant downpours flood everything.

Others have inversely applied this image to rebels and robbers, who show themselves most frequently during general conflagrations <note>Meaning widespread fires or social outbreaks.</note> and civil wars.

## The Crane

A flying crane is the symbol of the sublime, of high knowledge, and of <term>astronomy</term> <note>Literally "star-knowledge" (Sternkunde).</note>; because it raises itself above the clouds in its flight. It fears no storm and is therefore the image of fearlessness, as well as vigilance and prudence, in that it stands on only one foot while sleeping <note>This refers to a popular medieval and Renaissance belief that a crane holds a stone in its lifted claw; if it falls asleep, the stone drops and the noise wakes the bird, ensuring constant watchfulness.</note>. It is steadfast and constant, a quality that extends even to its feathers, the color of which is changed by no amount of time or weather; therefore, it is the symbol of a composed mind which, as Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – AD 65), a Stoic philosopher.</note> says, is wrapped in itself and is sufficient unto itself. It is the mark of a well-composed mind to remain with itself and to dwell within itself. <note>original Latin: "Bene compositæ mentis, secum consistere, secum morari." From Seneca's Moral Letters to Lucilius.</note>

# The Vulture

The symbol of keen-sightedness and <term>avarice</term> <note>From the German "Haabsucht," referring to a greedy or grasping nature.</note>, also the image of a laughing heir <note>The "laughing heir" is a traditional figure who feigns sorrow but is actually delighted by their inheritance.</note>. The Egyptians represented various hieroglyphs through this bird. It was the image of a mother, because it was believed that there were no male vultures, but only females, which were impregnated by the wind at certain times <note>This ancient myth is found in the works of writers like Horapollo and Plutarch, who claimed vultures conceived via the North Wind.</note>.

The vulture was therefore also the image of the year, because it was assumed that it divided the year into three parts with its activities: it was pregnant for 120 days, required 120 for the upbringing of its young, and spent the following 120 caring for itself; the five remaining days <note>120+120+120+5 = 365, the days of a solar year.</note>, however, were used for a new conception by the wind.

War was also represented by this bird; it was even considered a genuine omen of war, and it was said that even in the field, it would indicate the location seven days in advance where a battle would occur and where there would be the most corpses for it to feed upon. For this reason, ancient generals always sent out scouts who had to pay close attention and report where the most vultures were gathering; from this, they sought to determine in advance the loss of life in both their own and the enemy’s army, and thereby predict whether victory would fall to their side or to the enemy’s.

**Vocabulary:** Vulture, Egyptians, hieroglyphs, keen-sightedness, avarice, war, generals, symbol.

<summary>This page discusses the vulture's symbolism in Egyptian and classical thought, highlighting its association with motherhood, the solar year, and its supposed ability to predict the outcomes of battles.</summary>
<keywords>vulture, Egyptian hieroglyphs, motherhood, solar year, omens, warfare, ancient military strategy, avarice</keywords>

...can contract and expand, it is almost insatiable; for it can store a considerable number of fish and a large amount of water within it, and for this reason it is also easily trained for fishing. The *pelican* is furthermore also the image of stupidity; for although it could lay its eggs on high ground, it instead lays them openly on the flat earth and thereby becomes a prey for bird-catchers, who surround [the nests] with glowing coals covered by brushwood. As soon as the *pelican* notices the smoke rising from them, it hurries over to extinguish the fire with its wings, but by doing so it only fans the flames all the more, so that it singes its wings in the process and falls into the hands of the hunters. The *pelicans* were therefore eaten only by the common people in *Egypt*, but not by the priests.
