The swan was also the symbol of an elderly poet and musician, hence the well-known metaphor of the "swan song." <term>Aelian</term> <note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175 – c. 235 AD), a Roman author who wrote 'On the Nature of Animals'.</note> likewise mentions a solitary bird, the <term>Cyanus</term> <note>original: "Cyanus"; likely the Blue Rock Thrush (*Monticola solitarius*), which lives in rocky, isolated terrain.</note>, which loves only wildernesses, mountain peaks, abysses, and remote, desolate islands; and a poetic, perhaps somewhat misanthropic hermit expresses his feelings about it in the following verses:

> <note>The following is a translation of the original Latin poem: "Me jam desertis permittas degere petris..."</note>
> Let me now spend my time among deserted rocks,
> For every house, every human face—of whatever
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;kind it may be—
> Recalls all the former evils to my troubled mind.
> Now the burning shoals and inhospitable wastes,
> Whether a swamp with greening slime, or black rocks
> In their retreat, hold me more unrefined, where with the sun removed
> Hollow caves preserve their ancient darkness;
> Or leave me prostrate on the high, jutting cliffs,
> Taking my rest upon the cold turf.

> <note>The following is a translation of the German poetic version of the Latin text above.</note>
> Let me only linger among the desolate rocks;
> For every house, every human form
> Leads me back to all the sufferings I have endured—
> I am satisfied by burning deserts, inhospitable wastes,
> Mud-green swamps, or black rocky clefts,
> Where, unadorned, no sunbeam reaches me;
> Where eternal night dwells in the deepest mountain caves,
> Or let me, upon high overhanging cliffs,
> Seek a little rest upon the cold moss.

# The Goose.

The image of stupidity and aversion toward arts and sciences. It was once believed that the goose had a deadly loathing for the laurel tree <note>In classical tradition, the laurel was sacred to Apollo, the god of music, poetry, and wisdom; therefore, the "stupid" goose was thought to naturally flee from it.</note>. A flying goose with a stone in its beak was the image of clever discretion. It was said that wild geese, when flying in rows over high mountains, would hold a stone in their beaks to suppress any sound they might involuntarily make, so as not to be noticed by eagles, of whom they are very afraid <note>This legend appears in various ancient natural histories, such as those by Plutarch and St. Ambrose, to illustrate the virtue of silence.</note>.

# The Great Bustard.

The image of feebleness and incapacity, or clumsiness. It often weighs 20 to 30 pounds and therefore cannot fly well. A <term>bustard</term> <note>original: "Trappe" or "Trappgans"; the Great Bustard (*Otis tarda*), one of the heaviest living flying birds, known for its difficult take-off.</note> fleeing from a barking dog signifies the lazy and the incorrigible who do not wish to be taught. Bustards are said generally to fear dogs very much.

<summary>This page describes the symbolic meanings of the goose and the bustard, contrasting the goose's supposed stupidity with a legendary example of its caution, and the bustard's physical weight with the vice of laziness.</summary>
<keywords>Goose, Great Bustard, symbolism, stupidity, silence, laziness, natural history, animal metaphors</keywords>

## The Magpie.

The image of a chatterer and a liar; for whoever is always talking cannot possibly produce nothing but the truth; rather, usually very little and often nothing at all of it is true. The magpie mimics all sorts of syllables, words, and sounds, but alters them as it pleases in various ways, and is therefore also the image of an inconstant person <note>Original: "Wankelmüthiger," describing someone fickle, flighty, or wavering in their convictions.</note> who is of a different opinion every moment and asserts whatever he wishes according to his own whim; of whom Horace <note>Quintus Horatius Flaccus (65–8 BC), a leading Roman lyric poet.</note> also says:

<note>original: "Mille adde catenas / Effugiet tamen hæ sceleratus vincula Proteus; / Quum rapies in ius malis ridentem alienis, / Fiet aper modo auis, modo saxum et cum vo- / let arbor." — These lines are from Horace's Satires, II.3.71, describing the shape-shifting sea god Proteus to illustrate how a debtor or liar evades responsibility.</note>

->Yet this wicked Proteus will escape these bonds;<-
->When you drag him to court as he laughs with another’s jaws,<-
->He will become now a boar, now a bird, now a rock, and when<-
->he wishes, a tree.<-

# The Phoenix.

Even though this bird has long been recognized as a mythical subject, it is nonetheless such an important hieroglyph <note>In this context, the author uses "hieroglyph" to mean a sacred symbol or emblem used by the ancient Egyptians to convey complex spiritual ideas, rather than literal writing.</note> of the Egyptians that one must not overlook it. Many are also of the opinion that the ancients understood the Bird of Paradise <note>The Bird of Paradise was often confused with the phoenix in early modern natural history because of its vibrant plumage and the myth that it lived entirely in the air without feet.</note> to be this creature. According to the fable, the phoenix is said to live for many centuries, and only one is ever to be found at a time. The phoenix was the image of the sun and of a very great age. 

This bird also symbolized a person who left their homeland for foreign parts while young and only returned in their old age. For this was precisely the way of the phoenix; it departed Egypt young and only returned after many centuries to die there, where it was then honored with great funeral pomp. Through the phoenix, one also prophetically signified a new state of things after many centuries; in that through its strange birth, as it were, a trans- <note>The text breaks off here at "Vers-", likely the beginning of "Versjüngung" (rejuvenation) or "Verwandlung" (transformation).</note>

<vocab>Proteus, Phoenix, Hieroglyph, Egyptians, Bird of Paradise, Sun, Age, Homeland, Funeral pomp, Prophetic, Birth</vocab>

change and renewal occurred with it. Namely, the old, worn-out phoenix fell to the earth with such force that it received a wound, from the discharge <note>The German "Eiter" literally means pus; the text describes a less common version of the myth where the new bird is born from the bodily fluids of the dying parent rather than from its ashes.</note> of which a new phoenix emerged. This new bird, as soon as its wings had grown, flew with its father to Heliopolis <note>A major city in ancient Egypt, its name means "City of the Sun," and it was the center of solar worship.</note>, where the father died at the rising of the sun; whereupon the young phoenix flew away again and left the sacred corpse to the priests.

However, the fable varies even on this point. According to Herodotus <note>A famous Greek historian (c. 484–425 BC), often called the "Father of History."</note>, the old phoenix encloses the young one in an egg made of myrrh-wood and flies with it to the Altar of the Sun; or as Lactantius <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD) was an early Christian author; his poem *De Ave Phoenice* is one of the most famous literary treatments of this legend.</note> says:

> ->With balsamic ointment, myrrh, and dissolved incense,<-
> ->He preserves it, and shapes it into a ball with a pious mouth;<-
> ->Carrying it with his feet, he hastens toward the rising of the sun,<-
> ->And alighting upon the altar, he places it in the sacred temple.<-

The phoenix, says another, is more beautiful than the peacock, which shines only with gold and silver colors; but the phoenix gleams with emeralds, hyacinths <note>In ancient and early modern texts, "hyacinth" usually refers to a blue or violet gemstone, perhaps a sapphire or zircon, rather than the flower.</note>, and other precious gemstone colors, having a crown on its head and feathered spurs on its feet. It lives for many centuries without food or drink, solely on the wind. As its end approaches, however, before it flies to Heliopolis for the sacrifice, it studs its wings with all manner of spices. Upon its arrival, it alights on the altar, where it is soon reduced to ashes...

## The Bird of Paradise,
### called Apus, the Footless.

Had the Egyptian fable given the Phoenix no feet, or had not another fable—or perhaps a common custom—taken the feet from the Bird of Paradise, then the latter would certainly be the true Phoenix of the Egyptians; at the very least, in terms of its actual existence, it would no longer be a mere fable. Since, however, the Bird of Paradise has truly had feet from the beginning, it can still be considered the Phoenix of the ancients. It possesses consistently magnificent, gold-shimmering feathers that display a play of all sorts of colors, and long feet which are, however, almost entirely hidden by its long plumage. It lives on the Spice Islands <note>The Moluccas in modern-day Indonesia, then the global center of the spice trade.</note>, where it feeds on spices and insects. The Indians <note>A historical term used here by the author to refer to the indigenous peoples of the East Indies.</note> adorn themselves with its feathers. They also cut off its feet and sell the bird to foreigners as a "footless wonder." <note>It was a common historical misconception in Europe that Birds of Paradise lived their entire lives in flight because the specimens traded by local hunters had their legs removed during preservation.</note>

# The Bird of Paradise,
## called Apus, the Footless. <note>The name "Apus" is derived from the Greek "apous," meaning "without feet."</note>

Had the Egyptian fable given the Phoenix no feet, or had another fable or custom not taken the feet away from the Bird of Paradise, then the latter would certainly be the true Phoenix of the Egyptians and, at least regarding its actual existence, would no longer be a fable. Since, however, the Bird of Paradise has truly had feet from the beginning of time, it can still pass for the Phoenix of the ancients. It possesses magnificent feathers throughout, shimmering with gold and playing with all sorts of colors, as well as long feet which are, however, almost entirely covered by its long feathers. It lives on the Spice Islands <note>The Moluccas in modern-day Indonesia, which were the world's primary source of nutmeg and cloves during the era of early exploration.</note>, where it feeds on spices and insects. The Indians <note>In this historical context, "Indians" refers to the indigenous peoples of the East Indies or Malay Archipelago.</note> adorn themselves with its feathers. They also cut off its feet and sell it to foreigners as a footless miracle—

### The Parrot.

The image of docility and empty chatter; also of a person who loves finery <note>The original German "Putz" refers to showy dress or ornamentation.</note>, but otherwise concerns themselves with nothing else and fathoms nothing, merely parroting the sense and nonsense of others. A young parrot learns easily and fears the rod <note>A reference to the "Schulrute" or birch rod, historically used for corporal punishment in teaching.</note>; but an old one can be brought neither by kindness nor by severity to repeat even the slightest thing; hence the proverb: Se- <note>This is a catchword, indicating the first syllable of the next page, likely continuing the proverb "Seltner als ein weißer Rabe" (rarer than a white raven) or a similar phrase starting with "Se-".</note>

Several parrots symbolize collective foresight and diligence for the common good. For when several graze together, one or two of them always keep watch on the treetops so that the others may eat in peace, and, as soon as the slightest danger is present, they are notified by them so that they can fly away in time. But if everything remains quiet and the others have eaten their fill, then the sentinels also come forward and enjoy their own share.

So that everyone may be protected by him alone.

# The Kingfisher.

The image of marital love and faithfulness, and of domestic industry. The kingfisher, about the size of a sparrow, has beautifully variegated feathers; the head and wings are mostly blue-green, and the remaining part is white and purple-colored. It frequents places rich in water, and in winter stays by large rivers, because it lives on fish. It makes its nest in the earth from fish bones. Mythology <note>original German: *Fabellehre*</note> tells us of two persons who were transformed into kingfishers: Alcyone, daughter of Aeolus and wife of Ceyx, and Halcyon, son of Lucifer <note>In this mythological context, Lucifer refers to the morning star (Phosphorus), the father of Ceyx in Greek myth.</note>, after he suffered shipwreck. Plutarch <note>Plutarch (c. 46–120 AD) was a Greek philosopher and biographer known for his writings on the virtues of animals.</note> calls the kingfisher the wisest and most ingenious water bird, and the ancients believed that it did not decay; the female is said to be so devoted to the male that she cares for him even in extreme old age and carries him on her back when he can no longer move, and they were called the "happy couple" described to us by Horace <note>Horace (65–8 BC) was a Roman lyric poet.</note>.

<vocab>Parrots, Kingfisher, Mythology, Alcyone, Aeolus, Ceyx, Halcyon, Lucifer, Plutarch, Horace</vocab>

## The Woodpecker.

The image of **perseverance** <note>original: *Unverdrossenheit*</note> and **cleverness**; because it was believed that by pecking with its beak at the trees, it could judge from the sound whether the wood was firm or loose, in order to more easily hollow it out for a nest, in which task it would let no effort discourage it. Furthermore, it was said that if someone were to block its nest with a stone, it would seek out a herb that the stone cannot tolerate and stuff the stone with it, so that the stone—as if offended by the plant—would come loose and the nest would open again, and so on; all of which is very wonderfully and beautifully imagined. — The only truth is that the woodpecker, which lives on insects, if it does not find enough on the outside of the trees, will drill through them if they are hollow, stick its beak into the opening, and raise its voice to [attract] the little creatures

### The Hoopoe.

The image of an insufferable dandy <note>original: *Stutzer*</note>, a peacock-like <note>The original German *fauischen* is likely a variation of *pfauisch*, referring to the vanity of a peacock.</note> person in beautiful clothes. A hoopoe with wall-pellitory <term>Wall-pellitory: A common herb (*Parietaria*) often found growing on old walls, used in traditional medicine.</term> in its beak signifies a remedy against drunkenness or against an overindulgence of grapes; for the hoopoe is said, if it has become sick from overeating grapes, to regain its health in a short time through the help of the wall-pellitory that it seeks out for itself.

### The Dove.

The opposite of the previous [entry], the image of innocence and cleanliness, tenderness and gentleness. It was believed that doves had no gall <note>In ancient and medieval physiology, the gall bladder was thought to be the source of bitterness and anger; its perceived absence in the dove made the bird a symbol of perfect sweetness.</note>, and would not be attacked by the plague during times of pestilence, just as those who ate them at that time remained free from it. Consequently, no other food was then served to the King. A dove with a laurel leaf in its beak is the symbol of medicine; because when it is sick, it carries laurel leaves into its nest and

Thus, *Aelianus* <note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175 – c. 235 AD) was a Roman author and teacher of rhetoric known for his works on natural history and anecdotes.</note> relates that the famous *Aspasia* <note>Aspasia of Phocaea, a woman famed for her beauty who became the favorite concubine of Cyrus the Younger.</note>, daughter of a poor man named *Hermotimus* from *Phocis*, developed a large boil on her chin. Because of her poverty, she could not afford a doctor, but in a dream, she saw a dove that transformed into a woman. This figure spoke words of encouragement and advised her to crush dried rose-garlands of *Venus* and apply them to the boil. This remedy worked so well that she regained her former beauty. When she subsequently attained great wealth through her acquaintance with the young *Cyrus*, she commissioned a large statue of *Venus* featuring a dove inlaid with precious stones.

## The Hoopoe.

The image of an insufferable dandy—a slovenly person dressed in fine clothes <note>In European folklore, the hoopoe was often depicted as beautiful but "filthy" because it was believed to build its nest using animal dung.</note>. A hoopoe with wall-pellitory <term>wall-pellitory: (Parietaria officinalis), a herb traditionally used in folk medicine for its cooling and diuretic properties</term> in its beak signifies a remedy against drunkenness or against an overindulgence in grapes; for the hoopoe is said, if it has become sick from gorging on grapes, to recover its health in a short time with the aid of the wall-pellitory that it seeks out for itself.

## The Dove.

The opposite of the former: the image of innocence and cleanliness, tenderness and gentleness. It was believed that doves possessed no gall <note>original: "keine Galle". In the system of four humors, the gall or bile was the source of bitterness and anger; its absence explained the dove's peaceful nature.</note>, and that they were not attacked by the plague during times of pestilence; likewise, those who ate them during such times were said to remain free from the disease. Consequently, no other food was served to the King at those times. ## The Rooster.

The symbol of prophetic power, watchfulness, combativeness, male dominance, and high-flown pride. The rooster was also the image of victory and sacred to Mars <note>The Roman god of war; the rooster’s aggressive nature made it a fitting companion for a war deity.</note>. It signified the comfort of sleepless patients and other sorrowful people who long for the refreshing light of day, the arrival of which the rooster announces.

## The Hen.

The image of motherly love, fertility, security, and abstinence; because she, when she is brooding <note>The act of sitting on eggs to hatch them.</note>, fasts the entire day, and as soon as she has eaten a little, immediately returns back to her eggs. A mother hen...

### The Partridge.

The image of insatiability and thievery; in that it is not satisfied with being fertile enough itself and laying a multitude of eggs, but also robs other nests and carries the eggs into its own <note>This folklore stems from ancient natural history (like Pliny) and the Bible (Jeremiah 17:11), which claims partridges steal and hatch the eggs of others, only for the chicks to eventually fly back to their true mothers.</note>. The chicks of the partridge represent a premature development of the mind and a love for the sciences; yet at the same time, they signify an untimely desire and the vanity of showing off and boasting of one's knowledge. The chicks are said to be unable to wait for the proper incubation period, instead pecking through the shell with force, and as soon as they have emerged with only their head and feet, they run about for a good while with half the egg still attached while searching for food.

### The Black Grouse.

The image of boastfulness and "stinking self-praise," <note>A reference to the German proverb "Eigenlob stinkt" (self-praise stinks), meaning that those who brag about themselves only make themselves look bad.</note> because it shouts its own name exceedingly loud. Several black grouse signify—

The **black grouse** is also the image of a sincere zealot against evil; for the fable says that when the black grouse first came from Lydia <note>Lydia: An ancient kingdom in western Asia Minor, in what is now Turkey.</note> to Egypt, a plague arose due to the flooding of the Nile which claimed many lives. Then these birds of misfortune flew everywhere and cried out with the clear and audible voice of a boy: "Three times evil to the evil ones" <note>original Greek: "τρὶς τοῖς κακοῖς τὰ κακά"</note>.

### The Nightingale.

The symbol of the **musical arts** <term>Tonkunst: The art of music or sound; often used in German to imply the spiritual and technical mastery of composition.</term> and **eloquence**, of peace of mind and innocence, as well as true greatness and skill, which loves solitude and darkness. A nightingale on the back of a **dolphin** signifies high esteem for music. It was said that a dolphin carried her across the sea and delighted in her song. The Thracian nightingales that nested around the grave of **Orpheus** <note>Orpheus: The legendary Greek hero and musician whose songs were so beautiful they could charm all living things and even the gods of the underworld.</note> were held—

to be the most ingenious and penetrating; because it was believed that the spirit of the immortalized poet <note>Referring to Orpheus, the legendary Greek musician.</note>, hovering around them, filled them, and that they shared the same temperament as his high innocence; for if one wanted to name a truly innocent life, one called it an **Orphic Life** <term>Orpheisches Leben: A life lived in harmony with nature, music, and purity, named after Orpheus.</term>; and a calm, innocent soul that delights in the fine arts, a **Nightingale-Soul**. And if one wished to depict an oppressor of innocence, whom an evil conscience would not allow to rest because of some abominable deed, one portrayed a person dining on a dish of nightingales. The meat of these birds was held to be useful for promoting wakefulness, but this quality was transformed in a moral sense into a tormenting insomnia. Such **nightingale-eaters** were considered neither wise nor good men; for since sleep, according to **Homer** <note>Homer: The semi-legendary Greek author of the Iliad and the Odyssey.</note>, was the blessing of gods and men, such a wretched striving to induce intentional sleeplessness could not suggest a good spirit. Tyrants and the ambitious—creatures who are only harmful to humanity—could not sleep, as was rightly believed, because of all their foolish and destructive schemes; only the virtuous man, the farmer, and everyone who, like the deity, makes himself useful and purposeful for everyone—

## The Garden Warbler.

The image of poverty and industry, as well as the striving for skill. The **garden warbler** <term>Grasmücke: A small songbird known for its modest appearance but exceptionally beautiful song, often compared to the nightingale.</term> has a poor nest and a wretched appearance, but a lovely song that comes very close to the notes of the nightingale. Extraordinary abilities are usually found more among the poor than among the rich; their meager food keeps their **vital spirits** <term>Lebensgeister: In historical medicine, these were believed to be subtle fluids that carried life and thought through the body.</term> pure and their souls bright, and they are moreover patient and mild. 

On the other hand, the dainties and abundance of the rich suffocate their abilities by clouding their souls and overstraining their vital spirits, only to make them more sluggish and dull, and rendering their character intolerant, passionate, and hard; for reason dwells only in a sober soul. The poor man helps himself through his own industry, even if—as is usually the case—he is not promoted; for the rich of that sort are too noble and too unfeeling for such a task; he is satisfied if one simply does not hinder his industry.

<vocab>Garden warbler, Poverty, Industry, Nightingale, Reason, Vital spirits, Character</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the garden warbler as a moral symbol for the industrious poor, arguing that a humble life preserves the soul's clarity and talent better than the "clouded" luxury of the rich.</summary>
<keywords>garden warbler, poverty, industry, vital spirits, class comparison, moral philosophy</keywords>

## The Siskin.

The image of docility, as well as of a humble person who raises himself from the dust; for the **siskin** <term>Zeisig: A small, greenish-yellow finch, historically popular as a caged bird because of its ability to learn tricks.</term> builds its nest on the highest peaks of the trees; it is also easily tamed and can be trained for all sorts of tricks.

## The Swallow.

The symbol of spring, of the art of building, of talkativeness, and a love of sociability; also the image of a messenger of victory during the dangerous, secret ambushes of enemies. **Alexander the Great** <note>Alexander III of Macedon (356–323 BCE). The omen mentioned here is a famous anecdote from his campaigns in Asia.</note> once had a multitude of swallows fly around his head, which was considered a good omen; whereupon he soon won a glorious victory and thwarted all the schemes of his enemies. When **Caecina Volaterranus** <note>Aulus Caecina of Volaterrae, a Roman knight. Pliny the Elder records that he used swallows to send news of race results back to his home by painting them the color of the winning faction.</note> went out against the enemy, he took a multitude of swallows with him, and when he had defeated them, he let them fly back painted with the color of victory, by which means the swiftest news of his victory was received. One also attributed a special building-spirit to the swallows and credited them with the famous **Chelidonian Wall** <term>Chelidonian Wall: From the Greek word "chelidon" for swallow; a legendary embankment or dam on the Nile.</term>; a high dam on the banks of the Nile, which the swallows from myriads of small 

<summary>This page describes the siskin as a symbol of humble social climbing and docility, and the swallow as a symbol of spring, architecture, and a messenger of military victory.</summary>
<keywords>Siskin, Swallow, Alexander the Great, Caecina Volaterranus, Chelidonian Wall, Nile, omens, messenger birds</keywords>

Pebbles gathered together so that the current does not flood the land prematurely. This great symbol was also used to denote the boundaries that the greater power of the small or the multitude opposes to the much lesser force of tyrants and the great; it was also intended as a warning never to side with such a monster, but rather with that multitude.

> Learn, I pray, what trust can be placed in small monsters, <note>original: "Disce, precor, breuibus quæ sit fiducia monstris"</note>

### The Wren. <note>The German name for the wren is *Zaunkönig*, literally "King of the Fence."</note>

The image of an industrious person who seeks to nourish himself in every way. The wren in the maw of the crocodile is the image of the community and the alliance of the mighty with the small and lowly who are useful to them. Leeches <term>Blutigel: archaic spelling of *Blutegel*, blood-sucking worms</term> sometimes settle in the crocodile's mouth, which the wren pulls out for it while it rests on the shore; for which service, however, the monster gives him nothing more, but considers it reward enough that it simply does not harm him or swallow him whole.

<meta>This page transitions from the political symbolism of small creatures—the wren and the leeches—to the symbolic meanings of aquatic life, shifting focus from land-based and amphibious allegories to the dominion of the sea.</meta>

# The Fish

The fish in general signified something detestable and hateful; it was also excluded from sacred meals. Nevertheless, the fish was the symbol of dominion over the sea, even according to the pronouncements of oracles. For once, when the Athenians were sacrificing on Delos <note>A Greek island and major religious center in antiquity.</note> and a boy, who had just drawn water from the sea, poured out fish along with it, the future mastery of the sea was prophesied to them from this event. And when Augustus <note>The text uses "August," referring to the first Roman Emperor, Caesar Augustus.</note> was very dejected because of the unfortunate battle against the Pompeians near Sicily, he recovered his spirits at once when a fish happened to leap out of the sea at his feet; for according to all interpretations by the priests, this was a sure sign that he would gain control over Sicily.

---

# The Swordfish <note>Literally "Rapier-fish" (*Degenfisch*); usually refers to the swordfish (*Xiphias gladius*).</note>

The image of a wealthy glutton <term>Prasser: a spendthrift or person who indulges excessively in food and drink, often squandering their fortune</term> who squanders everything through his throat and is therefore hated by all, just as this fish is hated by the others, for it even stalks whales and drags them away. Of such a glutton, Martial <note>Marcus Valerius Martialis, a Roman poet famous for his witty and biting epigrams.</note> says:

> You had given, Apicius, sixty million sesterces to your belly, <note>original: "Dederas, Apici, bis tricenties ventri,"</note>
> But ten million still remained to you in your extravagance; <note>original: "Sed adhuc supererat centies tibi laxum;" — This refers to the legendary Roman gourmand Apicius, who reportedly spent a fortune on food.</note>

<vocab>Fish, Symbol, Oracle, Athenians, Delos, Augustus, Pompeians, Sicily, Swordfish, Glutton, Martial, Apicius</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the symbolic nature of fish in antiquity, detailing how they could represent both detestable qualities and divine omens of maritime power, specifically regarding the Athenians and Emperor Augustus. It also introduces the swordfish as an allegory for destructive gluttony, citing the Roman poet Martial.</summary>
<keywords>Augustus, Sicily, Delos, Athenians, Swordfish, gluttony, Apicius, Martial, symbolism, oracles</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of aquatic symbolism, moving from the gluttony associated with the swordfish to the virtues of patience and the hazards of restless ambition, as illustrated through classical references and the "Adonis fish."</meta>

As for sleep, the Carthaginian <note>Hannibal, the famous general of Carthage who fought Rome in the Punic Wars.</note> <note>original: "Poenus vt ad somnos, vix totam cursibus actæ / Indulsit pubi noctem, vigil ille, nec vllam / Ad requiem facilis, redensque abscedere vitæ / Quod sopor eripiat tempus," from Silius Italicus’s epic poem *Punica*.</note> scarcely allowed the whole night to his troops, who were exhausted from their marches. He himself remained watchful and was not easily inclined toward any rest, believing that the time stolen by sleep was time taken away from his very life.

Scarcely did the Carthaginian general let
His army rest a little at night after heavy marches.
He himself was awake and inclined toward no sleep;
Because the time it stole from him
Seemed snatched away from his life itself.

### The Adonis Fish or Stone-fish <note>In historical natural history, the "Adonis fish" (sometimes associated with the Blenny) was famous for its ability to survive out of water in tidal pools, leading to its reputation for "patience."</note>

The image of patience and composure because, when left on dry land by the receding water or otherwise cast ashore, it patiently waits for the time when the water rises again, even enduring many teases from birds. It possesses a pleasant golden-yellow color, and through it, one signified a well-bred person of gentle manners who is beloved everywhere for his integrity and innocence—even if, for that very reason, he is envied and persecuted by certain flighty birds <note>Here used metaphorically for mischievous or untrustworthy people.</note> or quarrelsome and selfish monsters, wherein he easily does without what greed <term>Haabsucht: an older spelling of Habssucht, meaning an obsessive desire for possessions or avarice</term> refuses to let him keep.

A horse was once gifted to Emperor Henry <note>Likely referring to the Holy Roman Emperor Henry II (973–1024), known for his piety.</note> which had been purchased on the open market, but which had previously been stolen—

...[stolen] was. During his first pleasure ride, this soldier met him, who was bold enough to tell the Emperor that the horse belonged to him, as it had recently been stolen from him; and the head of justice <note>The "head of justice" refers to the Emperor himself in his role as the ultimate legal authority.</note> must grant him his rights in this matter. The Emperor said resolutely: “If the horse is yours, then take it along with the rider.” The soldier hesitated and took it for a joke. However, the Emperor forced him with his own hand to take the horse by the bridle and thus lead it, together with him, to his own property, where he settled the matter with him.

## The Aetna-Fish <note>Known in Latin as the *Aetnaeus*, this fish appears in ancient natural histories, specifically those of Aelian, and is associated with the waters around Mount Etna in Sicily.</note>

A fish in the Sicilian Sea, the image of chastity and marital fidelity. This fish, says Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175 – c. 235 AD) was a Roman author and teacher of rhetoric. His work *On the Nature of Animals* was a favorite source for later writers seeking moral lessons in the natural world.</note>, is extremely faithful to his spouse, without any law, without any dowry, and without fearing a Solon <note>Solon (c. 630 – c. 560 BC) was a famous Athenian lawmaker; he is used here as a symbol of the strict legal codes of human society.</note> nor fearing any punishment; but what use are even the noblest laws and all venerable republics, in which the wicked still do as they please!

<meta>This page continues the catalog of aquatic creatures and their moral allegories, moving from the Aetna-fish of the previous page to the Eel and the Kandhas.</meta>

## The Eel.

The image of vain people who allow themselves to be fooled by the wind of empty honor. The large, fat eels in the Nile were caught using intestines that were inflated with air; the eels would bite into them and, unable to immediately release their teeth, would suffocate from the swallowed air and be captured. This was therefore used to represent that aforementioned idea <note>The idea that vanity and the pursuit of "hot air" or empty praise leads to one's own destruction.</note>.

Since eels were also most frequently caught only during stormy weather, or when the ground was churned up with poles, the fishermen were compared to those restless republicans <note>In the historical context of this manuscript, "republicans" refers to political agitators or anti-monarchists seen as disrupting the "quiet" order of the state for personal gain.</note> who only stir up storms in the state so that they may better carry out their robberies. A cluster of eels was also used to designate "night owls" <note>German: *Nachtschwärmer*. In this period, the term referred to people who stayed out late for revelry or illicit activities.</note>, because they are only awake and gluttonous at night, while during the day they lie quietly in the mud.

## The Kandhas. <note>This likely refers to a species mentioned in ancient Greek natural histories, possibly related to the *cantharus* or a specific Indian sea creature described by writers like Aelian.</note>

A small fish in the Indian Sea, it was the image of silent merit that only becomes known after death; because this fish lives constantly hidden at the bottom of the sea, and only after death does it float upon its surface. Therefore, it served as a good example of *live in hiding* <note>original: λάθε (*lathe*). This is the first word of the Epicurean Greek maxim *lathe biōsas* (λάθε βιώσας), which means "live unnoticed" or "live in secrecy."</note>

<vocab>Eel, Kandhas, Nile, Republican, Night owl, Indian Sea, Merit, Live in hiding</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the moral symbolisms of the Eel—representing vanity, political agitation, and nocturnal revelry—and the Kandhas fish, which symbolizes silent merit and the virtue of living unnoticed.</summary>
<keywords>Eel, Kandhas, Nile, vanity, political unrest, republicans, Epicureanism, silent merit, natural history allegories</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page concludes the entry on the Kandhas fish from the previous page, specifically finishing the Greek and Latin motto regarding a life lived in obscurity, before moving on to the moral allegories of the Anthias, Scarus, and Callionymus.</meta>

...having lived <note>original: βιώσας (*biōsas*). This completes the phrase *lathe biōsas*, or "live in hiding."</note>; he who has hidden well, has lived well <note>original Latin: *qui bene latuit, bene vixit*</note>, or, to have lived in concealment is to have lived well.

## The Anthias and Scarus.

These sea fish, found near the **Chelidonian Islands** <note>A group of islands off the coast of Lycia in modern-day Turkey, known in antiquity for their treacherous currents.</note>, were the image of **mutual help and assistance**. They seek to save one another in every possible way—even at the risk of their own lives—whenever they have fallen into the fishermen's nets. This is despite the fact that they have never read any scholarly treatises on the duties of friendship or social loyalty.

## The Callionymus.

The image of a **bitter and angry person** <note>German: *Gallsüchtigen*. Literally "bile-addicted," referring to the ancient medical theory of the four humors where an excess of yellow bile caused a "choleric" or angry temperament.</note>. This is because this fish, in proportion to its body, contains an extraordinary amount of bile in its liver. Therefore, it is said of him quite rightly—to use the expression of **Horace**—that *the liver swells with bitter bile* <note>original Latin: *difficili bile tumet jecur*. From Horace’s Odes, describing the physical sensation of jealousy or rage.</note>. Yet, according to **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman naturalist who recorded various medicinal uses for animal parts in his *Natural History*.</note>, the bile of this fish has the benefit of being able to heal scars.

## The Capito.

The image of **abstinence** and **peaceableness**; because this fish attacks no living creature, but would rather go hungry until it finds something dead somewhere, so as not to live, as it were, at the risk and harm of others; for whatever still stirs in the slightest, that it leaves alone. This may, however, also happen out of fearfulness, so as not to first risk a dangerous struggle.
<note>The name "Capito" likely refers to the Grey Mullet (*Mugil cephalus*) or a similar large-headed fish, which was often described in ancient natural histories as a "clean" eater that did not prey on other fish.</note>

## The Lyrefish.

A beautiful gold- and purple-colored fish of the Red Sea, which is covered with black and gold-colored lines as if with strings, which, however, produce no sound. It is therefore the image of a person who boasts with the outward appearance of an art or science, but achieves not the slightest.
<note>The Lyrefish or "Leierfisch" usually refers to the Dragonet (*Callionymus lyra*), named for its high dorsal fin which resembles a lyre.</note>

> Because your library is stuffed with books you have bought,
> You think yourself a learned man and a scholar, O lover of the arts.
> <note>original Latin: *Emtis quod libris tibi bibliotheca referta est, Doctum et Grammaticum te philomuse putas.*</note>
>
> Because you boast of thousands of works you've bought,
> We know you only desire to *appear* learned,
> <note>original German: *Weil du mit tausenden gekauften Werken prangst, Weiß man, daß du gelehrt zu scheinen, nur verlangst.*</note>

->says Martial.<-
<note>Martial (Marcus Valerius Martialis) was a Roman poet famous for his witty and biting epigrams that mocked the hypocrisies of Roman society.</note>

<summary>This page uses the Capito and the Lyrefish to illustrate the virtues of peaceableness and the vice of intellectual vanity. It concludes with an epigram by Martial mocking those who buy books only to appear educated.</summary>
<keywords>Capito, Lyrefish, abstinence, peaceableness, vanity, Martial, Roman poetry, symbolism, Red Sea</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of sea creatures used as moral symbols. The previous page discussed the Capito as a symbol of abstinence and the Lyrefish as a symbol of empty boasting.</meta>

## The Tuna.

The image of **Prudence**. It has been observed that this fish sees clearly only with its right eye and follows its path accordingly. Since the right eye is generally seen as indicating the prudence that is grounded in pure reason, this fish has also been adopted as a symbol of the same. Reason, says Seneca, must lead us through our whole life; the greatest and the smallest matters must be carried out according to its counsel.
<note>Seneca (Lucius Annaeus Seneca) was a Roman Stoic philosopher. The idea that the tuna sees only with its right eye originates from ancient naturalists like Aristotle and Pliny, who believed tuna entered the Black Sea along the right shore because they were "blind" in the left eye.</note>

## The Melanurus or Ruffe.

The image of **Caution**; because these fish always stay at the bottom when the sea is calm and still, and only come up and swim to the shore when storms begin to rise; as if they knew that they have no fishermen to fear during stormy weather. This fits very well with Horace’s thought:

> The well-prepared heart hopes in adversity
> and fears in prosperity for the opposite lot.
> <note>original Latin: *Sperat infestis, metuit secundis / Alteram sortem bene præparatum / Pectus.* From Horace's Odes, Book II, 10.</note>
> 
> A well-composed spirit
> Hopes in danger and only fears in good fortune
> The easily changing fate.
> <note>original German: *Ein wohlgefaßter Muth / Hofft in Gefahr und fürchtet nur im Glück / Das leicht sich ändernde Geschick.*</note>

<note>The *Melanurus* (literally "black-tail") likely refers to the saddled seabream (*Oblada melanura*). The German "Kaulbars" usually refers to a freshwater Ruffe, but early modern naturalists often used familiar local names to identify exotic or Mediterranean species described in classical texts.</note>

<summary>This page describes the Tuna as a symbol of Reason-driven Prudence and the Melanurus as a symbol of Caution, using the tuna's supposed one-sided vision and the Melanurus's storm-avoidance behavior as moral lessons.</summary>
<keywords>Tuna, Prudence, Seneca, Reason, Melanurus, Ruffe, Caution, Horace, Fate, Symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of sea creatures used as moral symbols. Following the previous page's discussion on the Tuna (Prudence) and the Ruffe (Caution), this page explores the Flying Fish as a warning against false security and the Moray Eel as a lesson in patience and reason.</meta>

## The Flying Fish.

The image of the punishment of those who chase after false happiness; because this fish comes out above the water at the slightest glimpse of sun and calm seas and is snatched up by birds of prey when it least expects it. — 

> The place that seemed full of rest and tranquility was the very place where the greatest storms of trouble and whirlwind arose. <note>original Latin: *Qui locus quietis et tranquillitatis plenus esse videbatur, in eo maxime molestiarum et turbinum tempestates extiterunt.*</note> — Where one seemed to be most calm and safe, says Cicero, there the most unrest and storms arose.
<note>Marcus Tullius Cicero was a Roman statesman and orator. The author uses the flying fish's habit of leaping from the water to escape sea predators, only to be caught by birds, as a metaphor for how "calm" moments in life can hide unexpected dangers.</note>

## The Moray Eel.

The image of provoked patience. This fish is said to endure several blows patiently, but after many more, it falls into a rage. This is a lesson both for those who willfully provoke another’s patience—so that the danger does not fall upon their own heads—and for the patient person, that they should remain firm; for if it is not advisable to surrender to anger and rage at the first attack, it is no more so at the tenth; rather, one must always remain <term>rational|Vernunft</term> toward the malicious, for the means that reason provides are always, and alone, the safest.
<note>In ancient Rome, moray eels were famously kept in private ponds and were known for their power and perceived temperament. The author here argues against the "breaking point" of patience, suggesting that true reason should never give way to anger, regardless of the provocation.</note>

<summary>This page uses the Flying Fish to warn against the dangers of false security and the Moray Eel to advocate for unwavering reason and patience even under repeated provocation.</summary>
<keywords>Flying Fish, Moray Eel, Cicero, patience, reason, punishment, security, anger</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page concludes the entry on the Moray Eel by moving from its natural behavior to historical anecdotes involving Roman figures, illustrating themes of tyranny and emotional attachment. It then introduces the Crab as a new moral symbol of strategic vulnerability and strength.</meta>

When the inhabitants of Palestine asked *Pescenius Niger* for a reduction in taxes, as everything was already burdened by them, he said: be glad that I do not also demand a tax from you for the air; by which he made himself completely hated.
<note>Pescenius Niger was a Roman general who claimed the imperial title in 193 AD. This anecdote, recorded in the *Historia Augusta*, portrays him as a harsh and witty tyrant whose fiscal policies alienated his subjects.</note>

The moray eel is also said to be capable of being tamed and trained for all sorts of tasks. *Crassus* kept such a tame moray eel for a long time, and it was so dear to him that he wept for it when it died and had it buried magnificently; for which *Domitius* reproached him, calling it a most ridiculous folly. *Crassus* replied: I have indeed only mourned an animal; but you have not felt the slightest grief even at the loss of three wives.
<note>This famous exchange between the Roman orators Lucius Licinius Crassus and Gnaeus Domitius Ahenobarbus highlights the Roman practice of keeping "pet" fish in elaborate ponds (piscinae). The anecdote serves as a lesson in both the capacity for animal companionship and a sharp-witted defense against hypocrisy.</note>

## The Crab.

The image of a proper sense of one's own strength. The crab hides away at the time when it sheds its old shell; but as soon as it is covered with a new one again, it boldly goes to meet its enemy and offers its claws.
<note>The crab’s molting process is used here as a metaphor for <term>Prudence|Klugheit</term>. It teaches that one should recognize moments of vulnerability and withdraw, only returning to confrontation when one is fully prepared and fortified.</note>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of aquatic creatures and their moral symbolism. Having previously discussed the moray eel and the crab, the author now transitions to the stingray, the parasite fish, and the sea bream, focusing on themes of treachery, dependency, and foolish obsession.</meta>

### The Stingray or Angelfish.

The image of assassination; for whatever this fish touches with its stinger must die, and no medicine can help against it; for this reason, *Peleus* also carried a spear tipped with such a stinger.
<note>The <term>Stingray|Pastinac</term> was much feared in antiquity for its venomous tail spine. While the text mentions Peleus, father of Achilles, ancient myths more famously describe Odysseus being killed by his son Telegonus with a spear tipped with a stingray spine.</note>
Several fish of this kind served as an image of those who ruin themselves through revelries, because the fishermen, when catching them, lured the multitude into the net with nothing other than all kinds of singing and celebration. The dreamers, says *Juvenal*, never consider the consequences of vain pleasures. They never reflect on what their joys cost them.
<note>original: "Non vnquam reputant quanti sibi gaudia constent." This quote from the Roman satirist Juvenal warns against the hidden price of fleeting indulgence.</note>

### The Pedunculus.

The image of a parasite. It is a lazy companion to the dolphin, and fattens itself on the crumbs of its prey.
<note>The name <term>Pedunculus</term> here likely refers to the Remora or "suckerfish," which attaches itself to larger marine animals. In the moralizing tradition of this text, its behavior is seen as a symbol of the "social parasite" who lives off the labor of the powerful.</note>

### The Sargus.

The image of a person who follows only chimeras. The *Sargi* are said to love the scent of goats very much and, if they see even the shadow of a goat on the shore, they frequently gather there; for this reason, fishermen make use of this deception merely with a stuffed goat skin...
<note>The <term>Sargus</term> is the Sea Bream. Ancient naturalists like Aelian claimed these fish were so attracted to goats that they would leap out of the water toward them, making them a symbol of irrational obsession or "chasing shadows."</note>

<summary>This page uses the stingray, the parasite fish, and the sea bream to illustrate the dangers of assassination, the drain of social parasites, and the folly of being led astray by illusions.</summary>
<keywords>Stingray, Juvenal, Parasitism, Sea Bream, Peleus, Moral Symbolism, Deception</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of aquatic creatures and their moral symbolism, concluding the entry on the Sargus and moving on to the Theudites and the Physa. It maintains the theme of using animal behavior to illustrate human virtues and vices.</meta>

...en, in order to lure and catch a great number of fish of this kind.
<note>This completes the entry from the previous page regarding the <term>Sargus|Sea Bream</term>, which was said to be so obsessed with goats that fishermen would wear goatskins to lure them into nets.</note>

### The Theudites.

A type of swordfish that makes little use of its weapons and was therefore seen as the image of a lazy warrior. For this reason, *Themistocles* once said mockingly of the *Eretrians*: they were like the *Theudites*, who indeed have a sword but no heart.
<note>original: "Theudites". While translated here as "swordfish" following the German *Schwerdtfisch*, the ancient Greek term <term>Teuthis</term> actually referred to a squid or cuttlefish. The "sword" refers to the internal "pen" or bone of the cephalopod, which is sharp but the animal itself is soft and "heartless." Themistocles was a famous Athenian general; the Eretrians were residents of a rival Greek city-state.</note>

### The Physa.

An Egyptian fish, the symbol of the longing of lovers; for it was believed that the liver of the *Physa* would wax and wane along with the moon, and the entire fish would appear sometimes fat and sometimes lean. This effect of the moon’s waxing and waning upon this fish was compared to the effect of the presence and absence of a beloved object upon a lover. The shepherd *Lycidas* provided an example of this—an experience that surely many others had before and after him—as a Latin poet expressed in the following verses to his beloved:

> As Lycidas, I wander pining away without my mistress Phyllis; 
> Without you, alas, even lilies seem black to my wretched self.
<note>original: "Vt Lycidas domina sine Phyllide tabidus erro, / Te sine vae misero mihi lilia nigra videntur." These verses evoke the pastoral tradition where the natural world reflects the lover's internal state of "wasting away" (tabidus).</note>

<vocab>Theudites, swordfish, Themistocles, Eretrians, Physa, moon, Lycidas, Phyllis</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the Theudites as a symbol of cowardice and the Egyptian Physa fish as a symbol of the fluctuating emotions of lovers, influenced by the moon.</summary>
<keywords>Theudites, swordfish, cowardice, Themistocles, Physa, lovers' longing, lunar influence, Lycidas, Phyllis</keywords>

## The Octopus.
<note>original: "Der Polyp." In historical natural history and emblem books, the "polyp" or "polypus" refers to the octopus.</note>

The image of greed and envy. —
He is said to be very gluttonous, and at the same time very cunning in the catching of his prey. Since he does not always succeed, however, and often suffers from want: he eats his own parts, which nevertheless grow back for him, as it were, for a new feast.
<note>This reflects an ancient biological myth, recorded by writers like Aristotle and Pliny the Elder, claiming that octopuses would consume their own tentacles during the winter when food was scarce, only for them to regenerate later.</note>
In this same way, envious minds hide passion in their breasts and feed upon their own torment.

The head of the octopus is said to be very tasty but causes insomnia; therefore, it was used to signify the mixture of good and evil in human life. And *Synesius* says in the 4th Hymn:
<note><term>Synesius|Synesius of Cyrene</term> was a 4th-century philosopher and bishop whose hymns often blended Neoplatonic philosophy with Christian thought. The "4th Hymn" mentioned here likely refers to his reflections on the dualities of the physical and spiritual worlds.</note>

### A Frog and a Hydra.

This symbol represents all useless clamor. The frog that croaks the loudest to frighten the hydra <note>A multi-headed water serpent from Greek mythology.</note> is the one it devours first. The noble-minded person punishes all the empty shouting and threats of the powerless only with contempt; for fearful and weak dogs bark the loudest because they can do the least harm.

## The Bees.

By the bees, one designated a people who loyally obey their ruler, just as by the leader of the bees <note>Historically, the "head" of the hive was often referred to as a King in classical and medieval literature, before it was understood to be a Queen.</note> one indicated a wise and purposefully governing ruler, with whom a people has cause to be satisfied.

->When the honey is taken, the bees are driven away with smoke.<-
<note>original Latin: "Cum exiguntur mella fumo abiguntur apes."</note>

When one takes the honey, one drives the bees away with smoke, says Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman naturalist who described the behavior of bees in his *Natural History*.</note>.

Through this symbol, the Egyptians designated the vain incense of fame, with which true merit is only shamed <note>The author suggests that just as smoke drives away the hard-working bees, hollow praise or "incense" can mask or diminish the actual hard work and merit of an individual.</note>.

# The Power of Concord.

To represent this concept, a strange symbol was used: a group of two figures where an ant grows into an elephant, and an elephant ends in the shape of half an ant. If one also wished to indicate every kind of contrast and completely opposite things, fire and water were drawn alongside them. The advantages of concord have been confirmed by the history of all ages, according to the well-known proverb:

> Through harmony small things grow; through discord the greatest things fall apart. <note>original Latin: "Concordia parvæ res crescunt discordia maximæ dilabuntur." This is a famous maxim by the Roman historian Sallust from his work *The Jugurthine War*.</note>

->Through concord the smallest becomes great;<-
->The greatest falls apart through discord alone.<-

Or according to the Greek poet:

> Three bulls of one mind grazed together,
> Whom not even the wild beast, the lion, could often harm;
> But once a hateful strife and battle split them,
> It devoured each one, defenseless and alone.
> <note>original Greek: "Ομοφρονες νεμοντο τρεις ὁμου βοες / Ους ουδε θηρ εβλαπτε πολλακη λεων / Εχθρα δε μισει και μαχη διασχισασ' / Εκαστον εκβεβρωκε γυμνον ως ενα." This poetic excerpt refers to a classic Aesop’s Fable, "The Lion and the Three Bulls," which serves as a moral lesson on the strength of unity.</note>

->A trio of bulls of one mind<-
->Not even the wild lion can harm.<-
->But as soon as a hostile rift separates them:<-
->He tears each one apart with ease.<-

### Ants at the Heap of Gold.

The symbol of **avarice**. Strabo <note>a Greek geographer, c. 64 BC – 24 AD</note> and Pliny <note>the Elder, Roman naturalist and author of *Natural History*</note> mention a mountainous region of India inhabited by the Dardae, where there are many gold mines that are churned up by very large ants. The inhabitants seek to take possession of the excavated gold at great peril; for the ants not only defend themselves but also pursue and kill both people and livestock. Therefore, one first scatters pieces of meat here and there to distract the ants, and in this manner carries away the golden booty. In the same way, misers guard their hoarded lumps of gold, which they neither use themselves nor wish to leave for others to benefit from, which is the greatest nonsense, as Horace <note>the Roman lyric poet</note> very ingeniously remarks:

> If someone were to buy lyres and, once bought, store them all in one place,
> Though neither devoted to the study of the lyre nor to any Muse;
> If he bought shoemaker’s knives and lasts <note>original Latin: "scalpra et formas"</note> without being a shoemaker, or sails for ships
> While being averse to trade—he would everywhere be called
> Crazed and insane, and rightly so. How is he different from those
> Who hide away coins and gold, not knowing how to use
> The things they have gathered?
> <note>original Latin: "Si quis emat cytharas, emtus comportet in vnum, Nec studio cytharæ, nec Musæ deditus ulli: Si scalpra et formas non sutor, nautica vela Auersus mercaturis, delirus et amens, Undique dicatur merito; quid discrepat istis Qui numos aurumque recondit, nescius vti Compositis?"</note>

->If someone bought one lute after another and kept<-
->them in abundance,<-
->Without ever being devoted to the lute or any other Muse;<-

## The Fly.

The brevity of human life. The maximum age of a fly was once determined to be seven years <note>This refers to an archaic belief in natural history used to create a proportional scale for human life.</note>, and by that measure, the maximum human age—even were it a thousand years—was represented as very fleeting and short; as Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, Roman Stoic philosopher, c. 4 BC – 65 AD</note> also says:

> Understand how far a human being is permitted to proceed; how tiny a span it is for those brought forth for the shortest time; soon to yield our place to the one coming after, we look out from a guest-lodging agreed upon for a short stay. I speak of our own spans of life, which it is certain are rolled away with incredible speed. Count the centuries of cities; you will see how they have not stood for long, even those that boast of their antiquity. All human things are frail and short, occupying no part of infinite time.
> <note>original Latin: "Comprehende quantum plurimum procedere homini licet; quantulum est ad breuissimum tempus editi; cito cessuri loco venienti in pactum prospicimus hospitium. De nostris ætatibus loquor, quas incredibili celeritate conuolui constat. Computa vrbium sæcula, videbis quam non diu steterint, etiam quæ vetustate gloriantur. Omnia humana caduca sunt et brevia, infiniti temporis nullam partem occupantia." From Seneca's *Epistulae Morales*, 99.31-32.</note>

One should consider that no matter how long a person may live, how very short the time nonetheless is. We must be mindful at every moment, our

All human existence is vain and fleeting, a nothing in the vast space of infinity.

### Locusts behind a fleeing army.

A symbol that often the small hunt the great; likewise one also has examples concerning flies. When Trajan <note>Marcus Ulpius Trajanus, Roman Emperor from 98 to 117 AD, known for his military conquests.</note> had defeated the <term>Parthians</term> <note>An ancient Iranian empire and a major rival to Rome.</note>, he encamped against the <term>Agarenians</term> <note>Also known as Hagarenes, a nomadic people of the Arabian desert.</note>. (An Arabian people, derived from the tribal name Agar <note>Referring to the biblical Hagar, mother of Ishmael.</note>.) However, such an unbearable multitude of flies appeared that they were forced to withdraw again, as <term>Dio Cassius</term> <note>Cassius Dio, a Roman historian who wrote a 80-volume history of Rome in Greek.</note> writes. The ancients also intended to indicate by this that one should not underestimate even the lowest person. A single hair, they said, casts its shadow just as well as the trunk of an oak.

<summary>This page concludes a Stoic reflection on the brevity of human life and introduces a new historical emblem illustrating how small, seemingly insignificant forces—like flies—can overcome powerful emperors like Trajan.</summary>
<keywords>brevity of life, Seneca, Trajan, Agarenians, Dio Cassius, humility, military history, metaphors</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the moralizing natural history emblems, moving from the previous page's discussion of flies and historical defeat to the symbolic meanings of the weasel and the "light-midge" (pyrausta).</meta>

# The Weasel.

The taming of slanderers. The ancients <note>A common reference to classical Greek and Roman naturalists like Pliny the Elder, who often recorded folk remedies involving animal parts.</note> held it to be a proven remedy that one would never be barked at by dogs if one carried a weasel’s tail. It may be that the dogs’ keen sense of smell, being irritated by this, disrupts their impulse to bark. This much is certain: it is always some particular, superior quality that shuts the mouths of slanderers. <note>The author shifts from a literal folk remedy to a moral metaphor: excellence is the best defense against gossip.</note>

# The Light-Midge. (<term>Pyrausta</term>)
<note>The original term "Lichtmükke" refers to an insect attracted to light. The "Pyrausta" is a legendary insect mentioned by ancient writers that was said to live in fire or be destroyed by it; it is a common emblem for the dangers of attraction to worldly glory.</note>

The symbol of the vainly ambitious, who, driven by their small minds, allow themselves to be blinded by the radiance of empty honor and find their ruin within it; for truly great spirits never strived for vain positions of honor, but rather avoided them and often served their ungrateful age despite disgrace and shame. The vainly ambitious person does not care for the common good, for he has neither the sense nor the capacity for it; instead, he measures everything only by the standard of his private advantage, and he finds it very unwise to bring about even the slightest good if it causes any detraction from his vain honor and his... <note>The sentence ends abruptly as the text continues on the following page.</note>

# The Beetle.

This was a very important symbol for the Egyptians; for they held the beetle to be a particularly mysterious being. They believed that among all creatures, it alone was male and was formed each time from a round germ that opened in the water. <note>This reflects the ancient belief, recorded by writers like Plutarch, that the scarab beetle (Scarabaeus sacer) had no females and reproduced by rolling balls of dung, which were thought to be "germs" or eggs.</note> Therefore, they designated everything masculine and brave by a

<summary>This page concludes a moral warning against vanity using the fable of the Fir Tree and the Bramble, then introduces the beetle as an ancient Egyptian symbol of masculinity and mystery.</summary>
<keywords>vanity, pride, Aesop's fables, Fir Tree and Bramble, Egyptian symbolism, beetle, scarab, masculinity</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Egyptian symbolism regarding the beetle (scarab). The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing the belief that the beetle was formed from a "round germ" in the water.</meta>

beetle, and compared its germ with the origin of the world and the celestial body. <note>Ancient Egyptians associated the scarab's rolling of dung balls with the movement of the sun across the sky.</note> The warriors were bound by a special law to wear a ring with the image of a beetle, under the motto: "All those who fight for the fatherland must be men." Through this, they promised not only the required obedience to the commands of their generals; but rather they pledged themselves with the single word that they wished to be men—brave men. Upon this is based Seneca's expression: *You have promised to be a good man; you are bound by an oath.* <note>original: "Promisisti virum bonum, sacramento ligatus es." Seneca was a Roman Stoic philosopher; here he emphasizes that a moral commitment is as binding as a military oath.</note> "You have promised to be a brave man, and are now bound to it by oath."

If warriors had proven themselves negligent and lazy, they were no longer called men, but women, and that was shame enough. Thus Dio Cassius <note>A Roman historian of Greek origin.</note> also mentions that the British Queen Bunduica <note>Historically known as Boudica, the iconic warrior queen of the Iceni tribe who led an uprising against Roman rule.</note> called Emperor Nero "Neronides" <note>A mocking version of his name used to suggest he was unmanly or effeminate.</note> because of his more than womanly softness.

# The Tree.

The symbol of immortality. If one robs it of its branches, the trunk sprouts back all the more vigorously; if one cuts down the entire tree, it rises up again from the root. The tree was also the symbol of true philosophical serenity and strength; in that it endures everything, and through adversities is only further strengthened and flourishes; at which Horace’s <note>Quintus Horatius Flaccus, a leading Roman lyric poet during the time of Augustus.</note> simile also aims:

> As an oak, shorn by cruel axes
> On Mount Algidus, rich in dark leaves,
> Through losses, through slaughters,
> Draws wealth and courage from the very iron.
> <note>original: "Duris ut ilex tonsa bipennibus / Nigræ feraci frondis in algido / Per damna per cædes, ab ipso / Ducit opes animumque ferro." From Horace’s Odes, Book 4, Ode 4. The poet describes the Roman people’s resilience by comparing them to an oak tree that grows stronger the more it is pruned or attacked.</note>

> As the oak on the fertile Algidus
> When the crashing axe fells it,
> From the sharpness of the iron itself
> Receives new courage and strength.

# The Cypress.

The symbol of mourning and adversity, and also of dejection, which nevertheless rights itself again. Likewise, it is the symbol of everything that is meager and lowly, but which subsequently attains height. Martial <note>A Roman poet from Hispania best known for his twelve books of Epigrams.</note> says:

The cypress suffers no moths or worms and resists all decay; therefore, the ancients also used cypress wood for coffins and statues <note>The durability of cypress wood made it a favorite in antiquity for items intended to last centuries, such as temple doors or funerary chests.</note>.

## The Laurel Tree.

The first known advantage of this tree consisted in its healing power; its leaves were considered a universal preventative and remedy <note>term for *Universal-Vorbeugungs- und Hülfsmittel*: a "cure-all" or panacea intended to both prevent and treat ailments.</note> in all diseases. Therefore, a dove with a laurel leaf in its beak was an ancient symbol of medicine <note>original: *Heilkunde* — the art of healing.</note>; it also designated a person who, through some accidental or devised means, restores themselves from a disease. For it was claimed to have been noticed that sick doves carry laurel leaves into their nest and recover because of them. It was also believed that dove meat was the only beneficial food during illnesses.

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the laurel tree, shifting from its medicinal properties to its symbolic role as a reward for poets and heroes, and its conceptual superiority over material wealth.</meta>

Subsequently, laurel branches also became the ceremonial foliage of heroes and poets. To suggest the allure of glory, a composite symbol of laurel branches and figs, hanging from them like fruit, was used. For only that person was considered a sublime spirit who was driven to great and noble actions solely by the sweetness of glory, and who valued money and possessions far too little for them to serve as any kind of reward. Therefore, Ovid also has Mamurius say: *“Let glory be given to me as my reward.”* <note>original Latin: "Merces mihi gloria detur." From Ovid's *Fasti*, Book 3. Mamurius Veturius was the legendary smith who, according to Roman myth, forged the sacred shields (*ancilia*) for King Numa.</note> Glory should be my only reward. And Polybius says: “Money is a common possession of all people, but majesty and honor belong to the gods and to men born with such a nature.” <note>original Greek: "Αργυριον εστι κοινον παντων ανθρωπων κτημα, το δε καλον και τιμην ανηκον θεων και τουτοις πεφυκοτων ανδρων εστι." Polybius (c. 200–118 BCE) was a Greek historian known for his work *The Histories*.</note> Among the ancients, no one could claim great honor and glory if they had nothing more to show than great wealth—even though people often tried to force these distinctions through such means. Thus, the glory-seeking, wealthy courtesan Phryne <note><term>Hure</term>: Translated here as "courtesan" or "harlot." In the Greek context, Phryne was a famous *hetaira*, a high-class companion known for her beauty, wealth, and influence.</note> promised to rebuild the walls of Thebes if she were permitted this inscription:

->Alexander destroyed them; Phryne the courtesan restored them.<- <note>original Greek: "Αλεξανδρος κατεσκαψεν, ανεστησε δε Φρυνη η εταιρα."</note>

->Destroyed by Alexander, restored by the harlot Phryne.<-

# The Palm Tree.

The symbol of wisdom and virtue, of justice, victory, equality, durability, and constancy. The palm tree, it was said, loves salty soil and cannot tolerate manure; in the same way, virtue, when joined with wisdom, does not tolerate the filth of vice. *It shines with unsullied honors.* <note>original: "Intaminatis fulget honoribus," a quote from the Roman poet Horace (Odes 3.2) describing how true virtue does not depend on popular approval.</note> A palm tree that grows of its own accord on someone’s land and soil—*a palm born at home* <note>original: "palma domi nata," an idiom for success that comes naturally or without external struggle.</note>—was interpreted as a sign of exceptional and easily attained fame; just as anything one obtained at home was attributed to easy acquisition, for example: *Gold itself is born at his home* <note>original: "Aurum ipsi domi nascitur," a proverb meaning gold grows for him, as it were, in his own field or within his dwelling.</note> **Augustus** <note>The German text uses "August," referring to Augustus Caesar (63 BC – 14 AD), the first Roman Emperor.</note> had a palm tree that had sprouted between the joints of stones moved beneath the rain gutters of the household gods and had it carefully tended so that it might become large and strong. A palm tree that grew of its own accord on the Capitoline Hill in Rome was interpreted as a sign of enduring triumph and victory.

A palm tree upon which frogs sit signifies the constant companion of virtue: envy. **Plutarch** <note>A famous Greek biographer and essayist (c. 46 – 119 AD).</note> mentions such a bronze palm tree covered in frogs, which was found among other sacred legacies in a temple. The frogs, however, are

### The Sacred Grove of Trees.

This group comprised three trees: the **Nile tree**, the **late-fruiting tree**, and the **white poplar**. The Nile tree <note>Likely the *Persea* (Mimusops laurifolia), which was highly significant in ancient Egyptian religion.</note>, which loves the water of the Nile, was sacred to **Isis** <note>The major Egyptian goddess of healing, magic, and motherhood.</note> and was meant to remind mortals to always be vigilant over their heart and tongue; because the fruits of this sacred tree resembled the shape of a heart and the leaves resembled tongues. A pure heart and a pure tongue were therefore the true symbol of this tree. ## The Fig Tree.

The image of a person who promises nothing outwardly, but possesses all the more inner substance and utility. The fig tree does not boast any prior blossoms, yet it bears the sweetest fruit <note>Unlike most fruit trees, the fig's "blossoms" are actually hidden inside the fruit-like structure (the syconium), making it appear as if the fruit appears without flowering.</note>. To promise nothing, but to do all the more, was also a maxim of **Pythagoras** <note>A famous Greek philosopher and mathematician (c. 570–495 BC) who was often credited with bringing esoteric wisdom from Egypt to the West.</note>, who had studied the wisdom of the **Egyptians**.

## Fruit on a Bare Trunk.

The symbol of a useful man without vain ambition, who lives humbly and in obscurity. **Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 371–287 BC) often called the "Father of Botany" for his extensive writings on plants.</note> mentions a grapevine that produced the most magnificent grapes without any vines or leaves, as well as a fruit-bearing **olive tree** from which all branches had been removed. **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman author and naturalist whose *Natural History* became a standard reference for the wonders of the natural world.</note> also writes: "We have found a grapevine and a Punic apple tree that bore fruit from the trunk, not from a shoot or branch." <note>original: "Inuenimus vitem et malum punicam stirpe fructum tulisse, non palmite aut ramis."</note> A grapevine and a **Punic apple tree** (pomegranate <insert>tree</insert>)

### Asphodel or Goldwort.

The symbol of dominion in reference to the sovereign’s staff; for the asphodel is also called the <term>king's spear</term> <note>original Latin: "hasta regis"</note> and even in French the <term>royal axe</term> <note>original French: "la hache royale"</note>. The worms that usually find their way to the asphodel, often consuming it entirely, but finally gain wings and fly away, were interpreted as the favorites and flatterers who are showered by the regent with riches and honorary positions—

## Brazil Herb
<note>The "Brazil herb" or "Brasilienkraut" likely refers to the brazilwood or plants associated with the newly explored Americas, often used in emblems to represent sensitivity or hidden qualities.</note>

The symbol of a mild government. This herb has the property that it gives off a lovely scent when rubbed gently, but a most unpleasant one when it is pressed and squeezed, from which it afterwards develops poisonous worms. It is therefore a lesson for rulers.

> He who wishes to be loved should rule with a gentle hand;
> The Nile flows softly, yet it stands out as more useful
> Than all other rivers.
<note>original Latin: "Qui vult amari languida regnet manu; Lene fluit Nilus, sed cunctis amnibus extat Vtilior."</note>

->He who wishes to be loved, let him rule softly and gently;<-
->Softly the Nile flows along, and among all rivers it is<-
->The most useful.<-

### The Lily among Thistles.

**The image of virtue, which blooms even in adversity.** Virtue grows from a wound. <note>original Latin: "Crescit de vulnere virtus"</note>

->Virtue grows through injury.<-

The condition of mortals is subject to such changes that adversity is born from prosperity, and prosperity from adversity, <note>original Latin: "Habet has vices conditio mortalium vt aduersa ex secundis, ex aduersis secunda nascantur"</note> says Pliny <note>Pliny the Younger, a Roman lawyer and author, in his famous speech (Panegyricus) praising Emperor Trajan</note> in his panegyric to Trajan. The state of mortals is subject to constant change, so that misfortune arises from good fortune and good fortune from misfortune.

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 107 regarding the "golden age" and the comparison between the life-sustaining value of agriculture versus the destructive greed associated with mining and gold.</meta>

human race was precisely that time when nothing was yet known of that pernicious gold; when people were satisfied with the edible, simple fruits of the earth, living in innocence and harmony without any greed.

The lust for gold and money is the uninterruptedly progressing page number of world history, and of the private life of every individual. The common verse in Virgil:

> To what do you not drive mortal hearts, accursed hunger for gold? <note>original: "Auri sacra fames quid non mortalia cogis Pectora?" from Virgil’s Aeneid, Book 3.</note>

shows us antiquity from this perspective, as well as the confirmation of this sad truth since the discovery of the route around Africa to the East Indies and the discovery of America.

Ancient history tells us of a gold-greedy Pythes <note>Pythes (or Pythius) of Phrygia was a historical figure famed for his immense wealth in gold, mentioned by Herodotus and Plutarch.</note>, who drove his subjects so hard into mining that almost all other more necessary and useful occupations were thereby neglected, and the inhabitants eventually suffered from a lack of almost everything. Therefore, the women turned to the wife of Pythes and urgently begged her to bring him to other thoughts and distract him from this wretched obsession with gold. --- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Pythes of Lydia, illustrating the folly of prioritizing wealth over basic needs through a clever lesson from his wife.</meta>

...favorite meal. At first sight, Pythes was full of wonder and found an extraordinary joy in the whim of his wife. Finally, however, when he had looked his fill long enough, and saw no indication that the golden dishes—which he believed his wife had placed there only as a clever jest and a diverting surprise—would be cleared away to make room for more edible dishes, for which he now felt a greater longing: his wife said that in all seriousness there was nothing else to be had, because he kept so many hands busy with his endless gold mines <term>Goldgruben (gold mines)</term> that no one had time anymore to provide or look after any provisions <term>Lebensmittel (literally: means of life)</term> other than golden ones; therefore, one would now have to be satisfied with these. This maneuver then also achieved its desired effect.

One may compare this with the meal of Varus in the works of Martial <note>Marcus Valerius Martialis (c. 38–104 AD), a Roman poet famous for his short, witty, and often biting epigrams.</note>:

> Varus recently happened to invite me to dine:
> The display was rich, but the dinner was tiny.
> The table is weighed down with gold, not with delicacies; the servants
> Set much before the eyes, but little for the throat.
