# The Amnochrysus.
<note>A historical name for a gemstone—likely a variety of quartz or beryl with gold-colored inclusions—derived from the Greek words for "sand" and "gold."</note>

A gemstone mixed with gold and all kinds of sand and dust. ### Some Other Remnants of Egyptian Images and Conceptions, even among other Nations.

---

## The Universe.

The Egyptians represented this immeasurable symbol of the <term>Deity<note>original German: "Gottheit." In this context, it refers to the supreme divine principle or the creative force behind the cosmos.</note></term> through an <term>Egg<note>The "World Egg" or "Cosmic Egg" is a foundational motif in many ancient mythologies, representing the universe in its earliest state of potentiality before the different elements were separated.</note></term>, whose shell indicated the circumference, the white [represented] air and water, and the yolk [represented] fire. Some interpreters, however, have rightly noted that the Egyptians intended this symbol to signify the emergence of the universe rather than the developed and expanded universe itself; because according to that representation, the element of earth is missing—unless one were to assume the shell represents it, though here it is actually meant to represent the circumference of the universe. It is therefore certainly more probable that the idea of the origin of the universe lies at the basis of the symbol of the egg.

This ancient idea of the Egyptian sages was thus analogical. It was observed that everything [comes] from an e— <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("ei-"), likely leading into "einem Ei" (an egg) on the following page.</note>

<vocab>Egyptians, Universe, Symbol, Egg, Shell, Element, Deity, Emergence/Origin</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the Egyptian use of the "Cosmic Egg" as a symbol for the creation and structure of the universe, debating whether it represents the completed world or the process of its birth.</summary>
<keywords>Ancient Egypt, cosmology, World Egg, symbolism, elements, creation myths, Egyptian philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the "Cosmic Egg." It completes the thought that just as life begins from a germ, the universe also had a primal beginning. It then transitions into the Egyptian concept of darkness as a primary creative force.</meta>

originated from a [single] germ, and they concluded therefrom that the universe must also have had its primal germ, out of which it gradually developed—and continues to develop—over an immense number of myriads of years into its present immeasurable expansion.

The Egyptians also held darkness to be the first <term>principle<note>original Latin: *principium*. In this philosophical context, it refers to the fundamental source or origin of all existence.</note></term> of all that is, and asserted that the creative nature of the <term>Deity<note>original German: *Gottheit*.</note></term> had dwelt in darkness from eternity, shrouded in impenetrable, holy gloom. This conception is the exact opposite of our own, as we imagine the Divine Being much more as pure light—since He is light itself—whereas we conceive of the spirit of darkness and its works as an entirely opposite being.

According to the <term>Mosaic account<note>original German: "Mosaische Erklärung." This refers to the biblical creation story in the Book of Genesis, traditionally attributed to Moses.</note></term>: “God said, let there be light,” it also seems to follow that everything was dark beforehand. However, it is understandable that the Deity did not require material light, which was only intended to shine for the benefit of His creatures. The Deity was nonetheless in Himself full of divine light, which, to us creatures, is of course deep darkness.

The Egyptian priests also had the custom during their sacred rites of [invoking] three times the unknown darkness, by which they... <note>The text ends mid-sentence and continues on the following page.</note>

It contrasts this with the Judeo-Christian perspective where God is defined as light, suggesting that divine light is so intense it appears as darkness to human beings.</summary>

The Egyptians called the Deity *Kneph* or *Knaph*. Some say *Eneph*, others *Emeth*, the latter of which means Truth in <term>Hebrew<note>Referring to the word "Emet" (אמת), meaning truth or certainty.</note></term>. The feather was intended to signify the dim knowledge that humans have of the Deity. Therefore, **Moses** commanded the Israelites not to imagine the divine being under any kind of image.

The <term>Pythagorean<note>Followers of Pythagoras, who often emphasized mathematical and geometric order in the cosmos.</note></term> **Timaeus** calls God an **inimitable universe** <note>original Greek: Κοσμον ινδαλμικον (*Kosmon indalmikon*). This suggests the world is a reflected image or "likeness" of the divine.</note>. And if, as it is said, one recognizes the master by his works: then from the incomprehensible universe, one can conclude the existence of its incomprehensible author.

# The Heavens.

A heart on a burning altar represented the heavens among the Egyptians; perhaps to express the eternally flaming motion of the **celestial bodies**, just as the circulation of the blood depends on the constant motion of the heart. Some have also interpreted this symbol to mean that intelligent and rational beings always direct their hearts toward heaven. Generally, however, the Egyptians used this symbol associated with fire to point to the constantly productive and sustaining **motive force** in nature, where on the whole nothing stands still; even if, hourly, through the cessation of their individual motion, thousands of beings sink back into nothingness. The movement of the sun and the moon, the earth and the other celestial bodies, the ebb and flow <term>etc.<note>original German: *u.*, an abbreviation for *und so weiter*.</note></term> seems eternal to us; and yet perhaps all these motive forces will finally reach their end one day, just like those of our blood circulation.

According to **Cedrenus** <note>Georgios Kedrenos, a 12th-century Byzantine historian who compiled various historical and legendary accounts.</note>, King **Chosroes** in Persia had invented a **machine** that not only showed the rotation of the stars but also represented rain, thunder, and lightning, and in the center of which he himself sat like a god in human form. "He invented the artful image of the..." <note>original Greek: Ἔυρε τὸ μουσαῖον εἴδωλον τοῦ... This sentence appears to be cut off in the original manuscript transcription.</note>

Philostratus <note>Flavius Philostratus (c. 170–250 AD), a Greek scholar who wrote the *Life of Apollonius of Tyana*.</note> describes the royal court of justice in **Babylon** as a sky-blue sapphire vault, with golden images of the gods looking down upon the judges, alongside **Adrastea**, the goddess of revenge <note>Also known as Nemesis, she was the personification of divine retribution against hubris.</note>, both to warn wicked judges against passing an unjust sentence and to remind the king himself not to elevate himself above common humanity.

## The Sun.

Among the Egyptians, the sun was the image of **divine providence**. The image of the sun was frequently found on ships, where its spreading golden rays had a particularly enchanting effect; it was even said that the **crocodile** seemed soothed by it and would swim along calmly.

The sun priests at **Heliopolis** <note>An ancient Egyptian city dedicated to the sun god Ra; the name is Greek for "City of the Sun."</note> offered no wine to the sun and drank none themselves. The Greeks, too, offered to the sun only <note>The text ends mid-sentence here; historically, the Greeks often offered "sober sacrifices" (wineless) like honey and water to the sun god Helios.</note>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of solar worship rituals from the previous page, specifically concluding the list of offerings and detailing an Egyptian autumnal festival before beginning a new section on lunar iconography.</meta>

...honey, because they believed that the sun, as the ruler of all things, was far removed from wine and drunkenness. There was also a teaching that rulers and superiors must, like the sun, lead their subordinates by setting a good example and particularly abstain from wine and all excess.

The Egyptians also celebrated a special festival of the sun at the time of the autumnal equinox, namely on the 22nd of the month of **Phaophi** <note>The second month of the ancient Egyptian calendar, falling around September or October.</note>, which they called the **Festival of the Sun-Staves**; because the waning sun, which was now losing light and warmth as if due to old age, required walking staves to continue its journey. Based on this idea, sons were also called the "staves of their fathers," because well-reared sons rightly strive to be the supports for their fathers in their old age.

## The Moon.

A beautiful image of the moon was seen at **Apollinopolis** <note>Likely Apollinopolis Magna, modern-day Edfu, famous for its temple dedicated to Horus.</note> in Egypt. It was a male figure that held an eagle’s head in one hand and with the other swung a spear against a **hippopotamus**, through which the Egyptians represented **Typhon** <note>The Greek name for the Egyptian god Set, the deity of chaos and storms, who was often depicted as a hippopotamus.</note>, brother of **Osiris**

<summary>This page describes the Egyptian "Festival of the Sun-Staves" held during the autumn equinox and transitions into lunar symbolism, specifically an iconographic depiction of a moon deity triumphing over Typhon in Apollinopolis.</summary>
<keywords>Sun worship, Egyptian calendar, Phaophi, Festival of the Sun-Staves, filial piety, The Moon, Apollinopolis, Typhon, Osiris, Egyptian mythology</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of a lunar deity in Apollinopolis, Egypt, specifically describing the iconography of the figure attacking a hippopotamus (Typhon) mentioned at the end of the previous page.</meta>

they imagined, and which he seemed to pierce. The eagle’s head pointed to the kinship of the moon with the sun. Some also gave this moon-image two small horns, on the tips of which lay a circular disk. Furthermore, the entire image was colored green.

In *Carras* <note>The ancient city of Carrhae, modern-day Harran in Turkey, which was a major center for the worship of the moon god Sin.</note> in *Assyria*, people worshipped both a female and a male image of the moon. It was believed that whoever offered sacrifices to the female image would be ruled by his wife; however, the opposite was suspected of anyone who sacrificed to the male image. The head of this male image was also adorned with the symbols of the sun. This is the origin of the Latin terms **Moon God** and **Moon Goddess** <note>original: "Lunus Deus" and "Luna Dea"</note>.

The moon was also the symbol of inconstancy <term>inconstancy: the quality of being changeable or fickle in one's loyalties or temperament</term>, due to its waxing and waning. It represented a person who is at one moment angry, then kind-hearted, then sad, then joyful, and is generally moved by every external impression.

A certain *Dinias* <note>Likely a reference to a character from "The Wonders Beyond Thule," an ancient Greek travel novel by Antonius Diogenes that included a journey to the moon.</note> claimed that he had once been close to the moon and had seen that its disk consisted of a very pure earthly substance. And *Xenophanes* <note>A Greek philosopher who suggested that the moon was a pressurized cloud or an inhabited world.</note> taught, as *Cicero* <note>The Roman statesman Marcus Tullius Cicero, who often documented the opinions of earlier philosophers.</note> mentions, that the moon was inhabited and full of mountains and cities.

# The Star

The symbol of sublime mental faculties. — This ancient idea has remained unchanged, and its symbol is still the mark of the highest dignities and positions of honor. Antiquity immortalized its men, who were deified for their great merits, not so much through statues and monuments, which were all too transitory, but rather by placing them among the stars <note>This refers to the concept of "catasterism," where mythological figures or heroes were said to be transformed into constellations after death.</note>. The ancients also placed great value on the observation of the stars. A man, they said, whose gaze willingly rises to the stars, fills his soul with high feelings and attunes it to great and noble actions. The star was also the image of Providence, and on an ancient coin of <term>Emperor Pertinax</term> <note>Publius Helvius Pertinax (126–193 AD) was a Roman emperor who reigned for just three months during the "Year of the Five Emperors."</note>, one sees his likeness raising his hands toward a large star, with the inscription: *The Providence of the Gods* <note>original: "Provid. Deor.", an abbreviation for *Providentia Deorum*.</note>. To this also belongs the well-known verse:

> The stars show the way of virtue to anyone. <note>original: "Virtutis monstrant cuilibet astra viam."</note>

->The stars show everyone the way to virtue.<-

Or, the sight of the stars awakens in us good resolutions to flee the path of vice and to seek that of virtue.

<keywords>astronomy, symbolism, Emperor Pertinax, virtue, providence, catasterism, ancient Rome, morality</keywords>

# Fire

The symbol of majesty; for this reason, it was carried before rulers, a practice also in use under the Roman Emperors and in Constantinople under the name **escort fire** <note>original: "πῦρ προπεμπτικόν" (*pyr propemptikon*), a ceremonial fire carried in a procession to honor a dignitary or ruler.</note>. The King of Ethiopia sends new fire to his vassals every year; specifically, their old fire is extinguished and new fire is lit, which they then have distributed further among the rest of the subjects. Whoever does not accept the new fire is treated as a traitor and, if he is powerful, is even waged war upon.

The Persians preserve and revere a fire fallen from heaven, a portion of which they also formerly carried before their kings. The Indians also carried torches before their rulers, which they lit upon the altar of the sun. For among the ancients, the torch was the symbol of **wisdom** and an enlightened spirit.

The <term>Chaldeans</term> <note>An ancient people of Mesopotamia, often associated in historical texts with early astronomy, magic, and the worship of elemental forces.</note> also revered fire as the preeminent deity. They even once traveled through various lands to convince others of the excellence of their religion; because their god was the most powerful, to whom all others must yield. This they also proved in deed, by destroying all other idols through the power of the...

<keywords>fire symbolism, majesty, Roman Emperors, Ethiopia, Persian fire worship, Chaldeans, sacred torches, wisdom</keywords>

# Allegory
## of the Rivers of Paradise.

The Egyptians gave Paradise a vast extent; for according to their tradition, it was surrounded by the Ganges, the Nile, the Tigris, and the Euphrates; and through these four streams, they symbolized the four virtues of *Prudence*, *Temperance*, *Fortitude*, and *Justice*. <note>These are known as the four "cardinal virtues," a concept from classical philosophy that was later adopted by Christian theologians.</note>

By the Ganges, they understood *Prudence*, because this river—which winds through the whole of Hindustan <note>original: "Indostan," a historical name for the Indian subcontinent</note> in a thousand turns, as if weaving its way through—carries with it great riches of gold and precious stones.

By the Nile, they understood *Temperance*, because this stream always observes its proper time for overflowing and receding to ensure the fertility of the land. <note>The predictable flooding of the Nile was essential to Egyptian life and was seen here as a symbol of self-control and perfect timing.</note>

By the Tigris, they understood *Fortitude*, because among all rivers it is the swiftest and most turbulent, and it sweeps away everything that opposes it.

Finally, by the Euphrates, one understood *Justice*, because it spread uniform fertility and prosperity everywhere; which must be the primary endeavor of justice <term>Justiz: the legal system and the administration of law</term> and every wise government.

**Vocabulary:** Allegory, Paradise, Ganges, Nile, Tigris, Euphrates, Prudence, Temperance, Fortitude, Justice, Egyptians, Hindustan, Gold, Precious Stones, Justice, Government.

<summary>This page describes an Egyptian allegorical tradition that links the four rivers of Paradise to the four cardinal virtues: Prudence, Temperance, Fortitude, and Justice.</summary>
<keywords>Allegory, Paradise, Rivers, Cardinal Virtues, Egypt, Ganges, Nile, Tigris, Euphrates, Prudence, Temperance, Fortitude, Justice</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical exploration of geography and water started on the previous page. While the preceding text linked the four major rivers to the cardinal virtues, this section transitions from the Nile’s symbolic abundance under the Roman Emperor Vespasian to the negative symbolism of Lake Sides.</meta>

When **Vespasian** <note>Titus Flavius Vespasianus, Roman Emperor from 69–79 AD, known for stabilizing the empire after a period of civil war.</note> came to power, he visited **Egypt** and addressed the inhabitants there at his reception with the words: *Draw from me as from the Nile.* <note>original Latin: "Haurite a me tanquam a Nilo."</note> **Draw from me as from the Nile!**

## Lake **Sides** in **India**.

With this lake, the Egyptians signified the corruption of morals; for they believed that everything in this lake perished. If a nation or even an individual person allowed themselves to be so carried away by **sensuality**, **ambition**, and **greed** that they sacrificed everything to these desires, they compared them to **Sides**. They also **understood** it to mean **schemers** <note>original: "Ränkemacher," referring to those who plot or use intrigue for personal gain.</note> who, for the sake of their own advantage and pleasure, first ensnare honest people with smooth words, then incite them against one another and lead them into all kinds of misfortune. Thus, the stream of moral corruption has always existed and spread its manifold branches of **sensuality**, **ambition**, and **greed** so far that one found few steady minds that were not swept away in its whirlpool and brought to ruin.

# The Styx.

The ancients believed that the water of the **Styx** <note>In Greek mythology, the primary river of the Underworld, known for its miraculous and often corrosive or deadly properties.</note> would corrode any vessel, regardless of its material or hardness—even otherwise indestructible **gold**. Only vessels made from **Scythian donkey horn** <note>Scythia was an ancient region in Central Eurasia; the "donkey horn" mentioned here is a legendary material often cited in ancient lore as the only substance capable of containing the poisonous water of the Styx.</note> were supposedly resistant. **Alexander the Great** admired and valued these types of vessels so highly that he sent them to **Delphi** and dedicated them to **Apollo** <note>The Greek god of healing, music, and prophecy.</note>. Had they been made of gold, he naturally would not have valued them so much, but rather would have felt compelled to keep them for himself. Furthermore, to a deity, all matter is the same, and a god can quite easily make do with vessels made of donkey horn.

A primary circumstance underlying this **allegory**, however, is likely that donkeys were not such contemptible animals to the ancients as they are today, where people only value cats and foxes, etc. The ancients made the **donkey** a symbol of the highest **patience**, and patience the highest **virtue**; this is because it presupposes the highest **reason**, which so few people are capable of following, and which some do not even consider necessary. ## The Styx.

The water of the **Styx** <note>The primary river of the underworld in Greek mythology, known for its miraculous and often destructive properties.</note>, the ancients believed, would corrode every vessel, no matter its substance or hardness, even the otherwise indestructible **gold**; only vessels made of **Scythian donkey-horn** <note>Ancient legends, mentioned by writers like Pausanias and Plutarch, claimed that the poisonous water of the Styx could only be contained in a vessel made from the hoof of a horse or a donkey.</note> could resist it. **Alexander the Great** admired and valued these vessels so much that he sent them to **Delphi** and dedicated them to **Apollo**. Had they been made of gold, he naturally could not have valued them so highly, but would have had to keep them for himself. Moreover, to a deity, all matter is the same, and they can certainly make do with vessels of donkey-horn. A central point underlying this **allegory**, however, is that among the ancients, **donkeys** were not such contemptible animals as they are today, when only cats and foxes, etc., are prized. The ancients made the donkey a symbol of the highest **patience**, and patience the highest **virtue**; because it presupposes the highest **reason**, which so few people are capable of following, and in part do not consider necessary at all. ## The Power of Money.

The Phoenicians <note>An ancient maritime civilization of the Mediterranean, known for their trade and commerce.</note> usually gave their gods a bag of money in their hands; just as one otherwise observes with **Mercury**, the god of merchants. They did not only want to indicate by this that all power and all wealth stems from the gods; but also that money is truly that through which one accomplishes the most; as the poet says:

> Whoever has coins may sail with a safe breeze and mold Fortune to his own will. Let him marry Danae <note>In Greek myth, Danae was imprisoned in a bronze tower, but Zeus reached her by transforming into a shower of gold; here, the poet suggests money has the same power.</note>, and he may even command Acrisius <note>Danae's father, who tried to keep her hidden away.</note> himself to believe whatever he wishes regarding Danae. Let him compose poems, deliver speeches, make a stir, plead every case, and let him be greater than Cato <note>Marcus Porcius Cato (the Younger), a Roman statesman legendary for his incorruptibility and strict morality.</note>. I say much; whatever you desire, with money at hand, it will come to pass; the treasure chest possesses a locked-up Jupiter <note>The king of the gods; the poet implies that the one who holds the money holds the power of God himself.</note>.

# The Styx.

The water of the Styx <note>In Greek mythology, the Styx is the principal river of the Underworld, known for its miraculous and often corrosive powers.</note>, the ancients believed, would corrode any vessel, regardless of its material or hardness—even gold, which is otherwise indestructible. ## The Power of Money.

The Phoenicians <note>An ancient maritime civilization known for their commerce and trade across the Mediterranean.</note> usually placed a purse of money in the hands of their gods, just as is often seen with **Mercury**, the god of merchants. Through this, they intended to suggest not only that all power and wealth flow from the gods, but also that money is truly the most effective means of achieving one’s goals; as the poet says:

> Whoever has money may sail on a safe breeze and direct 
> Fortune according to his own will. Let him marry Danaë, 
> and he may even command Acrisius himself to trust 
> what Danaë says. Let him compose poems, deliver 
> speeches, make a noise, and argue all court cases, 
> and be greater than Cato. I say much; wish for 
> whatever you like so long as money is at hand—it 
> will happen, for the money-chest holds Jupiter himself captive.
<note>original Latin: "Quisquis habet nummos..." This passage is from Petronius’s *Satyricon*. It satirizes the Roman belief that wealth could buy anything, including the favor of the gods. It references the myth of Danaë, whom Jupiter (Jove) visited as a shower of gold to bypass her father Acrisius's guards, and contrasts this with the legendary incorruptibility of the statesman Cato.</note>

## Incense.

The symbol of piety. When *Alexander* <note>Alexander the Great (356–323 BCE), the King of Macedon who conquered the Persian Empire.</note> as a boy was very lavish with the incense he offered in sacrifice, his teacher *Leonidas* <note>Leonidas of Epirus, a strict tutor who oversaw Alexander’s early education and physical training.</note> advised him to wait with such extravagance until he had one day conquered the lands where incense grows. *Alexander’s* spirit could not quite follow the first part of that advice <note>meaning he remained extravagant</note>; all the more, however, did he follow the second. For when he subsequently conquered *Arabia*, he sent an entire shipload of incense home and requested that offerings be burned to the gods quite frequently.

### A Boy Playing with Eagles.

This hieroglyph <note>In the 18th century, the term "hieroglyph" was often used to mean an emblem or a symbolic image that contains a hidden moral meaning.</note> was intended to encourage youth toward serious and useful recreations. One would not be wrong to infer a boy's composed spirit and his future great destiny if, instead of common silly games, he chose those that revealed more gravity and intellect. Suetonius <note>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus (c. 69 – 122 CE), a Roman historian famous for his biographies of the first twelve emperors.</note> tells of the young Augustus <note>The first Roman Emperor (63 BCE – 14 CE). The text refers to him as "August."</note> that he trained several eagles to be so tame that they would descend from a considerable height to take bread from his hand, fly away, and then bring it back to him.

---

### A Lyre and a Flute beside a Plow.

Work comes before recreation; and in times of peace, agriculture and the arts flourish. When Cyrus <note>Cyrus the Great (c. 600–530 BCE), the founder of the Persian Empire.</note> intended to take the field against Astyages <note>The last king of the Median Empire, who was eventually overthrown by Cyrus.</note>, he gathered his army and had them perform heavy labor in the fields for one day, but on the next, he hosted them sumptuously. Afterward, he asked his warriors which way of life pleased them best; they said the latter. "Now, this is what I promise you," he replied, "but first, we must wage war against Astyages."

When the Ionians <note>A group of ancient Greeks living on the western coast of modern-day Turkey.</note>, says Herodotus <note>Herodotus (c. 484–425 BCE), a Greek historian known as the "Father of History."</note>, first rejected the peace proposals of Cyrus and later wanted to accept them, he gave them the following fable to understand: A piper had piped for a long time in vain to lure fish to him, so he cast out his net and pulled a whole school out of the water. When he saw them gasping, he said to them: "Do not trouble yourselves to dance now, since you did not want to dance to my pipe before."

### A Crowned Potsherd.

The nonsense of human honors <note>The original term *Ehrenbezeugung* refers to the various rituals, titles, and symbols used to show respect or status.</note>. One could compile an entire registry of such nonsense with which people show each other honor and shame, as almost everything is merely a play of their passions. He who wants to measure the approval or the blame of his actions not by the shortest path—namely, that of his inner conviction—but only by how it will please others to judge them, will waver back and forth like a reed and in the end be of use to no one. People often treat with the greatest shame today what they revered with distinguished approval yesterday. — When the Cypriots <note>The inhabitants of the Mediterranean island of Cyprus.</note> had murdered their own king, they stuck his head up in mockery...

## A Straw Wreath in the Mist.

The value of a thing depends mostly only on the prevailing opinion of men. The mist of imagination, through which they behold almost everything around them, makes them, as it were, take a straw wreath for gold. Whoever has once gained a reputation for an art or science can occasionally do sloppy work <note>The original German uses the term *pfuschen*, meaning to work unskillfully or to bungle a task.</note>; common opinion will still be satisfied with it.

Opinion also plays a great role in attacks by the enemy. — When Polyperchon <note>Polyperchon (c. 394–303 BCE) was a Macedonian general who served under Alexander the Great.</note> wanted to lead his army against the Peloponnesians <note>The inhabitants of the Peloponnese, the southern peninsula of Greece.</note>, he first showed them how insignificant the enemies were. Namely, he put on two old coats, placed an old felt hat <note>The original term *Filz* refers to a basic felt hat, used here to represent poverty or low status.</note> on his head, and took a club in his hand. "This is what the creatures look like," he said, "who are taking the field against you." Whereupon his warriors swore not to waste another moment in—

When Brennus <note>Brennus (died 279 BCE) was a chieftain of the Gauls who famously invaded Greece and attempted to loot the temple at Delphi.</note> intended to undertake a campaign into Greece, he presented several captured Greeks to his army; they were of small, frail stature, without beards or hair, dressed in filthy, tattered cloaks. He placed them directly beside the most handsome and sinewy warriors of his own legions. At this, he said: "These are the Greek heroes who await you; one should almost be ashamed to fight with them."

A similar anecdote is told of Frederick the Great <note>Frederick II (1712–1786), the King of Prussia known for his military reforms and Enlightenment ideals.</note> when he first led his Prussians against the Cossacks <note>A group of predominantly East Slavic-speaking people who became known as members of democratic, self-governing, semi-military communities; in this context, they served as irregular light cavalry for the Russian Empire.</note>.

### A Bundle of Sticks.

The symbol of unity. When Scilurus <note>Scilurus was a King of the Scythians in the 2nd century BCE. This story is a classic illustration of the proverb "Unity is strength."</note>, King of the Scythians, was near death, he summoned his eighty sons, each with a bundle of sticks before him, and asked them whether they might be capable of breaking these bundles. When they answered this in the negative, he took out various individual sticks and broke them. By this, he gave his sons to understand that no enemy would be able to harm them as long as they remained inseparable in complete uni— <note>The text ends mid-word, likely completing the word *Einigkeit* (unity).</note>

However, the following old anecdote testifies that it is not always advisable to wage war against divided peoples. When the Romans fell into conflict among themselves, the Dacians <note>The Dacians were an Indo-European people who inhabited the region of the Carpathian Mountains and the Black Sea; they were frequent adversaries of the Roman Empire.</note> were ready to declare war on them. But their leader did not find it advisable, and to make his opinion clear to them, he had two biting dogs brought forward which, as soon as they caught sight of each other, fell upon one another violently. At that moment, however, he let a wolf appear; quickly, the dogs separated and fell upon the wolf with united fury. No further argument was needed to dissuade the Dacians from their plan.

### Fortune with a Net

Whoever is once fortunate catches more and more; but Fortune <note>The German *Glück* can mean both "luck" and "happiness." In this context, it refers to "Lady Luck" or personified Fortune.</note> often ensnares her favorites so much that, blinded by their own brilliance, they do not see the looming danger that malicious Fortune prepares for them. — The powerful and successful commander <note>original: "Kayser" (Emperor). Historically, Timotheus (d. 354 BCE) was a celebrated Athenian general. His political rivals mocked his success by suggesting he was so lucky that cities fell into his nets while he slept.</note> Timotheus was once depicted by his enemies as a sleeping figure with a net, into which a multitude of cities flowed of their own accord. — He who is satiated becomes arrogant, but also lazy and cowardly. Thus, it is said that a full lion may be startled by deer and hares and flee from them; a depiction that has been observed among several hieroglyphs <note>In this period, "hieroglyphs" was a term often used for any ancient symbolic or emblematic carvings, such as those found on Roman or Greek stones and gems, rather than specifically Egyptian writing.</note> on an ancient stone.

### Dogs that let themselves be bound by cats.

Wicked judges, who allow themselves to be led by malicious accusers at their whim, act against the magnanimous and patient man who does not complain. "Bad judges," <note>original: "Judices mali"</note> as the ancients used to say, "do not look at the merits of the cases, but at the merits of the money; not at the laws, but at the bribes <note>original: "munera", which refers to gifts or favors intended to influence</note>; not what reason dictates, but what their will desires; not what the law intends, but what their mind craves; they do not incline their soul toward justice, but rather turn it away from their heart, and, fashioning a semblance of justice as they please, they resemble the unjust accusers whose malice they enjoy, arbitrarily condemning the innocent defendant whom they hate."

Wicked judges do not look at the weight of the matter, but rather the weight of the money; not at the laws, but at the bribes; not what reason commands, but what their own wicked will drives them toward; not what the law intends, but what suits their own purpose. They do not turn their hearts toward justice <term>justice|The original text uses "Gerechtigkeit" and the Latin "iustitia," referring here to the moral virtue of fairness and the impartial application of law</term>, but rather turn away from it; and, being quite like the unjust accusers in whose depravity they find pleasure, they invent a pretense of righteousness at their own convenience in order to arbitrarily condemn the innocent whom they hate. But it can sometimes happen—

<summary>This page employs the metaphor of "dogs bound by cats" to critique judicial corruption, specifically how weak or wicked judges are manipulated by malicious accusers and bribes. It contrasts the dictates of reason and law with the personal greed and whims of a corrupt judiciary.</summary>
<keywords>judicial corruption, bribery, justice, legal ethics, allegory, malicious accusers, innocent defendants, Judices mali</keywords>

# The Mariner Acessäus

The ancients depicted Acessäus with arms crossed over one another, directing his eyes toward heaven, as if to leave his successful voyage to it without any effort of his own. But heaven only helps those who help themselves, says Franklin <note>A reference to Benjamin Franklin's famous aphorism, emphasizing personal responsibility over passive waiting</note>, and whoever wants to fish must not gape at the moon to try and "fish out" from there, as it were, whether its light is shining properly everywhere. By the image of Acessäus, one understood not so much those who neglect their business and postpone work from one day to the next, as rather the wicked schemers <term>schemers|The original "Ränkemacher" refers to individuals who use intrigue and underhanded tactics to intentionally delay or obstruct progress</term> who delay and drag things out to the detriment of others. But—

> Malice itself drinks the largest part of its own poison, says Seneca. <note>original: "Malitia ipſa maximam partem veneni ſui bibit." Seneca was a Roman Stoic philosopher; this quote suggests that the act of being malicious harms the perpetrator more than the victim.</note>
> 
> Wickedness swallows the greatest part of its poison itself.

## Some Remarks

# Egyptian Priests.

---

Just as the sacred picture-writing of the Egyptians is known to us by the Greek name, **Hieroglyphs**; so too are the priests named for us as *Hierogrammateis* <note>original Greek: ἱερογραμματεῖς</note>, *Hierophoroi* <note>original Greek: ἱεροφοροι</note>, and *Hierostoloi* <note>original Greek: ἱεροστολοι</note>—that is, sacred scribes, supervisors, keepers, and interpreters of the sacred doctrines. According to Lucian: "because they carried the sacred discourse concerning the gods, pure from all superstition and meddling, within their souls as if in a chest, and shrouded it," <note>original Greek: ὅτι τὸν ἱερὸν λόγον περὶ θεῶν πάσης καθαρεύοντα δεισιδαιμονίας, καὶ περιεργίας ἐν τῇ ψυχῇ φέροντες, ὥσπερ ἐν κίστῃ καὶ περιστέλλοντες. Lucian of Samosata was a 2nd-century Syrian-Greek satirist who often wrote about ancient religious practices.</note> because they kept the sacred doctrine pure from all superstition and absurdity in their souls, as if in a secure vessel. They taught the people what was beneficial for them, but reserved the higher wisdom for the initiated. The ancient <term>priests|The original uses "Sacerdoten," a Latinate term emphasizing the formal, sacrificial, and high-status nature of the office.</term> shared the scepter with the kings, which they—

<summary>This page introduces the various classes of Egyptian priests, such as sacred scribes and keepers of doctrine, highlighting their role as guardians of esoteric wisdom. It explains that while they provided practical guidance to the public, they kept "higher" spiritual truths reserved for a small circle of initiates.</summary>

<keywords>Egyptian priests, Hieroglyphs, Hierogrammateis, Hierophoroi, Hierostoloi, Lucian, sacred wisdom, initiation, Scepter</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues from page 137, which discussed the symbols and status of Egyptian priests, ending with how they decorated their scepters.</meta>

decorated with the figure of the plow in honor of agriculture. They stood constantly at the King's side like the most distinguished Senate, supporting him with instruction as well as counsel and action. Among them were many exceptionally wise and politically astute men who, based on the current state of affairs, predicted much that actually came to pass; and *Suidas* <note>The Suda (or Suidas) is a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia containing much information on the ancient world.</note> claims it was clearly foretold by one such sage that an inspired *Israelite* (*Moses*) would become very dangerous to the Egyptian empire.

One must not, however, confuse the *Hierogrammates* <note>Sacred scribes.</note> with the *Arpedonapten*, who were merely mathematicians or land-surveyors; as a passage from *Democritus* <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 460–370 BCE) who traveled to Egypt to study mathematics and religion.</note> indicates: "I have listened to many learned men, and as yet no one has surpassed me in the construction of lines and their proofs, not even those called *Arpedonaptai* <note>original Greek: Και λογιων ανθρωπων πλειων εξηκουσα, και γραμμεων συνθεosιος μετ’ αποδειξιος ουδεις πω με παρηλλαξεν, ουδ’ οἱ Αιγυπτιων καλεομενοι Αρπεδοναπται. The term literally means 'rope-stretchers,' referring to surveyors who used knotted ropes to measure fields after the Nile flooded.</note> by the Egyptians." That is to say: I have heard many learned men, and so far no one has surpassed me in the composition of lines and their proofs, not even the so-called *Arpedonapten* of the Egyptians.

They enjoy an excellent reputation; for they live in close association with the priests and, as holy men, freely enter the sanctuary. <meta>Continuity: This page follows the discussion of the classes of Egyptian religious functionaries, specifically moving from the embalmers (Entaphiastai) mentioned on the previous page to the formal ranks of the priesthood as seen in public ritual.</meta>

Clement of Alexandria <note>Clement of Alexandria (c. 150–215 CE) was a Christian theologian whose writings provide some of the most detailed surviving descriptions of ancient Egyptian priestly grades and their "Hermetic" library.</note> describes to us a whole procession of Egyptian priests. First, he says, comes the priest <term>Odus|From the Greek 'ōidos' (ᾠδός), meaning singer or chanter.</term>, the singer, who presents something from the symbols of the art of music; he must know by heart two books of <note>The author uses 'Mercury' to refer to the Egyptian god Thoth, whom the Greeks identified with Hermes/Mercury. Thoth was the patron of scribes and the reputed author of all sacred texts.</note> Mercury, one of which contains the hymns to the gods, and the other the royal rules of life.

Following him is the <term>Horoscopus|From the Greek 'hōroskopos' (Ὡροσκόπος), meaning one who observes the hours; an astrologer or astronomer.</term>, the hour-teller, with a timepiece and a palm branch, the symbol of astrology. He must know by heart four books of Mercury concerning astrology.

Then follows the <term>Hierogrammates|From the Greek 'hierogrammateus' (ἱερογραμματεύς), meaning a sacred scribe.</term>, or sacred scribe, with feathers on his head, and a book, a ruler, an inkwell, and a reed pen in his hands. This man must not only understand all hieroglyphs, but also the entire description of the world and the earth, the course of the sun, the moon, and the five planets; the complete description of Egypt and the Nile, all sacred vessels and ornaments, all consecrated places, all measures and weights, and everything pertaining to the temples and sacred objects.

Next, with the sacrificial chalice, comes the <term>Stolist|From the Greek 'stolistēs' (στολιστής), the official responsible for the sacred wardrobe and the ritual dressing of divine statues.</term>, who cares for the ornaments and the festive robes, and who must know everything that belongs to the ceremonial pomp, the sacrifices, hymns, and prayers. ### Concerning the sacred wheels in the temples of the Egyptians.

These wheels, which constantly rotated around their axis with a special brilliance, pointed to the eternal motive power of the divine nature. This relates to Democritus's <gloss>mind in a spherical fire <note>original: "νουν εν πυρι σφαιροειδες"</note></gloss>—the circular motion of the world-soul in fire—or <note>Parmenides (c. 5th century BCE) was a pre-Socratic philosopher who described the universe in terms of "rings" or "crowns" of fire and darkness.</note> Parmenides' fiery belt of the world, whose creative light encompasses and sustains everything. The Egyptians generally considered every circular shape and movement to be something

## On the Branches and Leaves in Egyptian Temples.

No specific explanation for this can actually be given, although some wish to see them as an image of the first food sources or of transience <term>Vergänglichkeit: the philosophical concept that all things are fleeting and eventually fade away</term>. This custom does not necessarily need to be symbolic; it was, quite naturally, the first decoration used in all cases. Fresh foliage is one of the first objects to provide pleasure to the eye and the sense of smell; it is therefore entirely natural that one finds it used among all peoples for both secular and sacred purposes. — Thus the Athenians celebrated a festival of branches, during which they [processed] about in honor of Bacchus <note>Also known as Dionysus, the Greek god of wine, fertility, and ritual madness.</note> with grapevines and other branches

<summary>This page connects the Greek poet Anacreon's imagery of life as a rolling wheel to the broader theme of transience, then transitions into a discussion on the natural origins of using plant life as decoration in Egyptian and Greek religious rituals.</summary>
<keywords>Anacreon, transience, Egyptian temples, Athenian festivals, Bacchus, botanical symbolism, ritual decoration, Greek poetry</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion on the use of foliage in religious contexts, transitioning from the Greek festivals of Bacchus mentioned on the previous page to Jewish, Christian, and ancient philosophical perspectives.</meta>

...went. According to Josephus <note>Titus Flavius Josephus (37 – c. 100 AD) was a Romano-Jewish historian whose works provide key insights into first-century Judaism.</note>, the Jews also made use of myrtle and palm branches during thank-offerings. Similarly, the celebration of Palm Sunday remains among Christians as a commemoration of Christ’s entry into Jerusalem, when the people scattered palms and branches on the path in his honor. The use of May-bushes <term>Mayen: traditional green birch branches or small trees used as decoration, particularly in German-speaking regions</term> in churches during the festival of Pentecost <note>Also known as Whitsun, a Christian feast celebrating the descent of the Holy Spirit upon the Apostles.</note> is also a remnant of the ancient custom of nations to decorate the interior of their temples with fresh foliage. — The most ancient peoples offered neither incense nor animal sacrifices to the deity, but rather foliage and herbs, through which they honored the creator and sustainer of all things, according to Porphyry <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234 – c. 305 AD) was a Neoplatonic philosopher who wrote "On Abstinence from Animal Food," discussing early religious offerings.</note>. Ezekiel, by the way, rails strongly against this pagan use of branches; and after mentioning all the Egyptian abominations of idolatry, including all kinds of images of animals and worms on the inner walls of the temples, etc., he seems to conclude with the most loathsome practice when he says: "Behold, they hold the branches (or according to Luther’s translation) the grapevines to their noses!" However, the original expression *hazmorah* <note>original Hebrew: הַזְּמוֹרָה (haz-zemorah), appearing in Ezekiel 8:17; it traditionally refers to a vine-branch, though its ritual context here is debated.</note> means both a sound and a branch; therefore, some commentators wish to understand it as a mockery and contempt for God, in that one [makes a noise] with the nose...

...at the touch of the branches, at the same time, produced a mocking tone. Moreover, apart from the most loathsome idolatry, it can signify any false divine worship; for according to pagan custom, one demonstrated divine adoration whenever one touched the nose with the branches.

# The Celestial Sphere.

The book of all human destinies. This was one of the primary teachings of the Egyptians and Chaldeans <note>The Chaldeans were an ethnic group in Mesopotamia whose name became synonymous with "astrologers" or "astronomers" in the ancient world.</note>, which has perhaps been preserved longer than all others and still finds followers here and there. — Orpheus <note>A legendary Greek hero and musician whose name is attached to "Orphism," a set of religious beliefs and practices originating in ancient Greece.</note> calls the stars:

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the reference to the Roman poet Juvenal started on the previous page and shifts from discussing astrological fate to a philosophical examination of human agency and the social influences people exert on one another throughout the different stages of life.</meta>

...is what Juvenal <note>Decimus Junius Juvenalis (late 1st/early 2nd century AD) was a Roman poet famous for his *Satires*. The author here is finishing a thought on a quote from Satire 7 regarding the power of fate.</note> calls the eternal law of necessity. One certainly cannot attribute everything to an unavoidable fate; however, those who make mortals the sole masters of their destiny also go too far. One need only consider the different stages of a person’s life and ask at what age one is completely master of oneself and one's situation. How little or how much control does one have as a child, as a youth, as a man, as a husband, as a citizen, or as an old man?

The bad or good influence of the stars is surely of very little significance, whether such astrological claims be true or not. But the influence of people upon one another is a far more important chapter, which is especially to be taken to heart. Perhaps every person has more evil influences to fear from others than good ones to hope for. But should this be a necessary law, an unavoidable fate? That would be the same as claiming that human beings are destined more for irrationality than for reason. The greatest possible cultivation and following of the latter is, rather, our general purpose. By this standard, everyone can now easily judge and weigh their own influence and that of their fellow humans, and consider to what extent one should be cautious or carefree in this regard.

<summary>The author critiques absolute fatalism and astrology, arguing instead that the most significant factor in a person's life is the mutual influence humans exert on each other, which should be guided by reason.</summary>
<keywords>Juvenal, Law of Necessity, fate, stages of life, astrology, human influence, reason, morality, destiny</keywords>

The **Seres**, says an ancient Syrian writer, **Bardaisan**, a free and peaceful people, have a single law that forbids them from murder, adultery, theft, and all forms of idolatry. <note>original: "Bardesanes." Bardaisan (154–222 AD) was a Syrian Gnostic philosopher and scientist. He wrote the "Book of the Laws of Countries" to argue that human laws and free will are more powerful than the influence of the stars.</note> One therefore sees among them neither temples, nor thieves, murderers, or adulterers, and no single star is capable of inducing them to any misdeed. This man likely envisioned a people who had reached such a high degree of **practical reason** <term>practical reason|practische Vernunft: In Enlightenment philosophy, this refers to the capacity of a person to decide their actions based on logic and moral principles rather than impulse or superstition.</term> that they did not even require temples; but also knew nothing of a multitude of burdensome financial and administrative laws, which only hinder free trade and industry—the true prosperity of citizens—and give rise to a thousand transgressions. According to **Pliny**, the **Seres**, whom people consider to be the Chinese, were certain peoples in Asian **Scythia**, who spun a kind of cotton as skillfully as silk. <note>Ancient Mediterranean writers often confused silk (produced by silkworms) with "tree wool" (cotton) or fine linen, believing it was harvested directly from trees.</note>

# Symbols
->of some Gods and Altars of the Ancients.<-

Osiris, the most prominent deity of the Egyptians, was represented by the symbol of a scepter with an eye placed above it, in order to signify both the vigilance and the moderation of a prudent ruler who cares for the well-being of his people. For this reason, Theodosius <note>Emperor Theodosius I, also known as Theodosius the Great.</note> also gave the following instruction to Honorius <note>His son, who would become Emperor of the Western Roman Empire.</note>:

> Conduct yourself as a citizen and a father, look out for the welfare of all;
> Not for yourself; let not your own desires, but those of the public, move you.
> <note>original: "Tu ciuem patremque geras, tu consule cunctis; Nec tibi; nec tua te moueant, sed publica vota."</note>

> Be a citizen and a father, advise and help everyone,
> and not just yourself;
> Let not your own wishes, but only those of the public, move you.

->*  *  *<-

Isis, the goddess of fertility, was depicted with many breasts because she was responsible for nourishing all living creatures.

<meta>The text continues the exploration of Egyptian mythology and symbolism, moving from the figures of Osiris and Isis on the previous page to the mysterious Minerva of Sais and the complex iconography of Serapis.</meta>

The Minerva at Sais in Egypt, whom <term>Plato</term> <note>The Greek philosopher mentions the shrine at Sais in his dialogue, *Timaeus*.</note> also mentions, is considered by some to be <term>Isis</term>; others claim that eternal wisdom was worshipped there under the image of Minerva. This is made clear by her inscription: “I am all that was, is, and shall be, and no mortal has yet uncovered my veil.” <note>original Greek: "Εγω ειμι πᾶν το γεγονος, και ον, και εσομενον, und τον εμον πεπλον ουδεις των θνητων απεκαλυψεν." This famous "Veil of Isis" inscription at Sais was often cited by Enlightenment thinkers as a symbol of the mysteries of Nature.</note>

->* * *<-

<term>Serapis</term>, a three-headed idol in the form of a serpent. The Egyptians living along the Nile regarded a large, fiery serpent as a deity, which they worshipped under this three-headed image. Of these heads, the middle one was a large lion’s head, the right was a fawning dog’s head, and the left was a wolf’s head, all of which together carried a fruit basket above them.

Some understand the lion’s head to represent the present time; the wolf’s head the past; and the dog’s head the future. <note>The lion represents the strength of the present, the wolf the disappearance of the past, and the fawning dog the hope or uncertainty of the future.</note> Others understand the first as royal power; the second as military might; and the third as the faithful people.

->* * *<-

<meta>This page continues the catalog of various epithets and symbolic representations of Zeus/Jupiter, moving from his role as a political guardian to more specific mythological, local, and metaphorical manifestations.</meta>

## Zeus on Crete

**Zeus on Crete** <gloss>the Zeus in Crete</gloss> <note>original Greek: ὁ εν Κρητῃ Ζευς (*ho en Krētē Zeus*)</note>, or the **Cretan Jupiter**, was depicted without ears to signify that the deity does not hear the foolish prayers and wishes of mortals. This image was also used to describe a deaf ruler or judge.

---

## Zeus, the Fly-Averter

**Zeus, the expeller of flies** <gloss>Zeus the Fly-Averter</gloss> <note>original Greek: Ζευς απομυιος (*Zeus Apomyios*)</note>. By "flies," people understood slanderers and other rabble who irritate and feed upon the merit and good names of others. To frighten these away, one offered sacrifices to Zeus the Fly-Averter. **Hercules** is said to have once sacrificed to him, whereupon all the flies retreated across the **Alpheus**, a river in **Arcadia**.

---

## Zeus, the Painter of Centaurs

**Zeus, who paints Centaurs upon the clouds**; an image of a ruler who occupies himself with trifles <note>The phrase "painting Centaurs on clouds" implies creating illusions or pursuing empty, fantastical whims instead of governance.</note>, like the <term>Emperor Domitian</term> <note>Titus Flavius Domitianus (r. 81–96 AD), who was famously ridiculed by historians like Suetonius for spending his time catching and stabbing flies with a pen rather than attending to affairs of state.</note> the fly-catcher.

---

## Zeus with the Ram’s Skin

**Zeus with the ram's skin**. The **Thebans**, who otherwise abstained from rams, slaughtered one annually, hanging its skin around the image of Zeus. <note>This likely refers to the Egyptian city of Thebes; the Greeks identified their Zeus with the Egyptian god Amun, who was often depicted with ram's horns.</note>

<summary>This page explores diverse cultural and metaphorical interpretations of Zeus, including his depiction as a "deaf" god in Crete, his role as a protector against slanderers, and the ritualistic use of ram skins in Thebes.</summary>

## Zeus of the Council

The **Athenians** had erected a statue of this Zeus in their **Council of Five Hundred** <note>The *Boule*, a deliberative body of citizens who ran the daily affairs of the city in ancient Athens.</note>; it was meant to remind every member to desire only that which is in accordance with the will of the deity—that is, with the purest **reason**. By following reason alone, one would surely have no evil consequences to fear.

## Zeus the Hundred-Footed and the Beam-Strong

**Zeus the Hundred-Footed and the Beam-Strong**. Through this imagery, the ancients intended to symbolize **eternal Providence**, which has firmly established the foundations of everything beneath us and firmly supported everything above us. <note>The "hundred-footed" (*hekatonpodas*) and "beam-strong" (*erysipole*) epithets emphasize the structural stability of the cosmos and the far-reaching influence of divine law.</note>

->* * *<-

**Zeus on Crete**, <gloss>the Zeus in Crete</gloss> <note>original Greek: ὁ ἐν Κρήτῃ Ζεύς (*ho en Krētē Zeus*)</note>, or the **Cretan Jupiter**, was depicted without ears to indicate that the deity does not hear the foolish prayers and wishes of mortals. This image was also used to designate a deaf ruler or judge.

---

**Zeus, the Dispeller of Flies**, <gloss>Zeus the fly-averter</gloss> <note>original Greek: Ζεὺς ἀπόμυιος (*Zeus apomyios*)</note>. By "flies," the ancients understood slanderers and other scoundrels who vex the merit and good names of others. To drive these away, one offered sacrifices to Zeus the Fly-Dispeller. It is said that **Hercules** once sacrificed to him, after which all the flies migrated across the **Alpheus**, a river in **Arcadia**.

---

**Zeus, who paints Centaurs on the clouds**; the image of a ruler who occupies himself with trifles <note>The German *Possen* suggests buffoonery or trivial antics.</note>, much like the **Emperor Domitian**, known as "the fly-catcher." <note>The Roman Emperor Domitian was famously said to have spent his leisure time catching flies and stabbing them with a pen to relieve boredom.</note>

---

**Zeus with the Ram’s Skin**. The **Thebans**, who otherwise abstained from rams, slaughtered one annually and hung its skin around the statue of Zeus. <note>This ritual relates to a myth where Zeus appeared to Hercules while wearing a ram's head and fleece to avoid being fully recognized.</note>

Regarding the various forms that the ancients gave to the gods, **Xenophanes** <note>Xenophanes of Colophon (c. 570–478 BC) was a Greek philosopher who famously criticized the tendency of humans to conceive of gods in their own image.</note> made the observation that if animals could paint, each would create a god according to its own form.

A modern fable shares a similar idea: a lion, encountering various depictions in a picture gallery of **Hercules** and **Samson** tearing a lion apart, turns away in indignation and says to his guide: "You would see quite different things if my brothers could paint!"

---

**Juno Moneta**, Juno with the epithet of "the Coin" <note>The Latin title *Moneta* likely derived from *monere* (to warn), but because Rome’s mint was located in her temple, it became the root of our words "money" and "mint."</note>. The goddess Juno received this special title during the war that the **Romans** fought against **Pyrrhus** <note>Pyrrhus of Epirus was a Greek king and one of Rome’s most formidable early enemies; his name is still associated with "Pyrrhic victories," which are won at too great a cost.</note>. Due to a great shortage of money, the Romans consulted this goddess for advice. Her answer was that they should fight with the weapons of justice; if they did, they would never lack for money. This came to pass. They fought with justice and integrity, and the enemies were defeated. For this reason, the goddess received that title, and later, money was minted in her temple. Moreover, this oracular pronouncement has always been found to hold true— <note>The text ends mid-sentence with "befun-", likely completing the word "befunden" (found/proven).</note>

Among the **Gaditanians** <note>The people of Gades, modern-day Cádiz in Spain. In antiquity, they were known for their wealth and trade, but this text highlights a specific local virtue.</note>, an altar to **Poverty** and **Diligence** was the most distinguished, upon which they sacrificed most frequently; for the industrious poor were held by them in higher regard than the rich, who had no need to work. To be sure, the industrious person is never entirely poor, yet through mere labor one never arrives at any particular wealth. It brings all the more shame upon the government and the wealthy of a country, however, when the poor inhabitants who would gladly be industrious must complain of a lack of work, and find themselves forced to leave the land, or even perish from grief and sorrow because of various oppressions and obstacles to their diligence and trade. — The carefree rulers and the rich are no more fortunate for this, as they apply their fortunes only toward debauchery or ruinous undertakings, rather than for the promotion of diligence.

The **Athenians** sacrificed at the **Altar of Mercy** <note>The Altar of *Eleos* in the Athenian marketplace was a famous sanctuary for the oppressed, symbolizing the city's reputation for compassion.</note> as long as milder customs still prevailed among them. They feel punishment and reward within themselves, even if no divine or human being outside of them rewards them.

<meta>This page completes the sentence started on page 160 regarding Democritus's belief in two deities, Punishment and Reward. It transitions into a philosophical comparison between divine justice and human (worldly) justice, focusing on the role of conscience versus legal retribution.</meta>

...or punished. Divine power extends further in this respect than worldly power, which—often driven by its own vengefulness—punishes more than it rewards. Worldly power is always vigilant and ready to seek out and punish the unknown evildoer, yet it is not so inclined toward the unknown good man to reward him. For doing good is the duty <note>original: "Schuldigkeit"</note> of every individual, and he is free to perform as much of it without reward as he is ever able; this is because one considers a person’s inner reward <note>This "inner reward" refers to the psychological state of peace or a clear conscience.</note> to be perfectly sufficient; but the same is not true of a person's inner punishment <note>The author argues that while a good conscience is a sufficient reward for virtue, a guilty conscience is not a sufficient substitute for the legal punishment of a crime.</note>.

### 7.

> I am bound and joined by iron, intended to hold many others bound; <note>original: "Nexa ligor ferro multos habitura ligatos"</note> I myself am bound first, but being bound, I bind in turn; I have set many free, yet I myself am not released.
>
> I am forged in iron and, only to chain others, I am chained first; indeed, I even embrace the chains; I also release others from their bonds, and yet I myself am not released. <note>This riddle likely describes a **lock** or a **shackle**. To function, the mechanism must be "engaged" (bound), and while it allows a prisoner to be freed, the device itself remains a fixed object of iron.</note>

### 8.

> Four equal sisters run by design, <note>original: "ex arte," referring to mechanical skill or artifice.</note> as if they were competing even though their labor is shared by all; they are nearly side-by-side, yet they are unable to touch one another.
>
> Four sisters run with equal pace, in equal work, like a flash of lightning, and yet they can never reach one another. <note>This riddle refers to the **wheels of a carriage**. They move at the same speed and work toward the same goal, but because they are fixed to axles, they can never collide or overtake one another.</note>

### 9.

> Letters have fed me, yet I know not what a letter is; I have lived in books, but I am not more studious because of it; I have devoured the Muses, <note>In this context, "devouring the Muses" means eating the physical books containing poetry and art.</note> yet I have made no progress myself.
>
> I graze upon writings without knowing a single syllable; I live in books; I will never be called learned; I devour every saying and become no wiser for it. <note>This is a famous riddle describing a **bookworm** or a **moth**. It consumes the physical material of literature but remains ignorant of the knowledge contained within the text.</note>

### 10.

The night gives me my name, and I am called a most unlucky omen;
I sit in the darkness and do not entrust myself to the light of day.
<note>Latin: "Nox mihi dat nomen" / German: "Den Namen gab die Nacht." This riddle describes the Owl, whose Latin name "Noctua" is derived from "Nox" (Night). In many cultures, the owl's cry was considered a premonition of death.</note>

The night gave me my name and many an evil legend,
In the darkness I always sit, and never show myself by day.

### 11.

Long ago, a royal daughter sat upon my back,

I am carried through the heavens, yet I also walk upon the earth itself.

Europa sat upon my back,
Where Tartars pluck their herbs;
My image glows in the ancient sky,
Just as I am seen here on the earth.

->cares;<-

->am.<-

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a supplement titled "Nachtrag," likely added to a larger work on ancient rituals or secret societies. The tone is critical and reflects Enlightenment-era rationalism.</meta>

# Supplement

->concerning<-

## the Ceremony during the Initiations into the Egyptian Mysteries.

---

The ancients, like many moderns, made secrets out of subjects that are no such thing, except insofar as the primary root causes of things in general are—and will remain—mysteries. In doing so, they created only unnecessary fuss and preparations which acted more upon the imagination than the intellect <term>Verstand: the faculty of logical reasoning and understanding</term>, and for the most part fell into the realm of the tasteless and the ridiculous. Driven by sordid, lucrative motives, they promoted pride and conceit, an adventurous leaning toward the wondrous—often even toward superstition—rather than the genuine degree of a clear, impartial reason. Without such reason, a mortal, despite all knowledge and <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the author is arguing that without "clear reason," any amount of esoteric knowledge is useless or even harmful to the individual.</note>

<summary>This introductory page of a supplement critiques the "Egyptian Mysteries," arguing that ancient secret societies used unnecessary theatrics to promote superstition rather than genuine rational thought.</summary>
<keywords>Egyptian Mysteries, initiation ceremonies, Enlightenment rationalism, superstition, imagination vs. intellect</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues a philosophical argument regarding the necessity of subjecting human inquiry to the laws of reason. It appears to pick up mid-sentence from page 169.</meta>

...abilities never possesses true insight. Just as all human impulses and actions—both in regard to oneself and toward one’s fellow men—must be subordinate to the single law of <term>pure reason<note>German: *reine Vernunft*. A hallmark of Enlightenment philosophy, particularly that of Immanuel Kant, suggesting that logic and moral law are the ultimate authorities for human conduct.</note></term>, so this applies especially to the spirit of inquiry and the striving to reach the truth through ever deeper penetration into the secrets of nature. 

How harmful every deviation from this law is can be seen in the greater or lesser irregularities within each person, which will not remain hidden from anyone upon a closer examination of themselves. One should seek only the truth, even if it should be the most unpleasant. What use is all the false shimmer <note>original: *Schimmer*; here meaning a deceptive or superficial luster</note> that only lulls us into a sense of security and deceives us in the end? 

True reason alone is true wisdom; despite all the vain allure of human systems and dogmas, it does not allow itself to be deterred even by the saddest truths. Instead, it remains clear-headed and continues to be useful everywhere for as long as strength and consciousness are granted to it. That such bright minds existed even in the most distant antiquity is certainly true; but it is just as certain that they followed the current of adventurous <note>original: *abentheuerlicher*; in this context, it refers to the strange, irrational, or fantastical customs of the ancient world.</note> customs.

<summary>The author argues that all human actions and scientific inquiries must be governed by pure reason to avoid self-deception. He asserts that true wisdom requires facing even "sad truths" rather than being misled by superficial systems or ancient superstitions.</summary>

<keywords>pure reason, spirit of inquiry, truth, wisdom, human nature, Enlightenment, antiquity, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page transitions from a general philosophical discussion on reason and ancient customs to a specific, detailed account of the initiation rituals into the "Egyptian Mysteries." ### The manner of initiation into the Egyptian mysteries was as follows: <note>*) An excerpt from a manuscript by an anonymous author, which may perhaps provide an opportunity for a more detailed work in the future.</note>

Every initiate first had to be specifically recommended by an initiate. Usually, this was done by the King himself, through a letter to the priests, who directed him from Heliopolis to Memphis, and from there to Thebes; whereupon he was circumcised. Next, certain foods were forbidden to him, especially legumes <note>German: *Hülsenfrüchte*; ancient traditions often associated beans with the dead or ritual impurity</note> and fish; also, he was no longer allowed to drink wine until it was occasionally permitted to him again in a higher grade. 

Now he had to spend several months in an underground cave and write down his thoughts, which were carefully examined to learn about his intellect and his inner self. From there, he was led into the portico of <term>Hermes<note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure combining the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, regarded as the author of sacred texts and the founder of alchemy and magic.</note></term>, where he had to memorize a multitude of moral maxims <note>original: *Sittensprüche*; ethical aphorisms intended to guide conduct</note> located on the columns. 

Then came the <term>Thesmophoros<note>A Greek term meaning "Law-bringer," here identifying a specific official or priest who presided over the ritual proceedings.</note></term>, bound his eyes and hands with strong linen bands, and led him through the Gate of the Profane—while driving away the common people with a stout whip—to the Gate of the Mens-

---

<summary>This page describes the rigorous preliminary requirements for entering the Egyptian mysteries, including royal recommendation, dietary restrictions, and solitary reflection. It outlines the transition from intellectual preparation to the first physical stages of the secret ritual.</summary>

<keywords>Egyptian mysteries, initiation, Heliopolis, Memphis, Thebes, Hermes, Thesmophoros, circumcision, ritual purification</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the initiation ritual into the "Egyptian Mysteries," picking up mid-sentence from the previous page. It transitions from the candidate's arrival at the gate to the formal trials and the first degree of membership.</meta>

...ship, as the first degree of <term>Apprentice<note>German: *Lehrling*; the entry-level rank in this esoteric system.</note></term>, or <term>Pastophoros<note>original: *Pastophoros*; a Greek term for a lower-ranking Egyptian priest who carried sacred objects in processions.</note></term>. To one of these apprentices, who stood guard before the gate, the <term>Thesmophoros<note>The "Law-bringer" or master of ceremonies introduced on the previous page.</note></term> tapped on the shoulder, whereupon the guard announced the new apprentice by knocking. If he remained undaunted, the laws to which he had to submit were read to him; whereupon he had to bow on his bare knees before the Hierophant and—while the point of a sword was placed against his throat—pledge loyalty and secrecy; during which Sun, Moon, and Stars were invoked as witnesses to the truth. Now the apprentice’s eyes were uncovered <note>original: *öfnete man dem Lehrling die Augen*, literally "opened the eyes," referring to the removal of the blindfold mentioned on the previous page.</note> and he was placed between two square pillars, where a ladder of seven rungs, along with eight doors of various metals, were located; meanwhile the Hierophant delivered the following speech:

> “I turn to you, who have the right to hear me. Close all the doors so that no profane person or mocker may enter. But you, children of labor and of the heavenly for-”

...instruction, hear my speech and mark the great truths that I present to you. Guard yourselves against prejudices and passions, which only lead you away from the right path of <term>bliss<note>German: *Glückseligkeit*; in 18th-century philosophy, this refers to a state of profound moral and spiritual fulfillment rather than simple happiness.</note></term>. Direct your thoughts toward the Divine Being, and let Him be constantly before your eyes, in order to guide your heart and senses. If you wish to tread the path of bliss, then consider that you always walk before the eyes of the Almighty, who created the world. He is the only Being who has produced and maintains all things, and who exists of Himself. He sees all. No mortal eye can see Him, and no true child of diligence shall escape His gaze."

After this speech, the ladder was explained to the <term>Apprentice<note>German: *Lehrling*; the entry-level rank of this secret society.</note></term> as a symbol of the <term>transmigration of souls<note>German: *Seelenwanderung*; the belief that the soul passes into another body or state of existence after death.</note></term>; he was also taught that the names of the gods had a completely different meaning than what the common people believed. He was explained the causes of the winds, of lightning, and of thunder, and was taught the symbolic language and the common <term>hieroglyphic script<note>The author reflects a common Enlightenment-era belief that hieroglyphs were a secret code for scientific and philosophical truths hidden from the masses.</note></term>. In general, this first degree was dedicated to <term>natural science<note>German: *Naturlehre*.</note></term>, <term>anatomy<note>German: *Zergliederungskunst*; literally the 'art of dissection.'</note></term>, and the <term>medical arts<note>German: *Arzneykunst*.</note></term>.

The initiates were given the name of "Secrecy," and they also recognized one another by a special handshake. The new Pastopho- <meta>catchword: Pastophoros; this Greek term refers to a lower-ranking Egyptian priest.</meta>

### The fourth degree, Cistophoros, the Battle of the Shadows.

After the passage of the "days of wrath" <note>A symbolic period of mourning or trial mentioned in the previous degree's oath.</note> lasting a year and a half, the **Thesmophoros** <term>Thesmophoros: Literally "law-bearer"; an officer who introduces the candidate to the higher mysteries.</term> returned, greeted the initiate kindly, gave him a sword and shield, and proceeded with him through dark passages until several hideous figures appeared with torches and snakes in their hands and attacked him with shouts. The **Thesmophoros** commanded him to defend himself bravely; which, however, was of no use; for he was taken prisoner, his eyes were bound, a rope was thrown around his neck, and he was dragged in this manner to the hall where he was to receive a new degree. The shadows withdrew with a sudden outcry; he was raised up and led, exhausted <note>original: "kraftlos"</note>, into the assembly, where the blindfold was removed from his eyes.

Here he found himself in the most magnificent hall, adorned with the most beautiful paintings. The King himself was present with the **Demiurgi** <term>Demiurgi: Derived from the Greek word for "craftsmen" or "creators," referring here to the high council or architects of the rite.</term>, who all wore the Order of Truth, along with the other members. The **Odos** <term>Odos: From the Greek for "way" or "path," here designating the Orator or Master of Ceremonies.</term>, or orator, now wished the **Cistophoros** <term>Cistophoros: "Basket-bearer," the title for this fourth stage of initiation.</term> success in his endeavor, but also told him that he had only completed half of his labor; whereupon a very bitter
