<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the "Crata Repoa," a famous 18th-century German mock-Egyptian initiation rite. Having attained the seventh degree of "Prophet" described on the previous page, the initiate is now eligible for specific administrative and ritual offices within the secret society.</meta>

...could also, after a certain period, obtain offices within the society and become a <term>Demiurgos<note>From the Greek "demiurgos" (craftsman/worker); here it refers to a high-ranking administrative officer or "Overseer."</note></term>.

---

The most distinguished **Demiurgos**, or Chief Overseer, wore a sky-blue coat embroidered with stars and a yellow belt; additionally, he wore a gold chain around his neck, featuring a sapphire set in precious stones. He served simultaneously as the highest judge.

The <term>Hierophant<note>original: "Hierophantes"; the high priest or "revealer of sacred things" who interpreted the mysteries.</note></term> wore almost the same attire, with the only difference being that he wore a cross.

The <term>Stolistes<note>Historically a "vestry keeper"; the official responsible for the sacred garments and ritual equipment.</note></term>, who was responsible for the safekeeping of the garments, wore a white-striped coat and a special kind of boot.

The <term>Hierostolistes<note>A "sacred dresser" or scribe; often depicted in these rites as the keeper of records and writing materials.</note></term> wore a feather on his hat and held a cylindrical vessel with ink in his hand.

The <term>Thesmophoros<note>Literally "law-bearer"; in this context, the officer who introduced the initiates and conducted them through the trials.</note></term> was required to introduce the initiates.

The <term>Zacoros<note>A temple official or attendant; here acting as the society's treasurer.</note></term> managed the treasury.

The <term>Romastes<note>A title likely meaning "steward" or "provost" in this ritual system.</note></term>, under whom all the <term>Pastophori<note>original: "Pastophoren"; the lower-ranking priests who carried the sacred images or shrines during processions.</note></term> served, saw to the table <note>This likely refers to the management of the communal ritual meals or the "table" of offerings.</note>, and the <term>Odos<note>From the Greek for "song" or "path"; here referring to the ritual cantor or public speaker.</note></term> was the orator or singer.

---

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the "Crata Repoa" ritual offices and moves into the daily life and symbolic language of the initiates. The previous page ended while describing the Romastes (provost); this page begins with the communal dining customs following those ritual duties.</meta>

Before going to the table, one had to wash. No wine was drunk, but only beer; also, a skeleton was set up, while the <term>Odos<note>From the Greek "oidos" (singer/cantor); the ritual singer responsible for leading hymns during ceremonies.</note></term> led the song, "O death, come to us at the right hour!" <note>original: "O Tod komm uns zur rechten Stunde!"</note>, which was sung by all those present. After the meal, one went to business and contemplations, or retired to rest, unless the <term>Gate of the Gods<note>A term used in this ritual system for a specific observatory or window used for stargazing.</note></term> was opened for astronomical observations, in which case one remained awake through entire nights.

->* * *<-

The <term>Amanic<note>Likely referring to "Ammonite" (from the god Amun); a common term in 18th-century occultism for a supposed secret Egyptian alphabet.</note></term> or Royal script of the Egyptian priests was devised from three secret principles, namely:
1. the △ triangle, as the origin of all things.
2. the ☐ square, as the sign of truth, and
3. the X, as the sign of perfection.
From these three, the symbol |⊠|—or more precisely, four triangles—was also composed, which was then broken down into the following letters:

<meta>This page transitions from the technical discussion of cryptographic alphabets and Spartan symbols to the symbolic interpretation of Egyptian monuments. # Explanation
## of the Hieroglyphs of the Obelisk
### before the Lateran in Rome

->by<-

->C. F. K.<-

***

The raven, at the peak of the obelisk <note>The author is likely referring to the Lateran Obelisk, the largest standing ancient Egyptian obelisk in the world, brought to Rome in the 4th century. The identification of a 'raven' reflects the symbolic, often Hermetic interpretations of hieroglyphs popular in the 17th and 18th centuries.</note>, signifies a person more inclined toward evil than toward good, yet who feels a great thirst for knowledge; this is indicated by the ascending beetle <note>The scarab beetle (*Scarabaeus sacer*), which in ancient Egyptian religion symbolized the god Khepri and the movement of the sun.</note>, representing the drive for nourishment. Among the Egyptians, anyone who wished to be initiated <note>Likely referring to the "mysteries" or the process of becoming a priest.</note> was also called a raven. The beetle is also the image of the sun, and just as the sun brings about the day, so is the thirst for knowledge the primary means of dispelling darkness.

<vocab>Hieroglyphs, Obelisk, Lateran, Rome, Raven, Beetle, Egyptians, Sun, Thirst for knowledge</vocab>

<summary>This page offers a symbolic reading of the Lateran Obelisk, interpreting the raven and the scarab beetle as metaphors for the human soul's transition from ignorance to intellectual and spiritual enlightenment.</summary>
<keywords>Lateran Obelisk, Rome, hieroglyphs, raven symbolism, scarab beetle, Egyptian initiation, sun imagery, enlightenment</keywords>

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>Continuing the symbolic interpretation of the Lateran Obelisk’s hieroglyphs, this page moves from general animal symbolism (the raven and beetle) to a structured, sequential narrative of the initiation process into the Egyptian mysteries.</meta>

In the second section *) the registration for initiation <note>Einweihung: the process of being admitted into secret religious or philosophical knowledge.</note> takes place. Two persons in the customary white garments with high caps are initiates who are shaking hands. One is in the act of tapping the other on the shoulder, while the other indicates a knocking at the door of the temple with his finger.

In the third section, the admission itself is performed by the Demiurge <note>original: "Demiurgos"; in this context, a creator-figure or high priest who fashions the initiate's understanding of the universe.</note>; from the accompanying signs, it is evident that the discourse concerns the arrangement of the parts of the human body and the harmony of all nature, which is primarily indicated by the plumb-bob <term>plumb-bob (Bleywaage): a lead weight used by builders to find a vertical line, here symbolizing moral or natural balance</term> hanging over both.

In the fourth section, the newly admitted initiate stands already in priestly clothing before an eagle wearing a cap, which signifies a great scholar who instructs him on the creation of the world; this is evident from the globe surrounded by a serpent, which indicated the divine fertilization of nature. The cross with the ring <note>This refers to the Ankh, the ancient Egyptian symbol of life.</note> hanging from it shows that

***

<margin>*) A continuous line signifies a division; a mural crown <note>original: "Mauerkrone"; a crown representing city walls, used here as a symbolic border.</note> with an enclosure indicates a summary or the essence of something: but if it stands alone, these are sub-divisions; with twelve notches, it represents years.</margin>

<summary>This page describes the stages of an initiation ritual as interpreted from the obelisk's carvings, moving from the initial registration and temple knocking to a philosophical lecture on the creation of the world.</summary>
<keywords>initiation, Demiurge, plumb-bob, mural crown, serpent-wrapped globe, eagle, priestly garments, Egyptian mysteries, creation of the world, Ankh</keywords>

--- Page 225 ---
<meta>Continuing the interpretation of the fourth section from page 188, linking the symbol of the Ankh (the cross with a ring) to the discussion of eternity and the afterlife.</meta>

is the talk of eternity and the future life. The screw and other signs indicate the exact connection of concepts and sciences.

In the fifth section, the parts of the world are examined more closely. The butterfly <note>original: "Schmetterling"; in classical and later symbolism, the butterfly often represented the Greek "Psyche" or soul.</note> does not always signify the soul, but also insight. The instruction concerns the animal kingdom and agriculture, whereby certain signs indicate fields, meadows, forests, and marshes. A <term>cynocephalus: a dog-headed baboon, an animal sacred to the Egyptian god Thoth, the patron of wisdom and scribes</term> notes that one must pay close attention to the changes of the moon. A lotus is the sign of instruction in the plant kingdom, just as the star Canopus <note>original: "Kanopus"; one of the brightest stars in the night sky, used by ancient civilizations for navigation and marking the rising of the Nile.</note> represents the teaching of the seasons. The quails and owls signify diligence and experience.

The sixth section deals with hydraulic engineering <note>original: "Wasserbau"; the management of water resources, vital for Egyptian civilization.</note>, which is indicated by the eye alongside the level <term>level (Setzwage): an A-frame tool with a plumb line used by ancient builders to find a horizontal plane</term>; likewise, the man behind the head of Anubis <note>Anubis is the jackal-headed god associated with the afterlife, here interpreted as a symbol for a specific phase of the Nile's cycle.</note> indicates the flooding, which is to be prevented through diligent digging.

In the seventh section, a star signifies instruction in astronomy <note>original: "Sternkunde"</note>; where the apprentice indicates his wonder by raising his hands. — The ancient doctrine of the influence of the stars <note>original: "Einwirkung der Gestirne"; referring to the belief that celestial bodies directly affect earthly events or human temperaments.</note> is indicated by forks, trowels, and mallets, as symbols of wrath.

<summary>This page details sections five through seven of an interpretive guide to Egyptian hieroglyphs, focusing on how specific symbols represent the natural world, engineering, and astronomy.</summary>
<keywords>eternity, butterfly, cynocephalus, lotus, Canopus, hydraulic engineering, Anubis, astronomy, symbolism, Nile flood</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues the interpretation of a symbolic or hieroglyphic system, moving from the seventh section (astronomy and the apprentice's wonder) into the eighth and ninth sections, which cover intellectual professions, crafts, and the end of the human life cycle.</meta>

In the eighth section, occupations are indicated through the images of the owl and the falcon, for which insight and understanding are primarily required: namely, geometry and the various types of scripts, which the raven—representing the apprentice—learns through initiation. A beam, a trowel, and a level <term>level (Setzwage): an A-frame tool with a plumb line used by ancient builders to find a horizontal plane</term> indicate instruction in the art of building. Locks, pots, bows, etc. indicate artistic handicrafts. The female figure with a cap in the corner indicates the art of housekeeping, as well as the promotion of instruction through speaking and writing, where two snakes indicate reproduction <note>In this context, "reproduction" likely refers to the transmission or propagation of knowledge and the continuation of the household lineage.</note>.

In the somber ninth section, a <term>cynocephalus: a dog-headed baboon, an animal sacred to the Egyptian god Thoth, representing wisdom and the moon</term>—which indicates both the changing and waning of the moon as well as sadness in general—points toward medicine <note>original: "Arzneykunde"</note> through the signs ▽ and ☉ <note>These are likely alchemical or astrological symbols representing water (the inverted triangle) and the sun (the circled dot).</note>. A broken bow signifies the decline of the powers of the mind and the body. Man—after having been a child, a youth, and a man, as the quail indicates—becomes a naked, helpless child once more. The years advance his death; the grave is already dug for him. The swan, or the funeral oration <note>The "song of the swan" is a classical metaphor for a final performance or statement before death.</note>, determines whether he deserves to be buried according to Egyptian custom.
